But if the madness is furious, or if the mad person causes notable inconvenience to the inhabitants, the city attorney or any member of the community may also provoke the interdiction.

**Article 460:** The judge shall inform themselves of the previous life and habitual conduct of the alleged demented person, and shall hear the opinion of specialists of their confidence on the existence and nature of the dementia.

**Article 461:** The provisions of articles 446, 447, and 449 extend to the case of dementia.

**Article 462:** The curatorship of the demented person shall be granted:

1. To their spouse not judicially separated, without prejudice to the provisions of article 503;  
2. To their descendants;  
3. To their ascendants, but the father or mother whose paternity or maternity has been judicially determined against their opposition or who is married to a third party may not exercise the office;  
4. To their siblings, and  
5. To other collaterals up to the fourth degree.

The judge shall choose in each class designated in numbers 2, 3, 4, and 5, the person or persons deemed most suitable.

In the absence of all the aforementioned persons, dativa curatorship shall apply.

**Article 463:** The wife curator of her demented husband shall have the administration of the conjugal partnership. If due to an impediment the curatorship of her demented husband is not granted to her, she may at her option, once the impediment ceases, request this curatorship or the separation of property.

**Article 464:** If two or more curators are appointed for the demented person, the immediate care of the person may be entrusted to one of them, leaving the administration of the property to the others.

The immediate care of the person of the demented person shall not be entrusted to any person who is called to inherit them, unless it is their father or mother, or their spouse.

**Article 465:** The acts and contracts of the demented person, subsequent to the interdiction decree, shall be null; even if alleged to have been executed or entered into in a lucid interval.

And on the contrary, acts and contracts executed or entered into without previous interdiction shall be valid; unless it is proved that the one who executed or entered into them was then demented.

**Article 466:** The demented person shall not be deprived of their personal liberty, except in cases where it is to be feared that using it they will harm themselves, or cause danger or notable inconvenience to others.

Nor may they be transferred to a madhouse, nor confined, nor bound, except momentarily, while at the request of the curator, or of any member of the community, judicial authorization is obtained for any of these measures.

**Article 467:** The fruits of their property, and if necessary, and with judicial authorization, the capital, shall be employed mainly to alleviate their condition and to seek their recovery.

**Article 468:** The demented person may be rehabilitated for the administration of their property if it appears that they have permanently recovered reason; and may also be again incapacitated with just cause. In these cases, the provisions of articles 454 and 455 shall be observed.

---

## TITLE XXVI: SPECIAL RULES RELATING TO THE CURATORSHIP OF THE DEAF OR DEAF-MUTE

**Article 469:** The curatorship of the deaf or deaf-mute, who cannot make themselves clearly understood and has reached puberty, may be testamentary, legitimate, or dativa.

**Article 470:** Articles 449, 457, 458 paragraph 1, 462, 463, and 464 extend to the deaf or deaf-mute who cannot make themselves clearly understood.

**Article 471:** The fruits of the property of the deaf or deaf-mute who cannot make themselves clearly understood, and if necessary, and with judicial authorization, the capital, shall be employed especially to alleviate their condition and to provide them with a suitable education.

**Article 472:** The curatorship shall cease when the deaf or deaf-mute has become capable of understanding and being clearly understood, if they themselves request it, and have sufficient intelligence for the administration of their property; on which the judge shall take competent reports.

---

## TITLE XXVII: OF CURATORSHIPS OF PROPERTY

**Article 473:** In general, there shall be grounds for the appointment of a curator of the property of an absent person when the following circumstances concur:

1. That their whereabouts are unknown, or at least they have ceased to be in communication with their family, and from the lack of communication serious harm results to the absent person or to third parties;  
2. That they have not appointed an attorney, or have only appointed one for particular matters or business.

**Article 474:** This appointment may be requested by the same persons who are admitted to request the interdiction of the demented person. In addition, the absent person's creditors shall have the right to request that a curator of the property be appointed to answer their claims.

The debtor who hides is included among absent persons.

**Article 475:** The same persons as for the curatorship of the demented person in accordance with article 462 may be appointed for the curatorship of the absent person's property, and the same order of preference shall be observed among them.

The judge may, however, depart from this order, at the request of the legitimate heirs or creditors, if deemed convenient.

They may also appoint more than one curator and divide the administration among them, in the case of substantial property situated in different communes.

**Article 476:** The defender of absentees shall intervene in the appointment.

**Article 477:** If the absent person has left a wife not judicially separated, the provisions for this case in the title On the conjugal partnership shall be observed.

**Article 478:** If the absent person is a married woman, the husband may not be curator except in the terms of article 503\.

**Article 479:** The attorney appointed for certain acts or business of the absent person shall be subordinate to the curator; who, however, may not depart from the instructions given by the absent person to the attorney, except with judicial authorization.

**Article 480:** If the whereabouts of the absent person are unknown, the curator's first duty shall be to ascertain them.

Once the absent person's whereabouts are known, the curator shall do everything in their power to establish communication with them.

**Article 481:** A curator shall be appointed for the vacant estate, that is, for the property of a deceased person, whose inheritance has not been accepted.

The curatorship of the vacant estate shall be dativa.

**Article 482:** If the deceased person whose inheritance requires a curator has foreign heirs, the consul of the nation of these heirs shall have the right to propose the curator or curators who are to guard and administer the property.

**Article 483:** The magistrate shall discern the curatorship to the curator or curators proposed by the consul, if they are competent persons; and at the request of creditors, or of other interested parties in the succession, may add another or others to said curator or curators, according to the amount and location of the property composing the inheritance.

**Article 484:** After four years have elapsed from the death of the person whose estate is under curatorship, the judge, at the curator's request and with knowledge of the case, may order that all existing hereditary property be sold, and the proceeds be invested at interest with due securities, or if there are none, deposited in the State treasury.

**Article 485:** The property that is to correspond to the posthumous child, if born alive and in due time, shall be in charge of the curator who has been designated for this purpose by the father's will, or of a curator appointed by the judge, at the request of the mother, or at the request of any of the persons who are to succeed in said property, if the posthumous child does not succeed in it.

Two or more curators may be appointed, if convenient.

**Article 486:** The person designated by the father's will for the child's guardianship shall be presumed designated as well for the curatorship of the eventual rights of this child, if the father dies before its birth.

The provisions of this article and the preceding one shall not apply when the mother has parental authority.

**Article 487:** The curator of the property of an absent person, the curator of a vacant estate, the curator of the eventual rights of the unborn, are subject in their administration to all the restrictions of guardians or curators, and in addition they are prohibited from executing other administrative acts than those of mere custody and conservation, and those necessary for the collection of credits and payment of the debts of their respective represented persons.

**Article 488:** They are especially prohibited from altering the form of the property, contracting loans, and alienating even movable property that is not perishable, unless this alienation belongs to the ordinary course of the absent person's business, or the payment of debts requires it.

**Article 489:** Notwithstanding the provisions of the preceding articles, the acts prohibited in them to curators of property shall be valid, if their necessity or utility is justified and authorized by the judge beforehand.

The owner of the property shall have the right to have the nullity of any such act declared, not authorized by the judge; and once nullity is declared, the curator shall be responsible for any harm resulting to said person or to third parties.

**Article 490:** It is the role of curators of property to exercise the judicial actions and defenses of their respective represented persons; and persons who have claims against the property may assert them against the respective curators.

**Article 491:** The curatorship of the absent person's rights expires upon their return; or by the fact of a general attorney duly constituted taking charge of their affairs; or by their death; or by the decree that, in the case of disappearance, grants provisional possession.

The curatorship of the vacant estate ceases by the acceptance of the inheritance, or in the case of article 484, by the deposit of the proceeds of the sale in the State treasury.

The curatorship of the eventual rights of the unborn ceases upon birth.

Every curatorship of property ceases by the extinction or complete conversion of the same property.

---

## TITLE XXVIII: OF JOINT CURATORS

**Article 492:** Joint curators have over the property placed in their charge the same administrative faculties as guardians, unless they are added to curators of property.

In this case, they shall have no more faculties than those of curators of property.

**Article 493:** Joint curators are independent of the respective parents, husbands, or guardians.

The subsidiary responsibility imposed by article 419 on guardians or curators who do not administer, extends to the respective parents, husbands, or guardians with respect to joint curators.

---

## TITLE XXIX: OF SPECIAL CURATORS

**Article 494:** Special curatorships are dativa. Curators for litigation or ad litem are appointed by the court having jurisdiction over the lawsuit, and if they are court-appointed attorneys they shall not need to have the office discerned to them.

**Article 495:** The special curator is not obliged to prepare an inventory, but only to give receipt for the documents, amounts, or effects placed at their disposal for the performance of their office, of which they shall give a faithful and accurate account.

---

## TITLE XXX: OF INCAPACITIES AND EXCUSES FOR GUARDIANSHIP OR CURATORSHIP

**Article 496:** There are persons whom the law prohibits from being guardians or curators, and persons whom it allows to excuse themselves from serving the guardianship or curatorship.

### § 1\. Of Incapacities

**I. Rules relating to physical and moral defects**

**Article 497:** The following are incapable of any guardianship or curatorship:

1. The blind;  
2. The mute;  
3. The demented, even if not under interdiction;  
4. Bankrupts as long as they have not satisfied their creditors;  
5. Those who are deprived of administering their own property for prodigality;  
6. Those who lack domicile in the Republic;  
7. Those who cannot read or write;  
8. Those of notoriously bad conduct;  
9. Those convicted of a crime deserving afflictive punishment, even if pardoned thereof;  
10. Suppressed;  
11. One who has been deprived of exercising parental authority according to article 271;  
12. Those who by faulty or careless administration have been removed from a previous guardianship, or in the subsequent lawsuit have been condemned, for fraud or gross fault, to indemnify the ward.

**II. Rules relating to professions, employments, and public offices**

**Article 498:** The following are also incapable of any guardianship or curatorship:

1. Repealed.  
2. Repealed.  
3. Those who have to exercise for a long time, or for an indefinite time, a public office or commission outside Chilean territory.

**III. Rules relating to sex**

**Article 499:** Repealed.

**IV. Rules relating to age**

**Article 500:** Those who have not completed twenty-one years may not be guardians or curators.

However, if a guardianship or curatorship is granted to the ascendant or descendant who has not completed twenty-one years, they shall be waited for to reach that age to confer the office, and an interim shall be appointed for the intervening time.

The testamentary guardian or curator who has not completed twenty-one years shall be waited for in the same way. But the appointment of the minor guardian or curator shall be invalid, when upon reaching twenty-one they would only have to exercise the guardianship or curatorship for less than two years.

**Article 501:** When there is no certainty about the age, it shall be judged according to article 314, and if in consequence the office is discerned to the appointed guardian or curator, it shall be valid and subsist, whatever their actual age.

**V. Rules relating to family relationships**

**Article 502:** The stepfather may not be the guardian or curator of his stepchild.

**Article 503:** The husband and wife may not be curators of the other spouse if they are totally separated as to property.

However, this disqualification shall not apply in the case of article 135, in that of conventional separation, nor in the event of a regime of participation in the partnership gains between spouses, in all of which the judge, hearing the relatives, may grant the guardianship to the husband or wife.

**Article 504:** The child may not be curator of their spendthrift father.

**VI. Rules relating to conflict of interests or difference of religion between guardian and ward**

**Article 505:** One who disputes or has disputed a person's civil status may not be their guardian or curator.

**Article 506:** Creditors or debtors of a person, nor those who litigate with them, for their own or others' interests, may not be sole guardians or curators of that person.

The judge, as deemed more convenient, shall add other guardians or curators to administer jointly, or shall declare them incapable of the office.

The spouse and ascendants and descendants of the ward are not subject to the provision of this article.

**Article 507:** The provisions of the preceding article do not include the testamentary guardian or curator, if it is proved that the testator had knowledge of the credit, debt, or litigation at the time of appointing said guardian or curator.

Nor do they extend to credits, debts, or litigation of little importance in the judge's opinion.

**Article 508:** Those who profess a different religion from that in which the ward must be or has been educated, may not be their guardians or curators, except in the case of being accepted by the ascendants, and in their absence by the closest relatives.

**VII. Rules relating to supervening incapacity**

**Article 509:** The aforementioned causes of incapacity, which arise during the exercise of guardianship or curatorship, shall put an end to it.

**Article 510:** The dementia of the guardian or curator shall vitiate with nullity all acts executed by them during it, even if they have not been placed under interdiction.

**Article 511:** If the woman who exercises the guardianship or curatorship marries, she shall continue to perform it, provided that by the fact of marriage the ward is not to be subject to the husband's or wife's parental authority. In this case said guardianship shall cease.

**VIII. General rules on incapacities**

**Article 512:** Guardians or curators who have concealed the causes of incapacity that existed at the time of granting the office or that subsequently arose, in addition to being subject to all the responsibilities of their administration, shall lose the emoluments corresponding to the time in which, knowing the incapacity, they exercised the office.

Unknown causes of incapacity do not vitiate the guardian's or curator's acts; but once known by them, they shall put an end to guardianship or curatorship.

**Article 513:** The guardian who believes themselves incapable of exercising the guardianship or curatorship granted to them, shall have the same periods to provoke the proceeding on their incapacity as those prescribed for the proceeding on their excuses in article 520\.

If incapacity arises during the exercise of guardianship or curatorship, they must denounce it to the judge within three days following the day such incapacity began to exist or came to their knowledge; and this period shall be extended in the same manner as the thirty-day period prescribed in article 520\.

The incapacity of the guardian or curator may also be denounced to the judge by any of the ward's relatives, by their spouse, and even by any member of the community.

### § 2\. Of Excuses

**Article 514:** The following may excuse themselves from guardianship or curatorship:

1. The President of the Republic, the Ministers of State, the Justices of the Supreme Court and of the Courts of Appeals, the prosecutors and other persons who exercise the public ministry, the trial judges, the defender of minors, the defender of pious works, and other public defenders;  
2. Administrators and collectors of fiscal revenues;  
3. Those who are obliged to serve for a long time a public office at a considerable distance from the commune in which the guardianship is to be exercised;  
4. Those who have their domicile at a considerable distance from said commune;  
5. The father or mother who has the daily care of the household;  
6. Those who suffer from some serious habitual illness or have reached sixty-five years;  
7. The poor who are forced to live by their daily personal work;  
8. Those who already exercise two guardianships; and those who, being married or having children, already exercise one guardianship; but special curatorships shall not be taken into account. The judge may count as two the guardianship or curatorship that is too complicated and burdensome;  
9. Those who have under their parental authority five or more living children; counting also those who have died in action of war under the Republic's flags;  
10. Priests or ministers of any religion;  
11. Members of the National Defense Forces and the Carabineros Corps, who are in active service; including commissioners, doctors, surgeons, and other persons attached to line units or State vessels.

**Article 515:** In the case of the preceding article, number 8, the one who exercises two or more guardianships of persons who are not their children, shall have the right to request to be exempted from one of them in order to take charge of the guardianship of one of their children; but they may not excuse themselves from the latter.

**Article 516:** The excuse of number 9, article 514, may not be alleged to avoid serving the guardianship or curatorship of the child.

**Article 517:** The excuse of not finding sureties shall not be admitted, if the one alleging it has sufficient property; in this case they shall be obliged to constitute a mortgage or pledge on them up to the amount deemed sufficient to respond for their administration.

**Article 518:** One who for ten or more continuous years has served the guardianship of the same ward, as guardian or curator, or as guardian and curator successively, may excuse themselves from continuing in the exercise of their office; but the spouse, nor an ascendant or descendant, may allege this excuse.

**Article 519:** The excuses set forth in the preceding articles must be alleged, by the one wishing to take advantage of them, at the time the guardianship is granted; and they shall be admissible if they arise during it.

**Article 520:** Excuses for not accepting the guardianship granted must be alleged within the following periods:

If the appointed guardian or curator is in the jurisdictional territory where the judge who is to hear them resides, they shall allege them within thirty days following the day they were notified of their appointment; and if they are not in said jurisdictional territory, but are in the Republic's territory, this period shall be extended by four days for every fifty kilometers of distance between the head city of said jurisdictional territory and the current residence of the appointed guardian or curator.

**Article 521:** Any delay exceeding the legal period and which with medium diligence could have been avoided, shall impose on the guardian or curator the responsibility for the harm resulting from their delay in taking charge of the guardianship or curatorship; and shall also make their voluntary excuses inadmissible, unless it is in the ward's interest to accept them.

**Article 522:** Grounds for excuse that arise during the guardianship do not prescribe by any delay in alleging them.

**Article 523:** If the appointed guardian or curator is in a foreign country, and it is unknown when they will return, or if their whereabouts are unknown, the judge may, according to circumstances, set a period within which the guardian or curator must appear to take charge of the guardianship or curatorship or to excuse themselves; and once the period expires, may, according to circumstances, extend it, or declare the appointment invalid; which shall not be validated even if the guardian or curator later appears.

### § 3\. Common Rules for Incapacities and Excuses

**Article 524:** The proceeding on the incapacities or excuses alleged by the guardian shall be conducted with the respective defender.

**Article 525:** If the judge in the first instance does not recognize the causes of incapacity alleged by the guardian, or does not accept their excuses, and if the guardian does not appeal, or if the appeal court confirms the lower judge's ruling, the guardian shall be responsible for any harm resulting to the ward from their delay in taking charge of the guardianship.

This responsibility shall not apply if the guardian or curator, to exempt themselves from it, offers to take interim charge of the guardianship or curatorship.

---

## TITLE XXXI: OF THE REMUNERATION OF GUARDIANS AND CURATORS

**Article 526:** The guardian or curator shall have, in general, in recompense for their work, one-tenth of the fruits of that property of their ward they administer.

If there are several guardians or curators who administer jointly, the tenth shall be divided among them in equal parts.

But if one of the guardians exercises functions to which the receipt of fruits is not attached, the judge shall deduct from the tenth of the others the remuneration deemed fair to assign them.

They may also increase the tenth of one guardian, deducting this increase from the tenth of the others, when there is a manifest disproportion between the respective work and emoluments.

These two provisions shall be dictated by the judge, if necessary, at the request of the respective guardian, and with hearing of the others.

**Article 527:** The distribution of the tenth shall be made according to the general rules of the preceding article, paragraphs 1 and 2, as long as in accordance with paragraphs 3 and 4 it is not altered by agreement of the parties or by judge's decree; nor shall the new distribution apply except from the date of the agreement or decree.

**Article 528:** Necessary expenses incurred by guardians or curators in the performance of their office shall be reimbursed to them separately, and shall not be charged to the tenth.

**Article 529:** Any assignment expressly made to the testamentary guardian or curator in recompense for their work shall be charged to what of the tenth of the fruits is to belong to said guardian or curator; and if it is worth less, they shall have the right to have their remuneration completed; but if it is worth more, they shall not be obliged to pay the excess as long as it fits in the share of property the testator could freely dispose of.

**Article 530:** Accepted excuses deprive the testamentary guardian or curator of the assignment made to them in remuneration for their work.

But supervening excuses shall deprive them only of a proportional part.

**Article 531:** Preexisting incapacities remove from the guardian all right to the aforementioned assignment.

If the incapacity arises without the guardian's act or fault, or if the guardian dies during the guardianship, there shall be no place for restitution of the assigned thing, in whole or in part.

**Article 532:** If an interim guardian or curator relieves the owner of all their functions, the interim's entire tenth shall correspond for all the time their office lasts; but if the owner retains any part of their functions, they shall also retain a proportionate part of their tenth.

If the remuneration consists of a hereditary share or legacy, and the owner has made the appointment of the interim necessary for a justifiable cause, such as a public office, or to avoid serious harm to their interests, they shall retain their inheritance or legacy entirely, and the interim shall receive the tenth of the fruits of what they administer.

**Article 533:** The guardian or curator who administers fraudulently or who contravenes the provision of article 116, loses their right to the tenth, and shall be obliged to restore everything they have received as remuneration for their office.

If they administer carelessly, they shall not collect the tenth of the fruits in that part of the property that due to their negligence has suffered harm or experienced a considerable decrease in products.

In either case, the indemnification of damages to the ward also remains reserved.

**Article 534:** If the fruits of the ward's patrimony are so scarce that they barely suffice for their precise subsistence, the guardian or curator shall be obliged to serve their office gratuitously; and if the ward comes to acquire more property, whether during guardianship or after, the guardian may demand nothing in reason of the tenth corresponding to the previous time.

**Article 535:** The guardian shall collect their tenth as the fruits are realized.

To determine the value of the tenth, not only the expenses invested in the production of the fruits shall be taken into account, but all usufructuary pensions and charges to which the patrimony is subject.

**Article 536:** Regarding fruits pending at the time of beginning or ending the guardianship, the guardian's or curator's tenth shall be subject to the same rules as usufruct.

**Article 537:** In general, among the fruits from which the tenth must be deducted, those materials that once separated do not regenerate shall not be counted, nor those whose separation damages the property or decreases its value.

Consequently, wood or timber sold shall not be counted among the fruits, when cutting is not done with the regularity necessary to keep forests and groves in good condition.

The tenth shall, however, extend to the product of quarries and mines.

**Article 538:** Curators of property of absentees, curators of the eventual rights of a posthumous child, curators of a vacant estate, and special curators, have no right to the tenth. They shall be assigned by the judge an equitable remuneration on the fruits of the property they administer, or a determined amount, in recompense for their work.

---

## TITLE XXXII: OF THE REMOVAL OF GUARDIANS AND CURATORS

**Article 539:** Guardians or curators shall be removed:

1. For incapacity;  
2. For fraud or gross fault in the exercise of their office, and especially for those indicated in articles 378 and 434;  
3. For manifest ineptitude;  
4. For repeated acts of careless administration;  
5. For immoral conduct, from which harm to the ward's morals may result.

For the fourth of the preceding causes, the guardian or curator who is an ascendant, descendant, or spouse of the ward may not be removed, but another guardian or curator shall be associated with them in the administration.

**Article 540:** Habitual carelessness in administration shall be presumed from the fact of property deteriorating, or fruits decreasing considerably; and the guardian or curator who does not dispel this presumption by giving a satisfactory explanation of the deterioration or decrease, shall be removed.

**Article 541:** One who exercises several guardianships or curatorships and is removed from one of them for fraud or gross fault, shall by the same fact be removed from the others, at the request of the respective defender, or of any member of the community, or ex officio.

**Article 542:** Removal may be requested by any of the ward's relatives, and by their spouse, and even by any member of the community.

The ward themselves, having reached puberty, may request it, by appealing to the respective defender.

The judge may also promote it ex officio.

The relatives and the public ministry shall always be heard.

**Article 543:** An interim guardian or curator shall be appointed for as long as the removal proceeding is pending, whenever the court, hearing the relatives, considers said appointment convenient.

The interim shall exclude the owner who is not an ascendant, descendant, or spouse; and shall be added to one who is.

**Article 544:** The removed guardian or curator shall fully indemnify the ward.

They shall also be criminally prosecuted for the crimes committed in the exercise of their office.

---

## TITLE XXXIII: OF JURIDICAL PERSONS

**Article 545:** A juridical person is a fictitious person, capable of exercising rights and contracting civil obligations, and of being represented judicially and extrajudicially.

Juridical persons are of two kinds: corporations and public benefit foundations.

Private law corporations are also called associations.

An association is formed by a gathering of persons around objectives of common interest to the associates. A foundation, through the allocation of assets to a determined purpose of general interest.

There are juridical persons that participate in both characters.

**Article 546:** Foundations or corporations that have not been established by virtue of a law, or that have not been constituted in accordance with the rules of this Title, are not juridical persons.

**Article 547:** Industrial societies are not included in the provisions of this title; their rights and obligations are regulated, according to their nature, by other titles of this Code and by the Commercial Code.

Nor do the provisions of this title extend to public law corporations or foundations, such as the nation, the treasury, municipalities, churches, religious communities, and establishments funded by the State treasury: these corporations and foundations are governed by special laws and regulations.

**Article 548:** The act by which associations or foundations are constituted shall be in a public or private deed signed before a notary, Civil Registry Officer, or municipal official authorized by the mayor.

A copy of the constitutive act, authorized by the minister of faith or official before whom it was executed, shall be deposited in the municipal secretary's office of the domicile of the juridical person in formation, within thirty days from its execution. This period shall not apply to foundations constituted by testamentary dispositions.

Within thirty days following the date of deposit, the municipal secretary may object on grounds to the constitution of the association or foundation, if the requirements set forth by law or regulation have not been met. Clauses of the statutes that reproduce models approved by the Ministry of Justice may not be objected. The objection shall be notified to the applicant by certified mail. If by the expiration of this period the municipal secretary has not notified any observation, it shall be understood by operation of law that they do not object to the constitution of the organization, and proceedings shall be in accordance with paragraph five.

Without prejudice to the applicable administrative and judicial claims, the juridical person in formation shall remedy the observations made, within thirty days from their notification. The new background information shall be deposited in the municipal secretary's office, proceeding in accordance with the preceding paragraph. The governing body of the juridical person in formation shall be understood to be empowered to introduce in the statutes the modifications required for these purposes.

If the municipal secretary has no objections to the constitution, or the period for formulating them has expired, ex officio and within five days, the municipal secretary shall file a copy of the background information of the juridical person and send them to the Civil Registry and Identification Service for registration in the National Registry of Non-Profit Juridical Persons, unless the interested party formally requests to make the registration directly. The association or foundation shall enjoy legal personality from this registration.

**Article 548-1:** In the constitutive act, in addition to identifying those who appear executing it, the will to constitute a juridical person shall be expressed, its statutes shall be approved, and the authorities initially in charge of directing it shall be designated.

**Article 548-2:** The statutes of the juridical persons referred to in this Title shall contain:

a) The name and domicile of the juridical person; b) The duration, when not constituted for an indefinite time; c) The indication of the purposes for which it is intended; d) The assets forming its initial patrimony, if any, and the manner in which they are contributed; e) The provisions establishing its governing bodies, how they shall be integrated, and the powers corresponding to them, and f) The provisions relating to the reform of statutes and the extinction of the juridical person, indicating the non-profit institution to which its assets shall pass in the latter event.

The statutes of every association shall determine the rights and obligations of the associates, the conditions for membership, and the form and grounds for exclusion.

The statutes of every foundation shall also specify the assets or rights contributed by the founder to its patrimony, as well as the basic rules for the application of resources to the fulfillment of the foundation's purposes and for the determination of beneficiaries.

**Article 548-3:** The name of the juridical persons referred to in this Title shall refer to their nature, object, or purpose.

The name may not coincide or have a similarity susceptible to causing confusion with any other active juridical person or organization, whether public or private, nor with natural persons, except with the express consent of the interested party or their successors, or twenty years have elapsed from their death.

**Article 548-4:** All those to whom the statutes of the corporation cause harm may resort to the courts, in a brief and summary proceeding, for them to be corrected or for any injury or harm resulting or that may result from the application of said statutes to be repaired.

**Article 549:** What belongs to a corporation does not belong, in whole or in part, to any of the individuals who compose it; and reciprocally, the debts of a corporation do not give anyone the right to demand them, in whole or in part, from any of the individuals who compose the corporation, nor do they give action over their own property, but over the corporation's property.

However, members may, by expressing it, bind themselves individually, at the same time as the corporation binds itself collectively; and the liability of the members shall then be joint and several, if solidarity is expressly stipulated.

But liability shall not extend to heirs, except when the members of the corporation have expressly bound them.

If a corporation does not have legal existence according to article 546, its collective acts bind all and each of its members jointly and severally.

**Article 550:** The majority of the members of a corporation who have deliberative vote according to its statutes, shall be considered as an assembly or legal meeting of the entire corporation.

The assembly shall meet ordinarily once a year, and extraordinarily when the needs of the association require it.

The will of the majority of the assembly is the will of the corporation.

All this is understood without prejudice to the modifications that the corporation's statutes prescribe in this regard.

**Article 551:** The direction and administration of an association shall fall on a board of directors of at least three members, whose term may extend up to five years.

Persons who have been convicted of an afflictive punishment may not be part of the board.

The director who during the performance of their office is convicted of a crime or simple offense, or incurs in any other impediment or cause of disqualification or incompatibility established by law or the statutes, shall cease in their functions, and the board shall appoint a replacement who shall serve for the time remaining to complete the replaced director's term.

The president of the board shall also be the president of the association, shall represent it judicially and extrajudicially, and shall have the other powers indicated by the statutes.

The board shall meet with the absolute majority of its members and its agreements shall be adopted by the absolute majority of those present, deciding in case of a tie the vote of the presiding officer.

The board shall report to the assembly on the investment of funds and the progress of the association during the period in which it exercises its functions.

Any of the associates may request information about the association's accounts, as well as its activities and programs.

**Article 551-1:** Directors shall exercise their office gratuitously, but shall have the right to be reimbursed for expenses, authorized by the board, which they justify having incurred in the exercise of their function.

However, and unless the statutes provide otherwise, the board may establish an adequate remuneration for those directors who provide services to the organization other than their duties as directors. Of all remuneration or compensation received by directors, or the natural or juridical persons related to them by kinship or cohabitation, or by interest or property, a detailed account shall be given to the assembly or, in the case of foundations, to the board.

The preceding rule shall apply with respect to any associate to whom the association entrusts any remunerated function.

**Article 551-2:** In the exercise of their functions, directors shall be jointly and severally liable even for slight fault for the damages they cause to the association.

The director who wishes to save their responsibility for any act or agreement of the board, must state their opposition, and account of this must be given in the next assembly.

**Article 552:** The acts of the corporation's representative, insofar as they do not exceed the limits of the office entrusted to them, are acts of the corporation; insofar as they exceed these limits, they only bind the representative personally.

**Article 553:** The statutes of a corporation have binding force over the entire corporation, and its members are obliged to obey them under the sanctions imposed by the same statutes.

The disciplinary power that corresponds to an association over its associates shall be exercised through an ethics committee, honor tribunal, or other similar body, which shall have disciplinary faculties with respect to the members of the respective association, which it shall exercise through a rational and fair procedure, with respect for the rights conferred by the Constitution, laws, and statutes on its associates. In any case, the position on the administrative body is incompatible with the position on the disciplinary body.

**Article 554:** Repealed.

**Article 555:** Crimes of fraud, waste, and embezzlement of the corporation's funds shall be punished according to its statutes, without prejudice to what the common laws provide on the same crimes.

**Article 556:** Associations and foundations may acquire, hold, and dispose of all kinds of property, gratuitously or for consideration, by acts between living persons or by reason of death.

The patrimony of an association shall also be integrated by the ordinary or extraordinary contributions that the assembly imposes on its associates, in accordance with the statutes.

The profits, utilities, benefits, or surpluses of the association may not be distributed among the associates, even in case of dissolution.

**Article 557:** The supervision of associations and foundations shall be the responsibility of the Ministry of Justice.

In exercise of this power, it may require their representatives to submit for examination the minutes of assemblies and board meetings, approved accounts and reports, accounting books, inventories, and remuneration records, as well as any other information regarding the development of their activities.

The Ministry of Justice may order corporations and foundations to remedy any irregularities it verifies or to pursue the corresponding responsibilities, without prejudice to requiring the judge to take the measures necessary to urgently and provisionally protect the interests of the juridical person or third parties.

Non-compliance with the instructions issued by the Ministry of Justice shall be deemed a serious infraction of the statutes.

**Article 557-1:** The juridical persons governed by this Title shall be obliged to keep accounts in accordance with generally accepted accounting principles. They shall also annually prepare an explanatory report of their activities and a balance sheet approved by the assembly or, in foundations, by the board.

Juridical persons whose patrimony or total annual income exceeds the limits defined by resolution of the Minister of Justice, shall submit their accounting, general balance sheet, and financial statements to the examination of independent external auditors appointed by the assembly of associates or by the board of the foundation from among those registered in the Registry of External Auditors of the Superintendency of Securities and Insurance.

**Article 557-2:** Associations and foundations may carry out economic activities related to their purposes. Likewise, they may invest their resources in the manner decided by their governing bodies.

The income received from those activities shall only be destined to the purposes of the association or foundation or to increase their patrimony.

**Article 557-3:** Minutes shall be kept in a book or registry that ensures the fidelity of the minutes of the deliberations and agreements of the board and, as applicable, of the assemblies.

Associations and foundations shall keep permanently updated registries of their associates, directors, and other authorities provided for in their statutes.

**Article 558:** The modification of an association's statutes shall be agreed by the assembly especially summoned for that purpose. The dissolution or merger with another association shall be approved by two-thirds of the associates attending the respective assembly.

The statutes of a foundation may only be modified by agreement of the board, with prior favorable report of the Ministry, provided the modification is convenient to the foundational interest. No modification shall be allowed if the founder has prohibited it.

The Ministry of Justice shall issue a report regarding the purpose of the foundation, as well as its administrative and governing body, as to its generation, integration, and powers. In any case, the formalities established in article 548 must be complied with.

**Article 559:** Associations shall be dissolved:

a) By the expiration of their duration period, if any; b) By agreement of the general extraordinary assembly, complying with the formal requirements established in article 558; c) By final judicial judgment, in case of:

1. Being prohibited by the Constitution or law or seriously infringing their statutes, or  
2. Having fully realized their purpose or it being impossible to realize it, and

d) For other causes provided in the statutes and laws.

The judgment referred to in letter c) above may only be issued in a proceeding brought at the request of the State Defense Council, in a brief and summary proceeding, which shall exercise the action upon reasoned petition of the Ministry of Justice. In the case referred to in number 2 of letter c) above, it may also be issued in a proceeding brought by the institution called to receive the assets of the association or foundation in case of extinction.

**Article 560:** Repealed.

**Article 561:** Once a corporation is dissolved, its property shall be disposed of in the manner prescribed for this case by its statutes; and if they have not provided for this case, said property shall belong to the State, with the obligation to employ them in objects analogous to those of the institution. It shall be the President of the Republic's role to designate them.

**Article 562:** Public benefit foundations that are to be administered by a collection of individuals, shall be governed by the statutes dictated by the founder; and if the founder has not expressed their will in this regard, or has only incompletely expressed it, proceedings shall be in the manner indicated in the second paragraph of article 558\.

**Article 563:** What in articles 549 to 561 is provided regarding corporations and the members who compose them, shall apply to public benefit foundations and the individuals who administer them.

**Article 564:** Foundations perish by the destruction of the property destined for their maintenance.

---

# BOOK TWO: OF PROPERTY, AND OF ITS DOMINION, POSSESSION, USE, AND ENJOYMENT

## TITLE I: OF THE VARIOUS CLASSES OF PROPERTY

**Article 565:** Property consists of corporeal or incorporeal things.

Corporeal are those that have a real existence and can be perceived by the senses, such as a house, a book. Incorporeal are those that consist of mere rights, such as credits, and active servitudes.

### § 1\. Of Corporeal Things

**Article 566:** Corporeal things are divided into movable and immovable.

**Article 567:** Movable are those that can be transported from one place to another, whether moving by themselves, such as animals (which for this reason are called semi-movable), or if they only move by an external force, such as inanimate things. Excepted are those which, being movable by nature, are deemed immovable by their destination, according to article 570\.

**Article 568:** Immovable, or estates, or real property, are things that cannot be transported from one place to another; such as lands and mines, and those that permanently adhere to them, such as buildings, trees. Houses and landed estates are called premises or farms.

**Article 569:** Plants are immovable, as long as they adhere to the ground by their roots, unless they are in pots or boxes that can be transported from one place to another.

**Article 570:** Things are deemed immovable, even if by their nature they are not, if they are permanently destined to the use, cultivation, and benefit of an immovable, even though they may be separated without damage. Such are, for example: The slabs of a pavement; The pipes of plumbing; Agricultural or mining tools, and animals actually destined for the cultivation or benefit of an estate, provided they have been placed on it by the owner of the estate; The fertilizers existing on it, and destined by the owner of the estate to improve it; The presses, boilers, vats, stills, barrels, and machines that form part of an industrial establishment attached to the ground, and belong to its owner; The animals kept in rabbitries, aviaries, ponds, beehives, and any other animal enclosures, provided they adhere to the ground, or are part of the ground itself, or of a building.

**Article 571:** The products of immovable property, and things accessory to them, such as grass of a field, wood and fruit of trees, animals in an enclosure, are deemed movable, even before their separation, for the purpose of constituting a right over said products or things to another person than the owner. The same applies to the earth or sand of a soil, the metals of a mine, and the stones of a quarry.

**Article 572:** Things of convenience or ornament that are nailed or fixed in the walls of houses and can be easily removed without damage to the walls, such as stoves, mirrors, pictures, tapestries, are deemed movable. If the pictures or mirrors are embedded in the walls, so as to form one body with them, they shall be considered part of them, even if they can be separated without damage.

**Article 573:** Things that, because they are accessory to real property, are deemed immovable, do not cease to be so by their momentary separation; for example, bulbs or onions that are pulled up to be replanted, and slabs or stones that are removed from their place, to make some construction or repair and with the intention of returning them to it. But from the moment they are separated with the object of giving them a different destination, they cease to be immovable.

**Article 574:** When by law or by man the expression movable property is used without other qualification, it shall include everything understood by movable things, according to article 567\.

In the movable property of a house, money, documents and papers, scientific or artistic collections, books or their shelves, medals, weapons, instruments of arts and crafts, jewelry, clothing and bedding, carriages or horses or their harnesses, grains, wines, merchandise, shall not be included, nor in general other things than those forming the household furniture.

**Article 575:** Movable things are divided into fungible and non-fungible.

To the former belong those of which the use convenient to their nature cannot be made without their being destroyed. Monetary species, insofar as they perish for the one who uses them as such, are fungible things.

### § 2\. Of Incorporeal Things

**Article 576:** Incorporeal things are real or personal rights.

**Article 577:** A real right is one we have over a thing without respect to a determined person. Real rights are those of dominion, inheritance, usufruct, use or habitation, active servitudes, pledge, and mortgage. From these rights arise real actions.

**Article 578:** Personal rights or credits are those that can only be claimed from certain persons, who, by their own act or the sole provision of law, have contracted the correlative obligations; such as the lender has against their debtor for the money lent, or the child against the parent for maintenance. From these rights arise personal actions.

**Article 579:** The right of ground rent is personal insofar as it can be directed against the rent charge debtor, even if not in possession of the charged estate, and real insofar as the estate is pursued.

**Article 580:** Rights and actions are deemed movable or immovable property, according to whether the thing on which they are to be exercised, or which is owed, is movable or immovable. Thus the right of usufruct over an immovable is immovable. Thus the buyer's action for delivery of the purchased estate is immovable; and the action of the one who lent money, to be paid, is movable.

**Article 581:** Acts that are owed are deemed movable. The action for a craftsman to perform the agreed work, or to compensate for damages caused by non-performance of the agreement, therefore belongs to the class of movable property.

---

## TITLE II: OF DOMINION

**Article 582:** Dominion (which is also called property) is the real right over a corporeal thing, to enjoy and dispose of it arbitrarily; not being contrary to law or to the rights of others. Property separated from the enjoyment of the thing is called bare or naked property.

**Article 583:** Over incorporeal things there is also a kind of property. Thus, the usufructuary has the property of their right of usufruct.

**Article 584:** The productions of talent or intellect are a property of their authors. This kind of property shall be governed by special laws.

**Article 585:** Things that nature has made common to all men, such as the high seas, are not susceptible to dominion, and no nation, corporation, or individual has the right to appropriate them. Their use and enjoyment are determined among individuals of a nation by its laws, and among different nations by international law.

**Article 586:** Things that have been consecrated for divine worship shall be governed by canon law.

**Article 587:** The use and enjoyment of chapels and cemeteries, situated in private possessions and accessory to them, shall pass together with them and together with the ornaments, vessels, and other objects belonging to said chapels or cemeteries, to the persons who successively acquire the possessions in which they are situated, unless otherwise disposed by will or by act between living persons.

**Article 588:** The modes of acquiring dominion are occupation, accession, tradition, succession by reason of death, and prescription. Of the acquisition of dominion by these latter means, treatment will be given in the Book On succession by reason of death, and at the end of this Code.

---

## TITLE III: OF NATIONAL PROPERTY

**Article 589:** National property is that whose dominion belongs to the entire nation. If in addition its use belongs to all inhabitants of the nation, such as streets, squares, bridges and roads, the adjacent sea and its beaches, they are called national property for public use or public property. National property whose use does not generally belong to the inhabitants is called State property or fiscal property.

**Article 590:** All lands that, being situated within territorial limits, lack another owner, are State property.

**Article 591:** The State is owner of all mines of gold, silver, copper, mercury, tin, precious stones, and other fossil substances, notwithstanding the dominion of corporations or private individuals over the surface of the land in whose entrails they are situated.

But private individuals are granted the faculty to prospect and dig in lands of any dominion to search for the mines referred to in the preceding paragraph, to work and benefit said mines, and to dispose of them as owners, with the requirements and under the rules prescribed by the Mining Code.

**Article 592:** Bridges and roads constructed at the expense of private individuals on lands belonging to them are not national property, even if the owners permit their use and enjoyment to all. The same extends to any other constructions made at the expense of private individuals and on their lands, even if their use is public, by permission of the owner.

**Article 593:** The adjacent sea, up to the distance of twelve nautical miles measured from the respective baselines, is territorial sea and of national dominion. But, for purposes concerning the prevention and sanction of infractions of its customs, fiscal, immigration, or sanitary laws and regulations, the State exercises jurisdiction over a maritime space called contiguous zone, which extends up to the distance of twenty-four nautical miles, measured in the same manner.

Waters situated within the baselines of the territorial sea form part of the State's internal waters.

**Article 594:** The seashore is understood to be the extent of land that the waves bathe and leave alternately up to where they reach at the highest tides.

**Article 595:** All waters are national property for public use.

**Article 596:** The adjacent sea that extends up to two hundred nautical miles counted from the baselines from which the width of the territorial sea is measured, and beyond the latter, is called exclusive economic zone. In it, the State exercises sovereign rights to explore, exploit, conserve, and manage the natural resources, living and non-living, of the waters superjacent to the seabed, of the seabed and the subsoil of the sea, and to develop any other activities with a view to the economic exploration and exploitation of that zone.

The State exercises exclusive sovereign rights over the continental shelf for the purposes of conservation, exploration, and exploitation of its natural resources.

In addition, the State has all other jurisdiction and rights provided for in International Law with respect to the exclusive economic zone and the continental shelf.

**Article 597:** New islands that form in the territorial sea or in rivers and lakes navigable by vessels of more than one hundred tons shall belong to the State.

**Article 598:** The use and enjoyment for transit, irrigation, navigation, and any other lawful purposes that correspond to private individuals in the streets, squares, bridges, and public roads, in the sea and its beaches, in rivers and lakes, and generally in all national property for public use, shall be subject to the provisions of this Code, and to the general or local ordinances promulgated on the matter.

**Article 599:** No one may construct, except by special permission of competent authority, any work on the streets, squares, bridges, beaches, fiscal lands, and other places of national property.

**Article 600:** Columns, pilasters, steps, thresholds, and any other constructions that serve for the convenience or ornament of buildings, or form part of them, may not occupy any space, however small, on the surface of streets, squares, bridges, roads, and other places of national property.

Buildings in which the contrary practice has been tolerated shall be subject to the provision of the preceding paragraph, if they are rebuilt.

**Article 601:** In buildings constructed on the sides of streets or squares, until the height of three meters, there may be no windows, balconies, belvederes, or other works that protrude more than half a decimeter outside the vertical plane of the boundary line, nor may there be any above that protrude from said vertical plane except up to the horizontal distance of three decimeters.

The provisions of the preceding article, paragraph 2, shall apply to the reconstruction of said buildings.

**Article 602:** Over works that, with permission of competent authority, are constructed in sites of national property, private individuals who have obtained this permission have only the use and enjoyment of them, and not the property of the land. Once the works are abandoned, or the time for which permission was granted ends, they and the land are restored by operation of law to the exclusive use and enjoyment of the State, or to the general use and enjoyment of the inhabitants, as the sovereign authority prescribes.

But this is not understood if the property of the land has been expressly granted by the State.

**Article 603:** Canals from rivers may not be drawn for any industrial or domestic purpose, except in accordance with the respective laws or ordinances.

**Article 604:** National or foreign vessels may not touch or approach any part of the beach, except to the ports designated for this purpose by law; unless an imminent danger of shipwreck, capture, or other similar necessity forces them to do so; and the captains or masters of vessels that otherwise do so shall be subject to the penalties imposed by the respective laws and ordinances.

Shipwrecked persons shall have free access to the beach and shall be assisted by local authorities.

**Article 605:** Notwithstanding the provisions of this title and of On accession relative to the dominion of the nation over rivers, lakes, and islands, rights acquired by private individuals prior to the promulgation of this Code shall subsist in them.

---

## TITLE IV: OF OCCUPATION

**Article 606:** By occupation, dominion is acquired over things that belong to no one, and whose acquisition is not prohibited by Chilean laws or by International Law.

**Article 607:** Hunting and fishing are species of occupation by which dominion over wild animals is acquired.

**Article 608:** Wild or savage animals are those that live naturally free and independent of man, such as wild animals and fish; domestic are those belonging to species that live ordinarily under the dependence of man, such as chickens, sheep; and domesticated are those that, although wild by nature, have become accustomed to domesticity and recognize in a certain way the rule of man. These latter, as long as they retain the habit of returning to the protection or dependence of man, follow the rule of domestic animals, and losing this habit, return to the class of wild animals.

**Article 609:** The exercise of hunting shall be subject to compliance with the special legislation regulating it. Hunting may not be done except on one's own lands, or on others' with the owner's permission.

**Article 610:** If someone hunts on another's lands without the owner's permission, when by law they were obliged to obtain it, what they catch shall be for the owner, whom they shall also indemnify for any damage.

**Article 611:** Maritime hunting and fishing shall be regulated by the provisions of this Code and, preferentially, by the special legislation governing it.

**Article 612:** Fishermen may make of the seashores the necessary use for fishing, constructing huts, bringing their boats and utensils and the product of the fishing to shore, drying their nets, etc.; taking care, however, not to make any use of the buildings or constructions there without permission of their owners, or to impede the legitimate use of other fishermen.

**Article 613:** They may also for the aforesaid purposes make use of adjacent lands up to the distance of eight meters from the beach; but they shall not touch the buildings or constructions within that distance, nor cross fences, nor enter into orchards, plantations, or crops.

**Article 614:** Owners of lands adjacent to the beach may not place fences, nor make buildings, constructions, or plantings within the said eight meters, without leaving from time to time sufficient and comfortable spaces for the needs of fishing. Otherwise, fishermen shall apply to local authorities for the appropriate remedy.

**Article 615:** Those who fish in rivers and lakes may not make any use of buildings and cultivated lands on the banks nor cross fences.

**Article 616:** The provision of article 610 extends to one who fishes in another's waters.

**Article 617:** The hunter or fisherman is understood to take possession of the wild animal and make it their own, from the moment they have seriously wounded it, so that it is no longer easy for it to escape, and as long as they persist in pursuing it; or from the moment the animal has fallen into their traps or nets, provided they have set them in a place where it is lawful to hunt or fish.

If the wounded animal enters another's lands where it is not lawful to hunt without the owner's permission, the latter may make it their own.

**Article 618:** It is not lawful for a hunter or fisherman to pursue a wild animal already being pursued by another hunter or fisherman; if they do so without their consent, and take possession of the animal, the other may claim it as their own.

**Article 619:** Wild animals belong to the owner of the cages, aviaries, rabbitries, beehives, ponds, or pens in which they are enclosed; but once they recover their natural freedom, any person may take possession of them and make them their own, provided the owner is not actually pursuing them, keeping them in sight, and provided that otherwise article 609 is not contravened.

**Article 620:** Bees that flee from the hive and alight on a tree that is not the owner's, return to their natural freedom, and anyone may take possession of them and of the honeycombs made by them, provided they do not do so without the owner's permission on another's enclosed or cultivated lands, or against the owner's prohibition on other lands; but the owner of the hive may not be prohibited from pursuing the fugitive bees on lands that are not enclosed or cultivated.

**Article 621:** Pigeons that abandon one dovecote and settle in another shall be deemed legitimately occupied by the owner of the second, provided they have not used some industry to attract and domesticate them. In such case they shall be obliged to indemnify for all damage, including restitution of the species, if the owner demands it, and if they do not demand it, to pay their price.

**Article 622:** In other respects, the exercise of hunting and fishing shall be subject to the special ordinances that may be issued on these matters. One may not, therefore, hunt or fish except in places, in seasons, and with weapons and procedures that are not prohibited.

**Article 623:** Domestic animals are subject to dominion. The owner retains this dominion over fugitive domestic animals, even if they have entered another's lands; except insofar as rural or urban police ordinances establish otherwise.

**Article 624:** Invention or finding is a species of occupation by which one who finds an inanimate thing belonging to no one acquires its dominion by taking possession of it. In this way dominion is acquired over stones, shells, and other substances washed up by the sea and showing no signs of previous ownership.

In the same way, things whose property their owner abandons are acquired, such as coins thrown so that the first occupant may make them their own.

Things that navigators throw into the sea to lighten the ship are not presumed abandoned by their owners.

**Article 625:** The discovery of a treasure is a species of invention or finding. Treasure is money or jewels, or other precious effects, which, crafted by man, have been long buried or hidden without memory or trace of their owner.

**Article 626:** Treasure found in another's land shall be divided in equal parts between the owner of the land and the person who made the discovery.

But the latter shall only have the right to their share when the discovery is fortuitous or when the treasure has been searched for with the landowner's permission.

In other cases, or when the landowner and discoverer are the same person, the entire treasure shall belong to the landowner.

**Article 627:** Any person may ask the owner of an estate or building for permission to dig in the ground to extract money or jewels they claim belong to them and are hidden there; and if they indicate the place where they are hidden and give competent security that they will prove their right over them, and that they will reimburse any damage to the owner of the estate or building, the latter may not refuse permission nor oppose the extraction of said money or jewels.

**Article 628:** If their right over said money or jewels is not proved, they shall be considered either as lost property, or as treasure found in another's land, according to the background information and signs.

In this second case, after deducting costs, the treasure shall be divided in equal parts between the denouncer and the landowner; but the latter may not claim damages, unless they renounce their share.

**Article 629:** If some movable species apparently lost is found, it shall be placed at its owner's disposal; and if no one appears to prove it is theirs, it shall be delivered to the competent authority, which shall give notice of the finding in a newspaper of the commune or of the capital of the province or of the capital of the region, if there is none in the commune.

The notice shall designate the kind and quality of the species, the day and place of the finding.

If the owner does not appear, this notice shall be given a third time, with thirty days between each notice.

**Article 630:** If within the month following the last notice no person appears to prove their ownership, the species shall be sold at public auction; the expenses of seizure, preservation, and any others incurred shall be deducted from the proceeds; and the remainder shall be divided in equal parts between the person who found the species and the respective municipality.

**Article 631:** The person who has omitted the diligences ordered here shall lose their share in favor of the municipality, and shall even be subject to action for damages, and according to circumstances, to the penalty for theft.

**Article 632:** If the owner appears before the species is auctioned, it shall be restored to them, upon payment of expenses, and what the competent authority awards as salvage to the one who found and denounced the species.

If the owner has offered a reward for the finding, the denouncer shall choose between the salvage award and the reward offered.

**Article 633:** Once the species is auctioned, it shall be regarded as irrevocably lost to the owner.

**Article 634:** If the species is perishable or its custody and preservation costly, the auction may be advanced, and the owner, appearing before the expiration of the month following the last notice, shall have the right to the price, after deducting, as stated, expenses and the salvage award.

**Article 635:** If any vessel shipwrecks on the coasts of the Republic, or if the sea washes onto them fragments of a vessel, or effects belonging, according to appearances, to the rigging or cargo of a vessel, the persons who see or know of it shall denounce the fact to the competent authority, meanwhile securing the effects that it is possible to save to restore them to whom they belong.

Those who appropriate them shall be subject to action for damages, and to the penalty for theft.

**Article 636:** Wrecked species that are saved shall be restored by the authority to the interested parties, upon payment of expenses and the salvage compensation.

**Article 637:** If interested parties do not appear, proceedings shall be made for the publication of three notices in newspapers, with fifteen days between each notice; and in other respects proceedings shall be as in the case of articles 629 and following.

**Article 638:** The competent authority shall fix, according to circumstances, the salvage compensation, which shall never exceed half the value of the species.

But if the salvage of the species is done under the orders and direction of the public authority, they shall be restored to the interested parties, upon reimbursement of expenses, without salvage compensation.

**Article 639:** All said in articles 635 and following is understood without prejudice to what may be stipulated on this matter with foreign powers, and to fiscal regulations for storage and importation of species.

**Article 640:** The State becomes owner of all property taken in war from nation to nation, not only from enemies but from neutrals, and even from allies and nationals according to circumstances, and disposes of them in accordance with the Ordinances of the Navy and Privateering.

**Article 641:** Prizes made by bandits, pirates, or insurgents do not transfer ownership, and when recaptured must be restored to their owners, who shall pay the salvage award to the recaptors.

This award shall be regulated by that granted to captors in cases analogous to war from nation to nation.

**Article 642:** If the owners do not appear, proceedings shall be as in the case of lost things; but the recaptors shall have over the property not claimed by their owners within one month from the date of the last notice, the same rights as if they had captured them in war from nation to nation.

---

## TITLE V: OF ACCESSION

**Article 643:** Accession is a mode of acquiring by which the owner of a thing becomes the owner of what it produces, or of what is joined to it. The products of things are natural or civil fruits.

### § 1\. Of Accessions of Fruits

**Article 644:** Natural fruits are those given by nature aided or not by human industry.

**Article 645:** Natural fruits are called pending while they still adhere to the thing that produces them, such as plants rooted in the soil, or products of plants while they have not been separated from them.

Natural fruits are called perceived when they have been separated from the productive thing, such as cut timber, harvested fruits and grains, etc.; and they are said to be consumed when they have been truly consumed or have been alienated.

**Article 646:** Natural fruits of a thing belong to its owner; without prejudice to the rights constituted by laws, or by an act of man, for the possessor in good faith, the usufructuary, the lessee.

Thus the vegetation that the land produces spontaneously or by cultivation, and the fruits, seeds, and other products of vegetation, belong to the owner of the land.

Thus also skins, wool, horns, milk, offspring, and other products of animals belong to their owner.

**Article 647:** Civil fruits are the prices, pensions, or rents of leases or ground rents, and the interest of demandable capital, or imposed as a charge on property.

Civil fruits are called pending while they are owed; and perceived from when they are collected.

**Article 648:** Civil fruits also belong to the owner of the thing from which they come, in the same manner and with the same limitation as natural fruits.

### § 2\. Of Accessions of the Soil

**Article 649:** Alluvion is the increase received by the bank of the sea or of a river or lake by the slow and imperceptible retreat of the waters.

**Article 650:** Alluvion land accedes to riparian estates within their respective boundary lines prolonged directly to the water; but in habilitated ports it shall belong to the State.

The soil that the water occupies and vacates alternately in its periodic rises and falls forms part of the bank or bed, and does not accede in the meantime to the adjoining estates.

**Article 651:** Whenever the aforesaid boundary lines, prolonged, intersect each other before reaching the water, the triangle formed by them and by the water's edge shall accede to the two lateral estates; a straight line dividing it into two equal parts, drawn from the point of intersection to the water, shall be the dividing line between the two estates.

**Article 652:** Over the part of the soil that by a flood or other violent natural force is transported from one place to another, the owner retains their ownership, for the sole purpose of taking it away; but if they do not claim it within the following year, the owner of the place to which it was transported shall make it theirs.

**Article 653:** If an estate has been flooded, the land restored by the waters within the following five years shall return to its former owners.

**Article 654:** If a river changes its course, the riparian proprietors, with permission of competent authority, may do the necessary works to restore the waters to their accustomed channel; and the part of the latter that permanently remains dry shall accede to the adjoining estates, like alluvion land in the case of article 650\.

If the riparian proprietors on one side meet with those on the other, a longitudinal line shall divide the new land into two equal parts; and each of these shall accede to the adjoining estates, as in the case of the same article.

**Article 655:** If a river divides into two branches that do not later rejoin, the parts of the previous bed that the water leaves uncovered shall accede to the adjoining estates, as in the case of the preceding article.

**Article 656:** Regarding new islands that are not to belong to the State according to article 597, the following rules shall be observed:

1. The new island shall be regarded as part of the riverbed, as long as it is alternately occupied and vacated by the waters in their periodic rises and falls, and shall not accede in the meantime to the riparian estates.  
     
2. The new island formed by a river that opens into two branches that later rejoin does not alter the previous ownership of the lands comprised in it; but the new land uncovered by the river shall accede to the adjoining estates, as in the case of article 654\.  
     
3. The new island formed in the bed of a river shall accede to the estates of that one of the two banks to which the entire island is closest; each estate corresponding to the part between its respective boundary lines prolonged directly to the island and over its surface.

If the entire island is not closer to one of the two banks than to the other, it shall accede to the estates of both banks; each estate corresponding to the part between its respective boundary lines prolonged directly to the island and over its surface.

The parts of the island that by virtue of these provisions correspond to two or more estates shall be divided in equal parts among the common estates.

4. For the distribution of a new island, any island or islands that pre-existed it shall be entirely disregarded; and the new island shall accede to the riparian estates as if it alone existed.  
     
5. Owners of an island formed by the river acquire ownership of everything by alluvion that accedes to it, whatever bank the new land abandoned by the waters is less distant from.  
     
6. To the new island formed in a lake, paragraph 2 of rule 3 above shall apply; but estates whose lesser distance from the island exceeds half the diameter of the island, measured in the direction of that same distance, shall not have a share in the division of the land formed by the waters.

### § 3\. Of Accession of One Movable Thing to Another

**Article 657:** Adjunction is a species of accession, and occurs when two movable things belonging to different owners are joined one to another, but in such a way that they can be separated and each subsist after separation; such as when the diamond of one person is set in the gold of another, or in another's frame a mirror of one's own is placed.

**Article 658:** In cases of adjunction, without knowledge of the fact on one side, nor bad faith on the other, the ownership of the accessory shall accede to the ownership of the principal, with the burden of paying the owner of the accessory part its value.

**Article 659:** If of the two united things one is of much greater value than the other, the former shall be regarded as the principal and the latter as the accessory.

The thing that has for its owner a great sentimental value shall be regarded as of greater value.

**Article 660:** If there is not so much difference in value, that one of the two things which serves for the use, ornament, or complement of the other shall be considered accessory.
