**Article 661:** In cases to which none of the preceding rules can be applied, the larger in volume shall be regarded as principal.

**Article 662:** Another species of accession is specification, which occurs when from material belonging to one person, another person makes a work or artifact of any kind, such as if from another's grapes wine is made, or from another's silver a cup, or from another's wood a ship.

Without knowledge of the fact on one side, nor bad faith on the other, the owner of the material shall have the right to claim the new species, paying for the workmanship.

Unless in the work or artifact the price of the new species is worth much more than that of the material, such as when a painting is made on another's canvas, or a statue from another's marble; in which case the new species shall belong to the specifier, and the owner of the material shall only have the right to damages.

If the material of the artifact is, in part, another's, and, in part, the own of the one who made or had it made, and the two parts cannot be separated without inconvenience, the species shall belong in common to the two proprietors; to one in proportion to the value of their material, and to the other in proportion to the value of theirs and the workmanship.

**Article 663:** If a thing is formed by mixture of dry or liquid materials belonging to different owners, without knowledge of the fact on one side, nor bad faith on the other, the ownership of the thing shall belong to said owners in common, in proportion to the value of the material belonging to each.

Unless the value of the material belonging to one of them is considerably higher, in which case the owner of it shall have the right to claim the thing produced by the mixture, paying the price of the remaining material.

**Article 664:** In all cases where it is not easy for the owner of one of the two united materials to replace it with another of the same quality, value, and fitness, and the first can be separated without damage to the rest, the owner of it, without whose knowledge the union was made, may demand its separation and delivery, at the cost of the one who used it.

**Article 665:** In all cases where the owner of a material that has been used without their knowledge has the right to the property of the thing in which it has been employed, they shall also have the right to demand that in place of said material they be returned an equal amount of the same nature, quality, and fitness, or its value in money.

**Article 666:** One who has had knowledge of the use that another person was making of their material shall be presumed to have consented and shall only have the right to its value.

**Article 667:** One who has used another's material without the owner's knowledge, and without just cause of error, shall in all cases be subject to losing their own, and to paying what more than this the damages inflicted on the owner are worth; besides the criminal action that may apply, when they have proceeded knowingly.

If the value of the work notably exceeds that of the material, the provision of the preceding paragraph shall not apply; except when knowingly proceeded.

### § 4\. Of Accession of Movable Things to Immovable Things

**Article 668:** If one builds with another's materials on their own land, the owner of the land shall become owner of the materials by the fact of incorporating them into the construction; but shall be obliged to pay the owner of the materials their fair price, or an equal amount of the same nature, quality, and fitness.

If on their part there was no just cause of error, they shall be obliged to reparation of damages, and if they proceeded knowingly, shall also be subject to the competent criminal action; but if the owner of the materials had knowledge of the use being made of them, only the provision of the preceding paragraph shall apply.

The same rule applies to one who plants or sows on their own land another's vegetables or seeds.

While the materials are not incorporated into the construction or the vegetables rooted in the ground, the owner may claim them.

**Article 669:** The owner of the land in which another person, without their knowledge, has built, planted, or sown, shall have the right to make the building, planting, or sowing their own, upon payment of the indemnities prescribed in favor of possessors in good or bad faith in the title On reclamation, or to oblige the one who built or planted to pay them the fair price of the land with legal interest for all the time they have had it in their power, and the one who sowed to pay them the rent and indemnify them for damages.

If building, planting, or sowing has been done with the knowledge and acquiescence of the landowner, they shall be obliged, to recover it, to pay the value of the building, planting, or sowing.

---

## TITLE VI: OF TRADITION

### § 1\. General Provisions

**Article 670:** Tradition is a mode of acquiring the dominion of things and consists of the delivery that the owner makes of them to another, having on the one hand the faculty and intention to transfer the dominion, and on the other the capacity and intention to acquire it.

What is said of dominion extends to all other real rights.

**Article 671:** The person who by tradition transfers the dominion of the thing delivered by them or in their name is called the tradent, and the person who by tradition acquires the dominion of the thing received by them or in their name is called the acquirer.

Their attorneys or legal representatives may deliver and receive in the name of the owner.

In forced sales made by judicial decree at the request of a creditor, at public auction, the person whose dominion is transferred is the tradent, and the judge their legal representative.

Tradition made by or to an attorney duly authorized is understood to be made by or to the respective principal.

**Article 672:** For tradition to be valid, it must be made voluntarily by the tradent or by their representative.

A tradition that was initially invalid for having been made without the will of the tradent or their representative, is validated retroactively by the ratification of the one who has the power to alienate the thing as owner or as representative of the owner.

**Article 673:** Tradition, to be valid, also requires the consent of the acquirer or their representative. But the tradition that was initially invalid for lack of this consent is validated retroactively by ratification.

**Article 674:** For tradition to be valid in which attorneys or legal representatives intervene, it is also required that they act within the limits of their mandate or their legal representation.

**Article 675:** For tradition to be valid, a translative title of dominion is required, such as sale, exchange, donation, etc.

It is also required that the title be valid with respect to the person to whom it is conferred. Thus the title of irrevocable donation does not transfer dominion between spouses.

**Article 676:** It is also required for the validity of tradition that there be no error regarding the identity of the species to be delivered, or of the person to whom the delivery is made, nor regarding the title.

If there is error only in the name, the tradition is valid.

**Article 677:** Error in the title invalidates tradition, whether when only one of the parties supposes a translative title of dominion, such as when on the one hand there is the intention to deliver by way of loan for use, and on the other the intention to receive by way of donation, or whether when both parties suppose translative titles of dominion, but different, such as if on the one hand loan for consumption is supposed, and on the other donation.

**Article 678:** If the tradition is made by means of attorneys or legal representatives, the error of these invalidates the tradition.

**Article 679:** If the law requires special formalities for alienation, dominion is not transferred without them.

**Article 680:** Tradition may transfer dominion under suspensive or resolutory condition, provided it is expressed.

Once delivery is made by the seller, the dominion of the thing sold is transferred, even if the price has not been paid, unless the seller has reserved dominion until payment, or until fulfillment of a condition.

**Article 681:** The tradition of everything owed may be demanded, as long as there is no pending term for its payment; unless a judicial decree intervenes to the contrary.

**Article 682:** If the tradent is not the true owner of the thing delivered by them or in their name, no other rights are acquired by means of tradition than the transmissible rights of the same tradent over the thing delivered.

But if the tradent subsequently acquires the dominion, it shall be understood to have been transferred from the moment of tradition.

**Article 683:** Tradition gives the acquirer, in the cases and in the manner that the laws indicate, the right to gain by prescription the dominion that the tradent lacked, even if the tradent did not have that right.

### § 2\. Of Tradition of Corporeal Movable Things

**Article 684:** Tradition of a corporeal movable thing shall be made by one party signifying to the other that they transfer dominion, and this transfer being represented by one of the following means:

1. Allowing the material apprehension of a present thing;  
2. Showing it;  
3. Delivering the keys of the granary, warehouse, chest, or any place where the thing is kept;  
4. One undertaking to place the thing at the other's disposal in the agreed place; and  
5. By sale, donation, or other title of alienation conferred on the one who has the movable thing as usufructuary, lessee, borrower for use, depositary, or on any other non-translative title of dominion; and reciprocally by the mere contract in which the owner constitutes themselves as usufructuary, borrower for use, lessee, etc.

**Article 685:** When, with permission of the owner of an estate, stones, pending fruits, or other things that form part of the estate are taken from it, tradition is effected at the moment of separation of these objects.

The one to whom the fruits of a crop, vineyard, or plantation are owed may enter to gather them, the day and hour being fixed by common agreement with the owner.

### § 3\. Of Other Species of Tradition

**Article 686:** Tradition of the dominion of real property shall be effected by the registration of the title in the Registrar's Registry. In the same manner, tradition of the rights of usufruct or use constituted over real property, of the rights of habitation or ground rent, and of the right of mortgage shall be effected.

Regarding the tradition of mines, the provisions of the Mining Code shall apply.

**Article 687:** The registration of the title of dominion and of any other of the real rights mentioned in the preceding article shall be made in the Registrar's Registry of the territory in which the immovable is situated, and if it belongs to several territories by location, the registration shall be made in the Registry of each of them.

If the title relates to two or more immovable properties, it shall be registered in the Registrar's Registries of all the territories to which they belong by location.

If by a partition act, the immovable properties or part of the immovable properties that were previously possessed in common are adjudicated to several persons, the partition act relating to each immovable or each adjudicated part shall be registered in the Registrar's Registry in whose territory the immovable is located.

**Article 688:** At the moment the inheritance is granted, effective possession of it is conferred by operation of law to the heir; but this legal possession does not enable the heir to dispose in any way of an immovable, as long as there does not precede:

1. The registration of the judicial decree or the administrative resolution granting effective possession: the former before the registrar of real property of the commune or grouping of communes in which it was pronounced, together with the corresponding will, and the latter in the National Registry of Effective Possessions;  
2. The special registrations provided in the first and second paragraphs of the preceding article: by virtue of them, the heirs may jointly dispose of the hereditary immovable property, and  
3. The registration provided in paragraph three: without this, the heir may not dispose by themselves of the hereditary immovable property that fell to them in the partition.

**Article 689:** Whenever by a final judgment dominion or any other of the rights mentioned in articles 686 and following is recognized as acquired by prescription, this judgment shall serve as title, and shall be registered in the respective Registry or Registries.

**Article 690:** To carry out the registration, an authentic copy of the respective title, and of the judicial decree if applicable, shall be exhibited to the Registrar.

The registration shall begin with the date of this act; shall express the nature and date of the title, the names, surnames, and domiciles of the parties, and the designation of the thing, according to all appearing in the title; shall also express the office or archive where the original title is kept; and shall end with the Registrar's signature.

**Article 691:** The registration of a will shall include the date of its execution; the name, surname, and domicile of the testator; the names, surnames, and domiciles of the heirs or legatees requesting the registration, expressing their shares, or the respective legacies.

The registration of a judgment or decree shall include its date, the designation of the respective court or tribunal, and a literal copy of the dispositive part.

The registration of a legal act of partition shall include the date of this act, the name and surname of the judge-partitioner, and the designation of the parts or lots belonging to those requesting the registration.

The aforementioned registrations shall conform in other respects to the provisions of the preceding article.

**Article 692:** Whenever a right that has been previously registered is transferred, the previous registration shall be mentioned in the new one.

**Article 693:** For the transfer, by donation or contract between living persons, of the ownership of an estate that has not been previously registered, the Registrar shall require proof that notice of said transfer has been given to the public by means of three notices published in a newspaper of the commune or of the capital of the province or of the capital of the region, if there is none in the commune, and by a poster posted for at least fifteen days in the office of the respective Registrar of Real Property.

The same rule shall apply to the constitution or transfer by act between living persons of the other real rights mentioned in the preceding articles, and relating to unregistered immovable property.

**Article 694:** If the registration refers to minutes or documents not kept in the registry or protocol of a public office, said minutes or documents shall be kept in the Registrar's archive, under their custody and responsibility.

**Article 695:** A special regulation shall determine in other respects the duties and functions of the Registrar, and the form and solemnity of registrations.

**Article 696:** Titles whose registration is prescribed in the preceding articles shall not give or transfer effective possession of the respective right, as long as registration is not effected in the manner ordered in said articles; but this provision shall only apply with respect to titles granted after the period indicated in the aforementioned regulation.

**Article 697:** In the intervening time between the date this Code begins to govern and that in which registration becomes obligatory, registration of the real rights mentioned in the preceding articles shall be made in the following manner:

1. That of a right of dominion, usufruct, use, or habitation, by means of a public deed in which the tradent expresses delivering it, and the acquirer receiving it: this deed may be the same as the act or contract by which the right is transferred or constituted;  
2. That of a right of mortgage or ground rent, by annotation in the competent mortgage office;  
3. That of a right of inheritance, by the judicial decree granting effective possession;  
4. That of a legacy, by means of a public deed like that provided in number 1; and  
5. That of the object adjudicated in a partition act, by public deed in which the adjudication and the adjudicatee's acceptance appear.

**Article 698:** Tradition of a right of servitude shall be effected by public deed in which the tradent expresses constituting it, and the acquirer accepting it: this deed may be the same as the act or contract.

**Article 699:** Tradition of personal rights that an individual assigns to another is effected by delivery of the title made by the assignor to the assignee.

---

## TITLE VII: OF POSSESSION

### § 1\. Of Possession and Its Different Qualities

**Article 700:** Possession is the holding of a determined thing with the intention of lord or owner, whether the owner or the one who holds themselves as such has the thing by themselves, or through another person who holds it in their place and name.

The possessor is reputed owner, as long as another person does not justify being so.

**Article 701:** One may possess a thing by several titles.

**Article 702:** Possession may be regular or irregular.

Regular possession is that which proceeds from a just title and has been acquired in good faith; although good faith does not subsist after possession is acquired. One can therefore be a regular possessor and a possessor in bad faith, as conversely the possessor in good faith may be an irregular possessor.

If the title is translative of dominion, tradition is also necessary.

Possession of a thing with the knowledge and acquiescence of the one who obliged themselves to deliver it shall create a presumption of tradition; unless this should have been effected by registration of the title.

**Article 703:** Just title is constitutive or translative of dominion.

Occupation, accession, and prescription are constitutive of dominion.

Transpositive of dominion are those that by their nature serve to transfer it, such as sale, exchange, donation between living persons.

Belonging to this class are judgments of adjudication in divisory lawsuits, and legal acts of partition.

Judicial judgments on litigious rights do not form a new title to legitimize possession.

Transactions insofar as they are limited to recognizing or declaring pre-existing rights do not form a new title; but insofar as they transfer the property of an undisputed object, they constitute a new title.

**Article 704:** The following are not just title:

1. Counterfeit, that is, not actually granted by the person pretended;  
2. Granted by a person as attorney or legal representative of another without being so;  
3. That suffers from a defect of nullity, such as alienation that should have been authorized by a legal representative or by judicial decree and has not been; and  
4. Merely putative, such as the apparent heir who is not really an heir; the legatee whose legacy has been revoked by a later testamentary act, etc.

However, the putative heir to whom effective possession has been granted by judicial decree or administrative resolution shall be served by the decree or resolution as just title; as the putative legatee by the corresponding testamentary act that has been legally executed.

**Article 705:** The validation of the title that in its principle was null, effected by ratification, or by another legal means, retroacts to the date the title was granted.

**Article 706:** Good faith is the consciousness of having acquired the dominion of the thing by legitimate means, free from fraud and any other defect.

Thus in translative titles of dominion, good faith supposes the persuasion of having received the thing from the one who had the faculty to alienate it, and that there has been no fraud or other defect in the act or contract.

A just error in a matter of fact does not oppose good faith.

But error in a matter of law constitutes a presumption of bad faith, which admits no contrary proof.

**Article 707:** Good faith is presumed, except in cases where the law establishes the contrary presumption. In all others, bad faith must be proved.

**Article 708:** Irregular possession is that which lacks one or more of the requirements indicated in article 702\.

**Article 709:** Vicious possessions are violent and clandestine.

**Article 710:** Violent possession is that acquired by force. Force may be actual or imminent.

**Article 711:** One who in the owner's absence takes possession of the thing, and upon the owner's return repels them, is also a violent possessor.

**Article 712:** The vice of violence exists, whether it has been employed against the true owner of the thing, or against the one who possessed it without being so, or against the one who held it in the place or name of another.

It is the same whether the violence is executed by one person or by their agents, and whether it is executed with their consent or if after executed it is ratified expressly or tacitly.

**Article 713:** Clandestine possession is that exercised by hiding it from those who have the right to oppose it.

**Article 714:** Mere holding is that exercised over a thing, not as owner, but in the place or name of the owner. The pledge creditor, the sequestrator, the usufructuary, the user, the one who has the right of habitation, are mere holders of the pledged, sequestered thing, or whose usufruct, use, or habitation belongs to them.

The foregoing applies generally to everyone who holds a thing recognizing another's dominion.

**Article 715:** Possession of incorporeal things is susceptible to the same qualities and vices as possession of a corporeal thing.

**Article 716:** The mere lapse of time does not change mere holding into possession; except in the case of article 2510, rule 3\.

**Article 717:** Whether one succeeds by universal or singular title, the successor's possession begins in them; unless they wish to add their predecessor's possession to theirs; but in such case, they appropriate it with its qualities and vices.

To one's own possession may also be added in the same terms that of a continuous series of predecessors.

**Article 718:** Each of the co-owners of a thing possessed in common shall be understood to have possessed exclusively the part that fell to them by division, during all the time the indivision lasted.

They may therefore add this time to their exclusive possession, and the alienations they have made by themselves of the common thing and the real rights with which they have encumbered it shall subsist over said part if it was included in the alienation or encumbrance. But if what was alienated or encumbered extended to more, the alienation or encumbrance shall not subsist against the will of the respective adjudicatees.

**Article 719:** If one has begun to possess in one's own name, it is presumed that this possession has continued until the moment it is alleged.

If one has begun to possess in another's name, the continuation of the same order of things is equally presumed.

If someone proves having possessed previously, and possesses currently, possession in the intervening time is presumed.

**Article 720:** Possession may be taken not only by the one who seeks to acquire it for themselves, but by their attorney, or by their legal representatives.

### § 2\. Of the Modes of Acquiring and Losing Possession

**Article 721:** If a person takes possession of a thing in the place or name of another of whom they are attorney or legal representative, the possession of the principal or represented begins in the same act, even without their knowledge.

If the one who takes possession in another's name is not their attorney or representative, the latter shall not possess except by virtue of their knowledge and acceptance; but their possession shall retroact to the moment it was taken in their name.

**Article 722:** Possession of the inheritance is acquired from the moment it is granted, even if the heir is unaware of it.

One who validly repudiates an inheritance is understood never to have possessed it.

**Article 723:** Those who cannot freely administer their own property do not need any authorization to acquire possession of a movable thing, provided that will and material or legal apprehension concur; but they cannot exercise the rights of possessors except with the appropriate authorization.

The demented and infants are incapable of acquiring possession by their will, either for themselves or for others.

**Article 724:** If the thing is one of those whose tradition must be made by registration in the Registrar's Registry, no one may acquire possession of it except by this means.

**Article 725:** The possessor retains possession, even if they transfer the holding of the thing, giving it in lease, loan for use, pledge, deposit, usufruct, or by any other non-translative title of dominion.

**Article 726:** One ceases to possess a thing from the moment another takes possession of it with the intention of making it their own; except in cases the laws expressly except.

**Article 727:** Possession of a movable thing is not understood to be lost as long as it remains in the possessor's power, even if they accidentally ignore its whereabouts.

**Article 728:** For registered possession to cease, the registration must be cancelled, either by will of the parties, or by a new registration in which the registered possessor transfers their right to another, or by judicial decree.

As long as the registration subsists, the one who takes possession of the thing to which the registered title refers does not acquire possession of it nor put an end to the existing possession.

**Article 729:** If someone, claiming to be owner, violently or clandestinely takes possession of an immovable whose title is not registered, the one who had possession loses it.

**Article 730:** If the one who holds the thing in the place and name of another usurps it, giving themselves as owner, possession is not lost on one side nor acquired on the other; unless the usurper alienates the thing in their own name. In this case, the person to whom it is alienated acquires possession of the thing and puts an end to the previous possession.

However, if the one who holds the thing in the place and name of a registered possessor gives themselves as owner and alienates it, possession is not lost on one side nor acquired on the other without the competent registration.

**Article 731:** One who legally recovers lost possession shall be understood to have had it during all the intervening time.

---

## TITLE VIII: OF THE LIMITATIONS OF DOMINION AND FIRSTLY OF FIDUCIARY PROPERTY

**Article 732:** Dominion may be limited in several ways:

1. By having to pass to another person by virtue of a condition;  
2. By the encumbrance of a usufruct, use, or habitation, to which a person has a right over things belonging to another; and  
3. By servitudes.

**Article 733:** Fiduciary property is that which is subject to the encumbrance of passing to another person, by the fact of a condition being verified.

The constitution of fiduciary property is called a trust.

This name is also given to the thing constituted in fiduciary property.

The transfer of property to the person in whose favor the trust has been constituted is called restitution.

**Article 734:** A trust may not be constituted except over the entirety of an inheritance or over a determined share of it, or over one or more certain bodies.

**Article 735:** Trusts may not be constituted except by act between living persons granted in a public instrument, or by testamentary act.

The constitution of any trust that includes or affects an immovable shall be registered in the competent Registry.

**Article 736:** The same property may be constituted at once in usufruct in favor of one person and in trust in favor of another.

**Article 737:** The beneficiary may be a person who at the time the fiduciary property is granted does not exist, but is expected to exist.

**Article 738:** The trust always presupposes the express or tacit condition of the existence of the beneficiary, or their substitute, at the time of restitution.

To this condition of existence, others may be added conjunctively or disjunctively.

**Article 739:** Any condition on which restitution of a trust depends, and that takes more than five years to fulfill, shall be deemed failed, unless the death of the fiduciary is the event on which restitution depends.

These five years shall be counted from the grant of the fiduciary property.

**Article 740:** Repealed.

**Article 741:** Dispositions to a day, which do not amount to a condition according to the rules of the title On testamentary assignments, § 3, do not constitute a trust.

**Article 742:** The one who constitutes a trust may appoint not only one, but two or more fiduciaries, and two or more beneficiaries.

**Article 743:** The constituor may give the beneficiary the substitutes they wish in case the beneficiary ceases to exist before restitution, by death or other cause. These substitutions may be of different degrees, one person being substituted for the beneficiary appointed in the first place, another for the first substitute, another for the second, etc.

**Article 744:** No substitutes shall be recognized other than those expressly designated in the respective act between living persons or will.

**Article 745:** It is prohibited to constitute two or more successive trusts, so that the trust once restored to one person is acquired by them with the encumbrance of eventually restoring it to another.

If they are in fact constituted, once the trust is acquired by one of the appointed beneficiaries, the expectation of the others shall be forever extinguished.

**Article 746:** If one or more beneficiaries of the first degree are appointed, and whose existence is to be awaited in accordance with article 737, the entirety of the trust shall be restored in due time to the beneficiaries who exist, and the others shall enter into its enjoyment as the condition imposed with respect to each is fulfilled. But once the period fixed in article 739 has expired, no place shall be given to any other beneficiary.

**Article 747:** Immovable property currently subject to the encumbrance of perpetual trusts, entails, or entailments shall be converted into capitalized ground rents, according to the special law or laws that have been or may be dictated for the purpose.

**Article 748:** When in the constitution of the trust the fiduciary is not expressly designated, or when the designated fiduciary fails for any cause, the condition still pending, the same constituor, if living, or their heirs shall enjoy the property in trust.

**Article 749:** If it is provided that while the condition is pending, the fruits are reserved for the person who, by virtue of the condition being fulfilled or failing, acquires absolute property, the one who is to administer the property shall be a fiduciary holder, who shall only have the faculties of curators of property.

**Article 750:** If there are two or more fiduciary proprietors, there shall be a right of accrual among them, according to the provisions for usufruct in article 780, paragraph 1\.

**Article 751:** Fiduciary property may be alienated between living persons and transmitted by reason of death, but in either case with the burden of keeping it undivided and subject to the encumbrance of restitution under the same conditions as before.

It shall not, however, be alienable between living persons when the constituor has prohibited alienation; nor transmissible by will or intestacy, when the day fixed for restitution is the day of the fiduciary's death; and in this second case, if the fiduciary alienates it during life, their death shall always determine the day of restitution.

**Article 752:** When the constituor has given the fiduciary property to two or more persons, according to article 742, or when the fiduciary's rights are transferred to two or more persons, according to the preceding article, the judge, at the request of any of them, may entrust the administration to the one who gives better security for preservation.

**Article 753:** If a person combines in themselves the character of fiduciary of one share, and absolute owner of another, they shall exercise over both the rights of fiduciary, as long as the property remains undivided; but may request division. The persons designated in article 761 shall intervene in it.

**Article 754:** The fiduciary owner has over the species they may be obliged to restore, the rights and burdens of the usufructuary, with the modifications expressed in the following articles.

**Article 755:** They are not obliged to give security for preservation and restitution, except by virtue of a judge's sentence ordering it as a conservatory provision, requested in accordance with article 761\.

**Article 756:** They are obliged to all extraordinary expenses for the preservation of the thing, including payment of debts and mortgages to which it is subject; but when restitution occurs, they shall have the right to be previously reimbursed by the beneficiary for said expenses, reduced to what they should have cost with medium intelligence and care, and with the following reductions:

1. If invested in material works, such as dikes, bridges, walls, they shall not be reimbursed for these works except for what they are worth at the time of restitution;  
2. If invested in immaterial objects, such as payment of a mortgage, or costs of a lawsuit that could not have been avoided without compromising the beneficiary's rights, a twentieth part shall be deducted from what these objects cost for each year that has elapsed since then until the day of restitution; and if more than twenty have elapsed, nothing shall be owed for this cause.

**Article 757:** Regarding the imposition of mortgages, ground rents, servitudes, and any other encumbrance, property possessed in trust shall be assimilated to the property of the person living under guardianship or curatorship, and the fiduciary's faculties to those of the guardian or curator.

If these encumbrances are imposed without prior judicial authorization with knowledge of the case, and with hearing of those who according to article 761 have the right to request conservatory provisions, the beneficiary shall not be obliged to recognize them.

**Article 758:** In other respects, the fiduciary has free administration of the species included in the trust, and may change their form; but preserving their integrity and value.

They shall be responsible for the diminution and deterioration arising from their act or fault.

**Article 759:** The fiduciary shall have no right to claim anything in reason of non-necessary improvements, except insofar as they have agreed with the beneficiary to whom restitution is made; but they may oppose in compensation the increase in value that the improvements have produced in the species, up to the amount of the indemnity they owe.

**Article 760:** If by the constitution of the trust the fiduciary is expressly granted the right to enjoy the property at their discretion, they shall not be responsible for any deterioration.

If they are also granted free disposal of the property, the beneficiary shall only have the right to claim what exists at the time of restitution.

**Article 761:** The beneficiary, while the condition is pending, has no right over the trust, but the simple expectation of acquiring it.

They may, however, request the conservatory provisions that suit them, if the property appears to be in danger or deteriorating in the fiduciary's hands.

The ascendants of the beneficiary who does not yet exist and whose existence is expected shall have the same right; the representatives of interested corporations and foundations; and the defender of pious works, if the trust is in favor of a charitable establishment.

**Article 762:** The beneficiary who dies before restitution does not transmit by will or intestacy any right over the trust, nor even the simple expectation, which passes ipso jure to the substitute or substitutes designated by the constituor, if any.

**Article 763:** The trust is extinguished:

1. By restitution;  
2. By the resolution of the right of its author, such as when the trust has been constituted over a thing purchased with a pact of retro-sale, and the retro-sale occurs;  
3. By the destruction of the thing in which it is constituted, in accordance with the provisions regarding usufruct in article 807;  
4. By the beneficiary's renunciation before the day of restitution; without prejudice to the rights of substitutes;  
5. By the condition failing or not being fulfilled in time; and  
6. By the confusion of the quality of sole beneficiary with that of sole fiduciary.

---

## TITLE IX: OF THE RIGHT OF USUFRUCT

**Article 764:** The right of usufruct is a real right consisting of the faculty to enjoy a thing with the obligation to preserve its form and substance, and to restore it to its owner, if the thing is not fungible; or with the obligation to return an equal quantity and quality of the same kind, or to pay its value, if the thing is fungible.

**Article 765:** Usufruct necessarily supposes two coexisting rights, that of the naked owner and that of the usufructuary.

It therefore has a limited duration, at the end of which it passes to the naked owner and consolidates with the property.

**Article 766:** The right of usufruct may be constituted in several ways:

1. By law;  
2. By will;  
3. By donation, sale, or other act between living persons;  
4. A usufruct may also be acquired by prescription.

**Article 767:** Usufruct that is to fall on immovable property by act between living persons shall not be valid if not granted by a registered public instrument.

**Article 768:** It is prohibited to constitute any usufruct under a condition or at a term that suspends its exercise. If constituted in fact, it shall have no value.

However, if the usufruct is constituted by will, and the condition has been fulfilled, or the term has expired before the testator's death, the usufruct shall be valid.

**Article 769:** It is prohibited to constitute two or more successive or alternative usufructs. If constituted in fact, the later usufructuaries shall be considered as substitutes, for the case of the earlier ones failing before the first usufruct is granted.

The first usufruct to take effect shall cause the others to lapse; but it shall not last except for the time designated for it.

**Article 770:** Usufruct may be constituted for a determined time or for the entire life of the usufructuary. When no time is fixed in the constitution of the usufruct for its duration, it shall be understood to be constituted for the entire life of the usufructuary.

Usufruct constituted in favor of any corporation or foundation may not exceed thirty years.

**Article 771:** To usufruct constituted for a determined time or for the entire life of the usufructuary, according to the preceding articles, a condition may be added, upon the verification of which it consolidates with the property.

If the condition is not fulfilled before the expiration of said time, or before the death of the usufructuary, as the case may be, it shall be regarded as not written.

**Article 772:** A usufruct may be constituted in favor of two or more persons, to be held simultaneously, equally, or according to the shares determined by the constituor; and in this case, the usufructuaries may divide the usufruct among themselves in any manner they may agree.

**Article 773:** Naked property may be transferred by act between living persons, and transmitted by reason of death. Usufruct is intransmissible by will or intestacy.

**Article 774:** The usufructuary is obliged to receive the fruit-bearing thing in the state in which it is found at the time of the grant, and shall have the right to be indemnified for any diminution or deterioration the thing has suffered since then in the power and by fault of the owner.

**Article 775:** The usufructuary may not have the fruit-bearing thing without having given sufficient security for preservation and restitution, and without a prior solemn inventory at their expense, like that of curators of property.

But both the one who constitutes the usufruct and the owner may exempt the usufructuary from security.

Nor is the donor obliged to it who reserves the usufruct of the donated thing.

The security of the usufructuary of fungible things shall be reduced to the obligation to return the same amount of the same kind and quality, or the value they had at the time of restitution.

**Article 776:** As long as the usufructuary does not give the security to which they are obliged, and the inventory is not finished, the owner shall have the administration with the obligation to give the net value of the fruits to the usufructuary.

**Article 777:** If the usufructuary does not give the security to which they are obliged, within an equitable period fixed by the judge at the instance of the owner, administration shall be adjudicated to the latter, with the obligation to pay the usufructuary the net value of the fruits, deducting the sum the judge fixed for the work and care of administration.

In the same case, the owner may take the fruit-bearing thing in lease, or borrow money on interest, in agreement with the usufructuary.

In agreement with the usufructuary, the owner may also lease the fruit-bearing thing, and lend money on interest.

In agreement with the usufructuary, the owner may also buy or sell fungible things and take or lend on interest the money arising from them.

Movable property included in the usufruct, necessary for the personal use of the usufructuary and their family, shall be delivered to them under oath to restore the species or their respective values, considering the deterioration from time and legitimate use.

The usufructuary may at any time reclaim the administration by giving the security to which they are obliged.

**Article 778:** The owner shall see that the inventory is made with due specification, and may not later challenge it as inaccurate or incomplete.

**Article 779:** It is not lawful for the owner to do anything that prejudices the usufructuary in the exercise of their rights; except with the formal consent of the usufructuary.

If the owner wishes to make necessary repairs, the usufructuary may require that they be done at a reasonable time and with the least possible prejudice to the usufruct.

If the owner transfers or transmits the property, it shall be with the burden of the usufruct constituted on it, even if not expressed.

**Article 780:** If there are two or more usufructuaries, there shall be a right of accrual among them, and the entirety of the usufruct shall last until the expiration of the right of the last of the usufructuaries.

This is understood if the constituor has not provided that once a partial usufruct ends, it consolidates with the property.

**Article 781:** The usufructuary of an immovable thing has the right to receive all natural fruits, including those pending at the time the usufruct is granted.

Reciprocally, fruits still pending at the termination of the usufruct shall belong to the owner.

**Article 782:** The usufructuary of an estate enjoys all active servitudes constituted in its favor, and is subject to all passive servitudes constituted in it.

**Article 783:** The usufructuary's enjoyment of an estate extends to forests and groves, but with the obligation to keep them in good condition, replanting the trees they fell, and being responsible for their deterioration, insofar as it does not depend on natural causes or fortuitous accidents.

**Article 784:** If the fruit-bearing thing includes mines and quarries in actual working, the usufructuary may take advantage of them, and shall not be responsible for the decrease in products that may result, provided they have observed the provisions of the respective ordinance.

**Article 785:** Usufruct of an estate extends to increases it receives by alluvion or other natural accessions.

**Article 786:** The usufructuary has no right over treasures discovered in the soil they usufruct, which the law grants to the owner of the soil.

**Article 787:** The usufructuary of a movable thing has the right to use it according to its nature and destination; and at the end of the usufruct, is not obliged to restore it except in the state in which it is found, being liable only for losses or deteriorations arising from their fraud or fault.

**Article 788:** The usufructuary of livestock or flocks is obliged to replace animals that die or are lost, but only with the natural increase of the same livestock or flocks; unless the death or loss is imputable to their act or fault, in which case they shall indemnify the owner.

If the livestock or flock perishes entirely or in great part by effect of an epidemic or other fortuitous event, the usufructuary shall not be obliged to replace the lost animals, and shall comply by delivering the remains that may have been saved.

**Article 789:** If the usufruct is constituted over fungible things, the usufructuary becomes owner of them, and the owner becomes merely a creditor for the delivery of other species of equal quantity and quality, or of the value they have at the time the usufruct terminates.

**Article 790:** Civil fruits belong to the usufructuary day by day.

**Article 791:** The provisions of the preceding articles are understood without prejudice to agreements on the matter between the naked owner and the usufructuary, or to advantages expressly granted to the naked owner or the usufructuary in the constitution of the usufruct.

**Article 792:** The usufructuary is obliged to respect the leases of the fruit-bearing thing, contracted by the owner before the constitution of the usufruct by act between living persons, or before the death of the person who constituted it by will.

But they succeed in the collection of the rent or pension from the beginning of the usufruct.

**Article 793:** The usufructuary may lease the usufruct and assign it to whomever they wish for consideration or gratuitously.

Once the usufruct is assigned to a third party, the assignor always remains directly responsible to the owner.

But the usufructuary may not lease nor assign their usufruct if the constituor has prohibited it; unless the owner relieves them of the prohibition.

The usufructuary who contravenes this provision shall lose the right of usufruct.

**Article 794:** Even if the usufructuary has the faculty to lease or assign the usufruct by any title, all contracts they have entered into for that purpose shall be resolved at the end of the usufruct.

The owner shall, however, grant the lessee or assignee the time they need for the next perception of fruits; and for that time they shall be substituted for the usufructuary in the contract.

**Article 795:** All ordinary expenses of conservation and cultivation correspond to the usufructuary.

**Article 796:** Pensions, rents, and in general periodic charges with which the fruit-bearing thing had been previously encumbered and which accrue during the usufruct, shall be for the account of the usufructuary. It is not lawful for the naked owner to impose new charges on it to the prejudice of the usufruct.

Payment of periodic fiscal and municipal taxes that encumber it during the usufruct, whenever established, also corresponds to the usufructuary.

If by the usufructuary not making these payments, the owner makes them, or the fruit-bearing thing is alienated or attached, the former shall indemnify the latter for all damage.

**Article 797:** Major works or repairs necessary for the conservation of the fruit-bearing thing shall be for the account of the owner, the usufructuary paying them, during the usufruct, the legal interest on the money invested in them.

The usufructuary shall inform the owner of the major works and repairs required for the conservation of the fruit-bearing thing.

If the owner refuses or delays the performance of these burdens, the usufructuary may, to free the fruit-bearing thing and preserve their usufruct, do them at their own expense, and the owner shall reimburse them without interest.

**Article 798:** Major works or repairs are those occurring once or at long intervals of time, and concerning the conservation and permanent utility of the fruit-bearing thing.

**Article 799:** If a building entirely falls to the ground by old age or fortuitous event, neither the owner nor the usufructuary are obliged to rebuild it.

**Article 800:** The usufructuary may retain the fruit-bearing thing until payment of the reimbursements and indemnities to which, according to the preceding articles, the owner is obliged.

**Article 801:** The usufructuary has no right to claim anything for improvements voluntarily made on the fruit-bearing thing; but they may allege them in compensation for the value of deteriorations imputable to them, or take away the materials, if they can separate them without damage to the fruit-bearing thing, and the owner does not pay them what they would be worth after separation.

This is understood without prejudice to agreements between the usufructuary and the owner regarding improvements, or to what may have been provided on this matter in the constitution of the usufruct.

**Article 802:** The usufructuary is responsible not only for their own acts or omissions, but for the acts of others to which their negligence has given rise.

Consequently, they are responsible for servitudes they have allowed to be acquired over the fruit-bearing estate by their tolerance, and for the harm that usurpations committed in the fruit-bearing thing have inflicted on the owner, if they have not notified the owner in time when able.

**Article 803:** The usufructuary's creditors may request that the usufruct be attached, and that they be paid with it up to the amount of their credits, giving the competent security for preservation and restitution to whom it corresponds.

They may therefore oppose any assignment or renunciation of the usufruct made in fraud of their rights.

**Article 804:** Usufruct is generally extinguished by the arrival of the day or the event of the condition fixed for its termination.

If the usufruct has been constituted until a person other than the usufructuary reaches a certain age, and that person dies before, the usufruct shall nevertheless last until the day that person would have reached that age, if they had lived.

**Article 805:** In the legal duration of usufruct, even the time in which the usufructuary has not enjoyed it, by ignorance or dispossession or any other cause, is counted.

**Article 806:** Usufruct is also extinguished: By the death of the usufructuary, even if it occurs before the day or condition fixed for its termination; By the resolution of the constituor's right, such as when it has been constituted over fiduciary property, and the case of restitution occurs; By consolidation of the usufruct with the property; By prescription; By renunciation of the usufructuary.

**Article 807:** Usufruct is extinguished by complete destruction of the fruit-bearing thing: if only a part is destroyed, the usufruct subsists in the remainder.

If the entire usufruct is reduced to a building, it shall cease forever by the complete destruction of the latter, and the usufructuary shall retain no right over the land.

But if the destroyed building belongs to an estate, the usufructuary of this shall retain their right over the entire estate.

**Article 808:** If a fruit-bearing estate is flooded, and the waters later withdraw, the usufruct shall revive for the time remaining for its termination.

**Article 809:** Usufruct ends, finally, by judge's sentence that at the instance of the owner declares it extinguished, for the usufructuary having failed in their obligations in a serious matter, or for having caused considerable damage or deterioration to the fruit-bearing thing.

The judge, according to the seriousness of the case, may order either that the usufruct absolutely cease, or that the fruit-bearing thing return to the owner, with the obligation to pay the usufructuary a determined annual pension, until the termination of the usufruct.

**Article 810:** The legal usufruct of the father or mother of a family over certain property of the child, and that of the husband, as administrator of the conjugal partnership, over the wife's property, are subject to the special rules of the title On parental authority and the title On the conjugal partnership.

---

## TITLE X: OF THE RIGHTS OF USE AND HABITATION

**Article 811:** The right of use is a real right consisting, generally, of the faculty to enjoy a limited part of the utilities and products of a thing.

If it refers to a house, and to the utility of dwelling in it, it is called the right of habitation.

**Article 812:** The rights of use and habitation are constituted and lost in the same manner as usufruct.

**Article 813:** Neither the user nor the inhabitant shall be obliged to give security.

But the inhabitant is obliged to an inventory; and the same obligation shall extend to the user, if the use is constituted over things that must be restored in species.

**Article 814:** The extent in which the right of use or habitation is granted is determined by the title that constitutes it, and in default of this determination in the title, it is regulated by the following articles.

**Article 815:** Use and habitation are limited to the personal needs of the user or inhabitant.

In the personal needs of the user or inhabitant are included those of their family.

The family includes the spouse and children; both those existing at the time of constitution, and those born later, and this even if the user or inhabitant is not married, nor has recognized any child on the date of constitution.

It also includes the number of servants necessary for the family.

It also includes persons who at the same date lived with the inhabitant or user and at their expense; and persons to whom they owe maintenance.

**Article 816:** In the personal needs of the user or inhabitant, those of the industry or trade in which they are engaged are not included.

Thus the user of animals may not employ them in carrying objects they trade in, nor the inhabitant use the house for shops or warehouses.

Unless the thing in which the right is granted, by its nature and ordinary use, and by its relation to the profession or industry of the one who is to exercise it, appears destined to serve them in it.

**Article 817:** The user of an estate has only the right to the common objects of food and fuel, not to those of superior quality; and is obliged to receive them from the owner or to take them with their permission.

**Article 818:** The user and the inhabitant must use the objects included in their respective rights with the moderation and care of a good father of a family; and are obliged to contribute to the ordinary expenses of conservation and cultivation, in proportion to the benefit they derive.

This latter obligation does not extend to use or habitation given charitably to needy persons.

**Article 819:** The rights of use and habitation are intransmissible to heirs, and may not be assigned by any title, lent, or leased.

Neither the user nor the inhabitant may lease, lend, or alienate any object to which the exercise of their right extends.

But they may give the fruits it is lawful for them to consume for their personal needs.

---

## TITLE XI: OF SERVITUDES

**Article 820:** An easement, or simply servitude, is an encumbrance imposed on one estate for the utility of another estate of a different owner.

**Article 821:** The servient estate is the one that suffers the encumbrance, and the dominant estate the one that derives the utility.

With respect to the dominant estate, the servitude is called active, and with respect to the servient estate, passive.

**Article 822:** A continuous servitude is one exercised or that can be exercised continuously, without the need for an actual act of man, such as the servitude of aqueduct through an artificial canal belonging to the dominant estate; and a discontinuous servitude is one exercised at more or less long intervals of time, and supposes an actual act of man, such as the servitude of transit.

**Article 823:** A positive servitude is, in general, one that only imposes on the owner of the servient estate the obligation to allow doing, such as either of the previous two; and a negative servitude, one that imposes on the owner of the servient estate the prohibition of doing something that, without the servitude, would be lawful for them, such as not being able to raise their walls except to a certain height.

Positive servitudes sometimes impose on the owner of the servient estate the obligation to do something, such as that of article 842\.

**Article 824:** An apparent servitude is one continuously in view, such as the servitude of transit, when it is made through a path or a door especially destined for it; and a non-apparent servitude, one not known by an exterior sign, such as the same servitude of transit when it lacks these two circumstances and other analogous ones.

**Article 825:** Servitudes are inseparable from the estate to which they actively or passively belong.

**Article 826:** Once the servient estate is divided, the servitude constituted on it does not change, and must be suffered by those to whom the part in which it was exercised belongs.

**Article 827:** Once the dominant estate is divided, each of the new owners shall enjoy the servitude, but without increasing the encumbrance of the servient estate.

Thus the new owners of the estate enjoying a servitude of transit cannot require that the direction, form, quality, or width of the path or road destined for it be altered.

**Article 828:** The one who has the right to a servitude has the right to the necessary means to exercise it.

Thus, the one who has the right to draw water from a fountain situated on the neighboring estate has the right of transit to go to it, even if not expressly established in the title.

**Article 829:** The one enjoying a servitude may do the indispensable works to exercise it; but they shall be at their own expense, if not otherwise established; and even if the owner of the servient estate has obliged themselves to do or repair them, they may free themselves from the obligation by abandoning the part of the estate on which the works are to be done or maintained.

**Article 830:** The owner of the servient estate may not alter, diminish, or make more inconvenient for the dominant estate the servitude with which theirs is encumbered.

However, if by the lapse of time the primitive mode of the servitude becomes more onerous for the servient owner, they may propose that it be changed at their own expense; and if the changes do not prejudice the dominant estate, they shall be accepted.

**Article 831:** Servitudes are either natural, which arise from the natural situation of the places, or legal, which are imposed by law, or voluntary, which are constituted by an act of man.

**Article 832:** The provisions of this title are understood without prejudice to the general or local ordinances on servitudes.

### § 1\. Of Natural Servitudes

**Article 833:** The lower estate is subject to receiving the waters that descend from the upper estate naturally, that is, without the hand of man contributing to it.

One may not therefore direct a drain or ditch over the neighboring estate, if this special servitude has not been constituted.

On the servient estate, nothing may be done that hinders the natural servitude, nor on the dominant estate, that aggravates it.

The servitudes established in this article shall be governed by the Water Code.

**Articles 834-838:** Repealed.

### § 2\. Of Legal Servitudes

**Article 839:** Legal servitudes relate to public use, or to the utility of private individuals.

Legal servitudes relating to public use are:

The use of banks insofar as necessary for navigation or floating, which shall be governed by the Water Code; And others determined by the respective regulations or ordinances.

**Article 840:** Repealed.

**Article 841:** Legal servitudes of the second kind are likewise determined by the ordinances of rural police. Here treatment is especially of those of demarcation, enclosure, transit, party walls, aqueduct, light, and view.

**Article 842:** Every owner of an estate has the right to have the boundaries separating it from neighboring estates fixed, and may require the respective owners to concur in it, the demarcation being made at common expense.

**Article 843:** If any of the landmarks delimiting neighboring estates has been removed from its place, the owner of the prejudiced estate has the right to request that the one who removed it replace it at their own expense, and indemnify them for the damages arising from the removal, without prejudice to the penalties with which the laws punish the crime.

**Article 844:** The owner of an estate has the right to enclose or fence it on all sides, without prejudice to the servitudes constituted in favor of other estates. The enclosure may consist of walls, ditches, live or dead fences.

**Article 845:** If the owner makes the enclosure of the estate at their own expense and on their own land, they may do it of the quality and dimensions they wish, and the neighboring owner may not use the wall, ditch, or fence for any purpose, unless they have acquired this right by title or by prescription of five years counted as for the acquisition of dominion.

**Article 846:** The owner of an estate may oblige the owners of neighboring estates to concur in the construction and repair of common dividing fences.

The judge, if necessary, shall regulate the manner and form of concurrence; so that no owner is imposed with a ruinous encumbrance.

The dividing fence constructed at common expense shall be subject to the servitude of party wall.

**Article 847:** If an estate is destitute of all communication with the public road by the interposition of other estates, the owner of the first shall have the right to impose on the others the servitude of transit, insofar as indispensable for the use and benefit of their estate, paying the value of the land necessary for the servitude and repairing all other damage.

**Article 848:** If the parties do not agree, the amount of indemnity and the exercise of the servitude shall be regulated by experts.

**Article 849:** If, once the servitude of transit is granted in accordance with the preceding articles, it ceases to be indispensable for the dominant estate, by the acquisition of lands that give it easy access to the road, or by other means, the owner of the servient estate shall have the right to request to be released from the servitude, restoring what was paid to them for the value of the land when it was established.

**Article 850:** If any part of an estate is sold or exchanged, or is adjudicated to any of the co-owners, and consequently this part comes to be separated from the road, a servitude of transit shall be understood to be granted in its favor, without any indemnity.

**Article 851:** Party wall is a legal servitude by virtue of which the owners of two neighboring estates that have common dividing walls, ditches, or fences are subject to the reciprocal obligations to be expressed.

**Article 852:** The right of party wall exists for each of the two neighboring owners, when it appears by any sign that they have made the enclosure by agreement and at common expense.

**Article 853:** Any wall of separation between two buildings is presumed a party wall, but only in the part that is common to the buildings themselves.

Every enclosure between yards, gardens, and fields is presumed a party wall, when each of the contiguous surfaces is enclosed on all sides: if only one is enclosed in this way, the enclosure is presumed to belong exclusively to it.

**Article 854:** In all cases, and even when it appears that a fence or dividing wall belongs exclusively to one of the contiguous estates, the owner of the other estate shall have the right to make it a party wall in whole or in part, even without their neighbor's consent, paying them half the value of the land on which the enclosure is made, and half the current value of the portion of enclosure whose party wall they seek.

**Article 855:** Either of the two co-owners who wishes to use the party wall to build on it, or to have it support the weight of a new construction, must first request their neighbor's consent, and if the latter refuses, shall provoke a practical hearing in which necessary measures are ordered so that the new construction does not harm the neighbor.

In ordinary circumstances, it shall be understood that either of the co-owners of a party wall may build on it, inserting beams up to the distance of one decimeter from the opposite surface; and that if the neighbor wishes to insert beams in the same place or make a chimney, they shall have the right to cut back their neighbor's beams to the middle of the wall, without dislocating them.

**Article 856:** If it concerns wells, latrines, stables, chimneys, hearths, forges, ovens, or other works from which damage may result to neighboring buildings or estates, the rules prescribed by the general or local ordinances shall be observed, whether the dividing wall is a party wall or not. The same applies to deposits of gunpowder, humid or infected materials, and everything that may damage the solidity, security, and healthfulness of buildings.

**Article 857:** Either of the co-owners has the right to raise the party wall, insofar as permitted by the general or local ordinances; subject to the following rules:

1. The new work shall be entirely at their own expense.  
2. They shall pay the neighbor, as indemnity for the increase in weight about to be placed on the party wall, one-sixth of the value of the new work.  
3. They shall pay the same indemnity each time the party wall is to be rebuilt.  
4. They shall be obliged to raise at their own expense the neighbor's chimneys situated in the party wall.  
5. If the party wall is not sufficiently solid to support the increased weight, they shall rebuild it at their own expense, indemnifying the neighbor for the removal and replacement of everything on the neighbor's side that was loaded on or attached to the wall.  
6. If in rebuilding the party wall it is necessary to increase its thickness, this increase shall be taken from the land of the one constructing the new work.  
7. The neighbor may at any time acquire the party wall of the newly raised part, paying half the total cost of this, and the value of half the land on which the party wall has been extended, according to the preceding paragraph.

**Article 858:** The expenses of construction, conservation, and repair of the enclosure shall be for the account of all those who have property rights in it, in proportion to their respective rights.

However, either of them may relieve themselves of this burden by abandoning their party wall right, but only when the enclosure does not consist of a wall supporting a building belonging to them.

**Article 859:** Trees found in the party wall fence are likewise party wall; and the same extends to trees whose trunk is on the dividing line of two estates, even if there is no intervening fence.

Either of the two co-owners may require that said trees be felled, proving that they in some way harm them; and if by any accident they are destroyed, they shall not be replanted without their consent.

**Article 860:** Repealed.

**Article 861:** Every estate is subject to the servitude of aqueduct in favor of another estate lacking the water necessary for the cultivation of crops, plantings, or pastures, or in favor of a town needing it for the domestic service of its inhabitants, or in favor of an industrial establishment needing it for the movement of its machinery.

This servitude consists in the water being able to be conducted through the servient estate at the expense of the interested party; and is subject to the rules prescribed by the Water Code.

**Articles 862-869:** Repealed.

**Article 870:** The rules established in the Water Code for the servitude of aqueduct extend to those constructed to give outlet and direction to surplus waters, and to drain marshes and natural seepage by means of ditches and drainage canals.

**Articles 871-872:** Repealed.

**Article 873:** The legal servitude of light has for its object to give light to any enclosed and roofed space; but it is not directed to give it a view over the neighboring estate, whether enclosed or not.

**Article 874:** No window or opening of any kind may be opened in a party wall without the co-owner's consent.

The owner of a non-party wall may open them in it, in the number and dimensions they wish.

If the wall is not a party wall except in part of its height, the owner of the non-party part enjoys the same right in this part.

The contiguity of the wall to the neighboring estate does not oppose the exercise of the servitude of light.

**Article 875:** The legal servitude of light is subject to the following conditions:

1. The window shall be provided with iron bars, and a wire mesh whose openings have three centimeters or less.  
2. The lower part of the window shall be at least three meters from the floor of the dwelling to which it gives light.

**Article 876:** The one enjoying the servitude of light shall not have the right to prevent a wall from being raised on the neighboring land that takes away their light.

**Article 877:** If the dividing wall becomes a party wall, the legal servitude of light ceases, and only voluntary servitude applies, determined by mutual consent of both owners.

**Article 878:** Windows, balconies, belvederes, or terraces that give a view to the rooms, yards, or corrals of a neighboring estate, whether enclosed or not, may not be had unless there is a distance of three meters.

The distance shall be measured between the vertical plane of the most prominent line of the window, balcony, etc., and the vertical plane of the dividing line of the two estates, both planes being parallel.

If the two planes are not parallel, the same measure shall be applied to the lesser distance between them.

**Article 879:** There is no legal servitude of rainwater. The roofs of every building must shed their rainwater onto the estate to which they belong, or onto the public or neighborhood street or road, and not onto another estate, except with the consent of its owner.

### § 3\. Of Voluntary Servitudes

**Article 880:** Anyone may subject their estate to the servitudes they wish, and acquire them over neighboring estates with the will of their owners, provided they do not harm public order, nor contravene the laws.

Servitudes of this kind may also be acquired by judge's sentence in cases provided by the laws.

**Article 881:** If the owner of an estate establishes a continuous and apparent service in favor of another estate that also belongs to them, and later alienates one of them, or they pass to diverse owners by partition, the same service shall subsist with the character of servitude between the two estates, unless in the constitutive title of the alienation or of the partition it has been expressly established otherwise.

**Article 882:** Discontinuous servitudes of all classes and continuous non-apparent servitudes may only be acquired by means of a title; nor will immemorial enjoyment suffice to constitute them.

Continuous and apparent servitudes may be acquired by title, or by prescription of five years.

**Article 883:** The constitutive title of servitude may be supplied by the express recognition of the owner of the servient estate.

The previous destination, according to article 881, may also serve as title.

**Article 884:** The title, or possession of the servitude for the period indicated in article 882, determines the rights of the dominant estate and the obligations of the servient estate.

### § 4\. Of the Extinction of Servitudes

**Article 885:** Servitudes are extinguished:

1. By the resolution of the right of the one who constituted them;  
2. By the arrival of the day or condition, if established in one of these ways;  
3. By confusion, that is, the perfect and irrevocable reunion of both estates in the hands of the same owner.

Thus, when the owner of one of them purchases the other, the servitude perishes, and if by a new sale they are separated, it does not revive; except in the case of article 881: on the contrary, if the conjugal partnership acquires an estate that owes servitude to another estate of one of the spouses, there shall be no confusion except when, upon dissolution of the partnership, both estates are adjudicated to the same person;

4. By renunciation of the owner of the dominant estate;  
5. By having ceased to be enjoyed for three years.

In discontinuous servitudes, time runs from when they ceased to be enjoyed; in continuous ones, from when an act contrary to the servitude has been executed.

**Article 886:** If the dominant estate belongs to many in common, the enjoyment of one of them interrupts prescription with respect to all; and if prescription cannot run against one of them, it cannot run against any.

**Article 887:** If the servitude ceases because things are in such a state that it is impossible to use them, it shall revive when the impossibility ceases, provided this happens before three years have elapsed.

**Article 888:** A particular mode of exercising the servitude may be acquired and lost by prescription, in the same manner as the servitude itself could be acquired or lost.

---

## TITLE XII: OF RECLAMATION

**Article 889:** Reclamation or action of dominion is that which the owner of a singular thing, not in possession, has for the possessor of it to be condemned to restore it to them.

### § 1\. What Things May Be Reclaimed

**Article 890:** Corporeal things, real and movable, may be reclaimed.

Excepted are movable things whose possessor has purchased them in a fair, shop, warehouse, or other industrial establishment where movable things of the same class are sold.

Once this circumstance is justified, the possessor shall not be obliged to restore the thing, unless they are reimbursed for what they gave for it and what they spent on repairing and improving it.

**Article 891:** Other real rights may be reclaimed like dominion; except the right of inheritance.

This right gives rise to the action of petition of inheritance, dealt with in Book III.

**Article 892:** A determined share in common of a singular thing may be reclaimed.

### § 2\. Who May Claim

**Article 893:** The reclamation or action of dominion corresponds to the one who has full or naked property, absolute or fiduciary, of the thing.

**Article 894:** The same action is granted, even if ownership is not proved, to the one who has lost regular possession of the thing, and was in the case of being able to gain it by prescription.

But it shall not be valid against the true owner, nor against the one who possesses with equal or better right.

### § 3\. Against Whom It May Be Reclaimed

**Article 895:** The action of dominion is directed against the current possessor.

**Article 896:** The mere holder of the thing being reclaimed is obliged to declare the name and residence of the person in whose name they hold it.

**Article 897:** If someone, in bad faith, gives themselves as possessor of the thing being reclaimed without being so, they shall be condemned to indemnify all damage resulting to the actor from this deception.

**Article 898:** The action of dominion shall also apply against the one who alienated the thing, for the restitution of what they received for it, whenever by having alienated it its pursuit has become impossible or difficult; and if they alienated it knowing it was another's, for indemnification of all damage.

The reclaimer who receives from the alienator what was given for the thing, by the same fact confirms the alienation.

**Article 899:** The action of dominion is not directed against an heir except for the part they possess in the thing; but the payments to which the possessor was obliged by reason of fruits or deteriorations imputable to them pass to their heirs in proportion to their hereditary shares.

**Article 900:** Against the one who possessed in bad faith and by their act or fault has ceased to possess, the action of dominion may be brought as if they currently possessed.

In whatever way they have ceased to possess, and even if the reclaimer prefers to direct themselves against the current possessor, with respect to the time the thing has been in their power, they shall have the obligations and rights that according to this title correspond to possessors in bad faith in reason of fruits, deteriorations, and expenses.

If they pay the value of the thing and the reclaimer accepts it, they shall succeed in the reclaimer's rights over it.

The same applies even to the possessor in good faith who during the lawsuit has placed themselves in the impossibility of restoring the thing through their fault.

The reclaimer in the cases of the two preceding paragraphs shall not be obliged to warranty.

**Article 901:** If a corporeal movable thing is being reclaimed, and there is reason to fear it will be lost or deteriorate in the possessor's hands, the actor may request its sequestration; and the possessor shall be obliged to consent to it, or to give sufficient security for restitution, for the case of being condemned to restore it.

**Article 902:** If the dominion or other real right constituted over an immovable is claimed, the possessor shall continue to enjoy it until final judgment passed into res judicata.

But the actor shall have the right to request the necessary provisions to prevent any deterioration of the thing, and of the movable property and livestock attached to it and included in the claim, if there is just cause to fear it, or the defendant's means do not offer sufficient guarantee.

**Article 903:** The reclamation action extends to the attachment, in the hands of a third party, of what is owed by them as price or exchange to the possessor who alienated the thing.

### § 4\. Mutual Payments

**Article 904:** If the possessor is defeated, they shall restore the thing within the period the judge fixes; and if the thing was sequestered, the actor shall pay the sequestrator the expenses of custody and conservation, and shall have the right for the possessor in bad faith to reimburse them.

**Article 905:** In the restitution of an estate are included things that form part of it, or that are deemed immovable by their connection with it, according to what is said in the title On the various classes of property. Others shall not be included in restitution, if not included in the claim and judgment; but they may be reclaimed separately.

In the restitution of a building, its keys are included.

In the restitution of any thing, titles concerning it are included, if found in the possessor's hands.
