If the legacy of an alimentary pension is a continuation of what the testator paid during life, it shall continue to be provided as if the testator had not died.

Over all these rules, the testator's express will shall prevail.

**Article 1362:** Legatees are not obliged to contribute to the payment of legitimate shares, of assignments made with charge to the fourth of improvements, or of hereditary debts, except when the testator destines to legacies any part of the portion of property the law reserves for legitimaries or for compulsory assignees of the fourth of improvements, or when at the time the succession opens there has not been enough in it to pay hereditary debts.

The action of hereditary creditors against legatees is in subsidium of that they have against heirs.

**Article 1363:** Legatees who must contribute to the payment of legitimate shares, of assignments with charge to the fourth of improvements, or of hereditary debts, shall do so in proportion to the values of their respective legacies, and the portion of the insolvent legatee shall not burden the others.

However, those whom the testator has expressly exempted from doing so shall not contribute with the other legatees. But if, after the contributions of the other legatees are exhausted, a legitimate share remains incomplete or a debt insolvent, even legatees exempted by the testator shall be obliged to pay.

Legacies of pious or charitable works shall be understood to be exempted by the testator, without need for express disposition, and shall enter into contribution after expressly exempted legacies; but strictly alimentary legacies to which the testator is obliged by law shall not enter into contribution except after all others.

**Article 1364:** The legatee obliged to pay a legacy shall be obliged only up to the amount of the benefit they derive from the succession; but must prove the amount by which the burden exceeds the benefit.

**Article 1365:** If several immovable properties of the succession are subject to a mortgage, the mortgage creditor shall have joint action against each of said immovable properties, without prejudice to the recourse of the heir to whom the immovable belongs against their co-heirs for the share of the debt that falls to them.

Even if the creditor has subrogated the owner of the immovable in their actions against their co-heirs, each of the latter shall only be responsible for the part falling to them in the debt.

But the portion of the insolvent shall be distributed among all heirs proportionally.

**Article 1366:** The legatee who by virtue of a mortgage or pledge on the bequeathed species has paid a hereditary debt with which the testator has not expressly intended to burden them is subrogated by law in the creditor's action against the heirs.

If the mortgage or pledge has been accessory to the obligation of another person than the testator himself, the legatee shall have no action against the heirs.

**Article 1367:** Legacies with an onerous cause estimable in money contribute only with deduction of the burden, and the following circumstances concurring:

1. That the object has been effected.  
2. That it could not be effected except by the investment of a determined amount of money.

Both circumstances must be proved by the legatee, and only the amount proven to have been invested shall be deducted by reason of the burden.

**Article 1368:** If the testator leaves the usufruct of a part of their property or of all of it to one person and the bare property to another, the owner and the usufructuary shall be considered as a single person for the distribution of hereditary and testamentary obligations falling on the fruit-bearing thing; and the obligations jointly falling on them shall be divided between them according to the following rules:

1. It shall be the owner's responsibility to pay the debts falling on the fruit-bearing thing, with the usufructuary obliged to pay them the current interest on the amount paid, for all the time the usufruct continues.  
2. If the owner does not agree to this payment, the usufructuary may make it, and at the expiration of the usufruct shall have the right for the owner to reimburse the capital without interest.  
3. If the fruit-bearing thing is sold to cover a mortgage or pledge constituted on it by the deceased, the provision of article 1366 shall apply to the usufructuary.

**Article 1369:** Testamentary burdens falling on the usufructuary or on the owner shall be satisfied by whichever of the two the will imposes them on and in the manner ordered therein; without any indemnity or interest corresponding by the fact of satisfying them in that way.

**Article 1370:** When testamentary burdens are imposed on a thing in usufruct, if the testator does not determine whether it is the owner or the usufructuary who must suffer them, proceedings shall be in accordance with the provisions of article 1368\.

But if the burdens consist of periodic pensions, and the testator has not ordered otherwise, they shall be covered by the usufructuary during the entire time of the usufruct, and they shall have no right for the owner to indemnify them for this disbursement.

**Article 1371:** Usufruct constituted in the partition of an inheritance is subject to the rules of article 1368, if the interested parties have not agreed otherwise.

**Article 1372:** The fiduciary owner and the beneficiary shall in all cases be considered as a single person with respect to other assignees for the distribution of hereditary and testamentary debts and burdens, and the division of debts and burdens shall be made between the two in the following manner:

The fiduciary shall suffer said burdens with the quality that in due time the beneficiary reimburse them without interest.

If the burdens are periodic, the fiduciary shall suffer them without right to any indemnity.

**Article 1373:** Testamentary creditors may only exercise the actions the will gives them according to article 1360\.

If in the partition of an inheritance the legacies are distributed among the heirs in a different way, the legatees may bring their actions either in accordance with this distribution, or in accordance with article 1360, or in accordance with the agreement of the heirs.

**Article 1374:** If there is no concurrence of creditors, nor third-party opposition, hereditary creditors shall be paid as they present themselves, and once hereditary creditors are paid, legacies shall be satisfied.

But when the inheritance does not appear excessively burdened, legatees who offer security to cover their share in the contribution to debts may be paid immediately.

Nor shall this security be demandable when the inheritance is manifestly free from burdens that may compromise the legatees.

**Article 1375:** Necessary expenses for the delivery of bequeathed things shall be regarded as part of the same legacies.

**Article 1376:** If there is not enough in the succession for the payment of all legacies, they shall be reduced proportionally.

**Article 1377:** Executive titles against the deceased shall likewise be against the heirs; but creditors may not bring or carry out execution except eight days after judicial notification of their titles.

---

## TITLE XII: OF THE BENEFIT OF SEPARATION

**Article 1378:** Hereditary creditors and testamentary creditors may request that the deceased's property not be confused with the heir's property; and by virtue of this benefit of separation, they shall have the right for hereditary or testamentary obligations to be fulfilled from the deceased's property in preference to the heir's own debts.

**Article 1379:** To be able to obtain the benefit of separation, it is not necessary that what is owed be immediately demandable; it suffices that it is owed on a certain day or under condition.

**Article 1380:** Each creditor's right to request the benefit of separation subsists as long as their credit has not prescribed; but it does not apply in two cases:

1. When the creditor has recognized the heir as debtor, accepting a promissory note, pledge, mortgage, or surety from said heir, or a partial payment of the debt;  
2. When the succession property has already left the heir's hands, or has been confused with the heir's property, so that it is not possible to recognize them.

**Article 1381:** The heir's creditors shall not have the right to request, for the benefit of their credits, the separation of property mentioned in the preceding articles.

**Article 1382:** Once the separation of estates is obtained by some of the succession creditors, it shall benefit other creditors of the same succession who invoke it and whose credits have not prescribed, or who are not in the case of number 1 of article 1380\.

The surplus, if any, shall be added to the heir's property, to satisfy their own creditors, with whom succession creditors who do not enjoy the benefit shall concur.

**Article 1383:** Hereditary or testamentary creditors who have obtained separation, or taking advantage of it in accordance with paragraph 1 of the preceding article, shall have no action against the heir's property except after the property to which said benefit gave them a preferential right is exhausted; but even then, the heir's other creditors may oppose this action until they are satisfied in the total of their credits.

**Article 1384:** Alienations of the deceased's property made by the heir within six months following the opening of the succession, and which have not had for their object the payment of hereditary or testamentary credits, may be rescinded at the instance of any of the hereditary or testamentary creditors who enjoy the benefit of separation. The same extends to the constitution of mortgages or ground rents.

**Article 1385:** If there is real property in the succession, the decree granting the benefit of separation shall be registered in the Registry or Registries corresponding by the location of said property, with expression of the estates to which the benefit extends.

---

## TITLE XIII: OF DONATIONS BETWEEN LIVING PERSONS

**Article 1386:** A donation between living persons is an act by which a person gratuitously and irrevocably transfers a part of their property to another person, who accepts it.

**Article 1387:** Every person not declared incompetent by law is competent to donate between living persons.

**Article 1388:** Those who do not have the free administration of their property are incompetent to donate; except in the cases and with the requirements prescribed by law.

**Article 1389:** Every person not declared incapable by law is capable of receiving between living persons.

**Article 1390:** A donation between living persons may not be made to a person who does not exist at the time of the donation.

If donated under suspensive condition, it shall also be necessary to exist at the time the condition is fulfilled; except for the exceptions indicated in paragraphs 3 and 4 of article 962\.

**Article 1391:** The incapacities to receive inheritances and legacies according to articles 963 and 964 extend to donations between living persons.

**Article 1392:** Likewise null is the donation made to the donor's curator, before the curator has exhibited the accounts of the curatorship, and paid the balance, if there is one against them.

**Article 1393:** Donation between living persons is not presumed, except in cases expressly provided by law.

**Article 1394:** One who repudiates an inheritance, legacy, or donation, or fails to fulfill the condition to which an eventual right is subordinate, does not donate, even if they do so with the object of benefiting a third party.

Creditors may, however, be authorized by the judge to substitute themselves for a debtor who so acts, up to the amount of their credits; and of the surplus, if any, the third party shall take advantage.

**Article 1395:** There is no donation in the loan for use of any object, even if its use or enjoyment is customarily given on lease.

Nor is there in the loan for consumption without interest.

But there is in the remission or assignment of the right to receive interest on capital placed on interest or ground rent.

**Article 1396:** Gratuitous personal services do not constitute a donation, even if of those ordinarily paid.

**Article 1397:** One who constitutes themselves as surety for a third party, or constitutes a pledge or mortgage in favor of the latter, does not make a donation to them; nor does one who releases the surety from their obligations, or remits a pledge or mortgage, as long as the debtor is solvent; but one who remits a debt, or who knowingly pays what they do not owe, makes a donation.

**Article 1398:** There is no donation if, having on the one hand a decrease of patrimony, there is no on the other an increase; as when one gives for an object that consumes the value of the donated thing, and from which the donee derives no appreciable advantage in money.

**Article 1399:** There is no donation in failing to interrupt prescription.

**Article 1400:** A donation between living persons of any kind of real property shall not be valid unless executed by public deed and registered in the competent Registry.

Nor shall the remission of a debt of the same kind of property be valid without this requirement.

**Article 1401:** A donation between living persons not insinuated shall only take effect up to the value of two centavos, and shall be null in excess.

Insinuation is the authorization of the competent judge, requested by the donor or donee.

The judge shall authorize donations in which no legal provision is contravened.

**Article 1402:** When what is donated is the right to receive a quantity periodically, insinuation shall be necessary whenever the sum of the quantities to be received in a decade exceeds two centavos.

**Article 1403:** A donation at term or under condition shall produce no effect if not proven by private or public deed expressing the condition or term; and in it, public deed and insinuation and registration shall be necessary in the same terms as for present donations.

**Article 1404:** Donations with an onerous cause, such as for a person to embrace a career or state, or by way of dowry or for reason of marriage, shall be executed by public deed, expressing the cause; and if not, they shall be considered as gratuitous donations.

Donations with an onerous cause, mentioned in the preceding paragraph, are subject to insinuation in the terms of articles 1401, 1402, and 1403\.

**Article 1405:** Donations in which a pecuniary burden or one that can be valued in a determined sum of money is imposed on the donee are not subject to insinuation, except with discount of the burden.

**Article 1406:** Donations that spouses make to each other in matrimonial capitulations, with due requirements, do not require insinuation, nor any other public deed than the same capitulations, whatever the class or value of the donated things.

**Article 1407:** Universal donations, whether of the totality or of a share of property, require, in addition to insinuation and execution of public deed, and registration if applicable, a solemn inventory of the property, under penalty of nullity.

If any part of the property is omitted in this inventory, the donor shall be understood to reserve it, and the donee shall have no right to claim it.

**Article 1408:** One who makes a donation of all their property shall reserve what is necessary for their congruous subsistence; and if they omit to do so, they may at any time oblige the donee, from the donated property or from their own, to assign for this purpose, by way of property, or of a usufruct or life ground rent, what is deemed competent, considering the amount of donated property.

**Article 1409:** Universal donations shall not extend to the donor's future property, even if the donor disposes otherwise.

**Article 1410:** The provisions of article 1401 include fiduciary donations or with the burden of restoring to a third party.

**Article 1411:** No one may accept except personally, or through a person having special power for the purpose or general power for the administration of their property, or through their legal representative.

But any ascendant or descendant of the donee may accept for them, without special or general power, provided they are capable of contracting and obliging themselves.

The rules given on the validity of acceptances and repudiations of inheritances and legacies extend to donations.

**Article 1412:** As long as the donation between living persons has not been accepted, and the acceptance notified to the donor, the donor may revoke it at their discretion.

**Article 1413:** Donations with the burden of restoring to a third party become irrevocable by virtue of the fiduciary's acceptance, in accordance with article 1411\.

The beneficiary is not in the case of accepting until the moment of restitution; but they may repudiate before that moment.

**Article 1414:** Once the donation is accepted by the fiduciary, and the acceptance notified to the donor, the two may by common agreement make in the trust such alterations as they wish, substitute one beneficiary for another, and even revoke the trust entirely, without the beneficiary being able to oppose it.

Proceedings to alter the donation in these terms shall be as if it were an entirely new act.

**Article 1415:** The right of transmission established for succession by reason of death in article 957 does not extend to donations between living persons.

**Article 1416:** The rules concerning the interpretation of testamentary assignments, the right of accrual, and substitutions, terms, conditions, and modes relating to them, extend to donations between living persons.

In other respects that do not oppose the provisions of this title, the general rules of contracts shall be followed.

**Article 1417:** The donor of a gratuitous donation enjoys the benefit of competence in actions brought against them by the donee, whether to oblige them to fulfill a promise, or future donation, or demanding delivery of things donated in the present.

**Article 1418:** The universal donee shall have with respect to creditors the same obligations as heirs; but only with respect to debts prior to the donation, or future ones not exceeding a specific sum determined by the donor in the donation deed.

**Article 1419:** The donation of all property or of a share thereof or of its bare property or usufruct does not deprive the donor's creditors of the actions they have against the donor; unless they accept the donee as debtor expressly or in the terms of article 1380, number 1\.

**Article 1420:** In a singular donation, the donee may be burdened with paying the donor's debts, provided a determined sum is expressed up to which this burden extends.

Creditors shall, however, retain their actions against the original debtor, as in the case of the preceding article.

**Article 1421:** The donee's responsibility with respect to the donor's creditors shall not in any case extend except to what at the time of donation the donated things were worth, this value being proven by solemn inventory or other authentic instrument.

The same extends to the donee's responsibility for other burdens imposed on them in the donation.

**Article 1422:** The donee of a gratuitous donation has no warranty action even if the donation began with a promise.

**Article 1423:** Donations with an onerous cause give no warranty action for eviction, except when the donor has knowingly given another's thing.

However, if pecuniary burdens or appreciable in money have been imposed on the donee, they shall always have the right to be reimbursed for what they have invested in covering them, with current interest not appearing to be compensated by natural and civil fruits of the donated things.

The benefit of competence of the donor ceases with respect to this reimbursement.

**Article 1424:** A donation between living persons is not resolvable because after it one or more children have been born to the donor, unless this resolutory condition has been expressed in the public deed of the donation.

**Article 1425:** Donations are rescindible in the case of article 1187\.

**Article 1426:** If the donee is in default of fulfilling what has been imposed on them in the donation, the donor shall have the right either to oblige the donee to fulfill it, or to have the donation rescinded.

In this second case, the donee shall be considered as a possessor in bad faith for the restitution of the donated things and fruits, provided without serious cause they have failed to fulfill the imposed obligation.

The donee shall be reimbursed for what they have invested until then in fulfillment of their obligation, and of which the donor takes advantage.

**Article 1427:** The rescissory action granted by the preceding article shall end in four years from the day the donee was in default of fulfilling the imposed obligation.

**Article 1428:** A donation between living persons may be revoked for ingratitude.

Any offensive act of the donee that would make them unworthy to inherit from the donor is considered an act of ingratitude.

**Article 1429:** In the restitution to which the donee is obliged for cause of ingratitude, they shall be considered as a possessor in bad faith from the perpetration of the offensive act that has given rise to revocation.

**Article 1430:** The revocatory action ends in four years from when the donor had knowledge of the offensive act, and is extinguished by their death, unless it has been judicially attempted during their lifetime, or the offensive act has caused the donor's death, or has been executed after it.

In these cases, the revocatory action shall be transmitted to the heirs.

**Article 1431:** When the donor, having lost their judgment, or by other impediment, is rendered unable to bring the action granted by article 1428, it may be exercised in their name while they live, and within the period indicated in the preceding article, not only by their guardian, but by any of their descendants or ascendants, or their spouse.

**Article 1432:** The resolution, rescission, and revocation mentioned in the preceding articles shall not give action against third-party possessors, nor for the extinction of mortgages, servitudes, or other rights constituted on the donated things, except in the following cases:

1. When in the public deed of the donation (registered in the competent registry, if the quality of the donated things required it), the donee has been prohibited from alienating them, or the condition has been expressed;  
2. When before the alienations or the constitution of the said rights, the interested third parties have been notified that the donor or another person in their name intends to bring the resolutory, rescissory, or revocatory action against the donee;  
3. When the donated property has been alienated, or the said rights constituted, after the action was brought.

The donor who does not use said action against third parties may demand from the donee the price of the alienated things, according to the value they had on the date of alienation.

**Article 1433:** Remuneratory donations are those expressly made in remuneration for specific services, provided these are of those usually paid for.

If it does not appear by private or public deed, as applicable, that the donation has been remuneratory, or if the services are not specified in the deed, the donation shall be understood as gratuitous.

**Article 1434:** Remuneratory donations, insofar as they equal the value of the remunerated services, are not rescindible or revocable, and insofar as they exceed this value, they must be insinuated.

**Article 1435:** The donee who suffers eviction of the thing donated to them in remuneration shall have the right to demand payment for the services the donor intended to remunerate them with it, insofar as they do not appear to have been compensated by the fruits.

**Article 1436:** In other respects, remuneratory donations remain subject to the rules of this title.

---

# BOOK FOUR: OF OBLIGATIONS IN GENERAL AND OF CONTRACTS

## TITLE I: DEFINITIONS

**Article 1437:** Obligations arise either from the real concurrence of the wills of two or more persons, such as contracts or agreements; or from a voluntary act of the person who obliges themselves, such as in the acceptance of an inheritance or legacy and in all quasi-contracts; or as a consequence of an act that has caused injury or damage to another person, such as in crimes and quasi-crimes; or by disposition of law, such as between parents and children subject to parental authority.

**Article 1438:** A contract or agreement is an act by which one party obliges themselves to another to give, do, or not do something. Each party may be one or many persons.

**Article 1439:** A contract is unilateral when one of the parties obliges themselves to another who contracts no obligation; and bilateral when the contracting parties oblige themselves reciprocally.

**Article 1440:** A contract is gratuitous or charitable when it has for its object only the utility of one of the parties, the other suffering the burden; and onerous when it has for its object the utility of both contractors, each burdening themselves for the benefit of the other.

**Article 1441:** An onerous contract is commutative when each of the parties obliges themselves to give or do a thing that is regarded as equivalent to what the other party is to give or do in turn; and if the equivalent consists of an uncertain contingency of gain or loss, it is called aleatory.

**Article 1442:** A contract is principal when it subsists by itself without need of another agreement, and accessory when it has for its object to ensure the fulfillment of a principal obligation, so that it cannot subsist without it.

**Article 1443:** A contract is real when, for it to be perfect, tradition of the thing to which it refers is necessary; it is solemn when it is subject to the observance of certain special formalities, so that without them it produces no civil effect; and it is consensual when it is perfected by consent alone.

**Article 1444:** In each contract, things that are of its essence, things that are of its nature, and purely accidental things are distinguished. Things of the essence of a contract are those without which it either produces no effect, or degenerates into a different contract; things of the nature of a contract are those that, not being essential in it, are understood to belong to it without need of a special clause; and things accidental to a contract are those that neither essentially nor naturally belong to it, and are added to it by means of special clauses.

---

## TITLE II: OF ACTS AND DECLARATIONS OF WILL

**Article 1445:** For a person to oblige themselves to another by an act or declaration of will, it is necessary:

1. That they be legally capable;  
2. That they consent to said act or declaration and their consent does not suffer from any defect;  
3. That it concern a lawful object;  
4. That it have a lawful cause.

The legal capacity of a person consists in being able to oblige themselves by themselves, and without the ministry or authorization of another.

**Article 1446:** Every person is legally capable, except those whom the law declares incapable.

**Article 1447:** The following are absolutely incapable: demented persons, pubescents, and the deaf or deaf-mutes who cannot make themselves clearly understood.

Their acts produce not even natural obligations, and do not admit security.

Also incapable are adult minors and spendthrifts under interdiction to administer their property. But the incapacity of persons referred to in this paragraph is not absolute, and their acts may have value in certain circumstances and under certain respects determined by law.

In addition to these incapacities, there are other particular ones consisting of the prohibition imposed by law on certain persons to perform certain acts.

**Article 1448:** What a person executes in the name of another, being authorized by them or by law to represent them, produces with respect to the represented the same effects as if they had contracted themselves.

**Article 1449:** Anyone may stipulate in favor of a third person, even if they have no right to represent them; but only this third person may demand what is stipulated; and as long as their express or tacit acceptance does not intervene, the contract is revocable by the sole will of the parties who concurred in it.

Tacit acceptance constitutes acts that could only have been executed by virtue of the contract.

**Article 1450:** Whenever one of the contractors undertakes that by a third person, of whom they are not the legal representative, something is to be given, done, or not done, this third person shall not contract any obligation except by virtue of their ratification; and if they do not ratify, the other contractor shall have action for damages against the one who made the promise.

**Article 1451:** The defects from which consent may suffer are error, force, and fraud.

**Article 1452:** Error on a point of law does not vitiate consent.

**Article 1453:** Error of fact vitiates consent when it concerns the species of act or contract executed or celebrated, such as if one of the parties understands a loan for consumption and the other a donation; or on the identity of the specific thing concerned, such as if in the contract of sale the seller understands to sell a certain determined thing, and the buyer understands to buy another.

**Article 1454:** Error of fact also vitiates consent when the substance or essential quality of the object on which the act or contract concerns is different from what is believed; such as if by one of the parties it is supposed that the object is a silver bar, and it is really a mass of some other similar metal.

Error about any other quality of the thing does not vitiate the consent of those contracting, except when that quality is the principal motive of one of them to contract, and this motive has been known to the other party.

**Article 1455:** Error about the person with whom one intends to contract does not vitiate consent, except if the consideration of this person is the principal cause of the contract.

But in this case, the person with whom one has erroneously contracted shall have the right to be indemnified for damages incurred in good faith by the nullity of the contract.

**Article 1456:** Force does not vitiate consent except when it is capable of producing a strong impression on a person of sound mind, considering their age, sex, and condition. Any act that inflicts on a person a just fear of being exposed themselves, their spouse, or any of their ascendants or descendants to irreparable and serious evil is regarded as a force of this kind.

Reverential fear, that is, the mere fear of displeasing persons to whom deference and respect are owed, does not suffice to vitiate consent.

**Article 1457:** For force to vitiate consent, it is not necessary that it be exercised by the one who is benefited by it; it suffices that force has been employed by any person with the object of obtaining consent.

**Article 1458:** Fraud does not vitiate consent except when it is the work of one of the parties, and when moreover it clearly appears that without it they would not have contracted.

In other cases, fraud gives rise only to the action for damages against the person or persons who have fabricated it or who have taken advantage of it; against the former for the total value of the damages, and against the latter up to the amount of the benefit they have derived from the fraud.

**Article 1459:** Fraud is not presumed except in cases specially provided by law. In others, it must be proved.

**Article 1460:** Every declaration of will must have for its object one or more things to be given, done, or not done. The mere use of the thing or its holding may be the object of the declaration.

**Article 1461:** Not only things that exist may be the object of a declaration of will, but those expected to exist; but it is necessary that both be tradable, and that they be determined at least as to their kind.

The quantity may be uncertain provided the act or contract fixes rules or contains data serving to determine it.

If the object is an act, it is necessary that it be physically and morally possible. Physically impossible is that which is contrary to nature, and morally impossible that prohibited by law, or contrary to good morals or public order.

**Article 1462:** There is a unlawful object in everything that contravenes Chilean public law. Thus the promise to submit in Chile to a jurisdiction not recognized by Chilean laws is null for the defect of object.

**Article 1463:** The right to succeed by reason of death to a living person may not be the object of a donation or contract, even if the same person's consent intervenes.

Agreements between the person who owes a legitimate share and the legitary, relating to the same legitimate share or improvements, are subject to the special rules contained in the title On compulsory assignments.

**Article 1464:** There is a unlawful object in the alienation:

1. Of things not in commerce;  
2. Of rights or privileges that cannot be transferred to another person;  
3. Of things attached by judicial decree, unless the judge authorizes it or the creditor consents;  
4. Of species whose property is litigated, without permission of the judge hearing the litigation.

**Article 1465:** The pact not to claim more by reason of an approved account is not valid as to fraud contained in it, if not expressly remitted. Remission of future fraud is not valid.

**Article 1466:** There is likewise a unlawful object in debts contracted in gambling, in the sale of books whose circulation is prohibited by competent authority, of obscene engravings, paintings, and statues, and of publications condemned as abusive of press freedom; and generally in any contract prohibited by law.

**Article 1467:** There can be no obligation without a real and lawful cause; but it is not necessary to express it. Pure liberality or charity is sufficient cause.

Cause is understood to be the motive inducing the act or contract; and unlawful cause is that prohibited by law, or contrary to good morals or public order.

Thus the promise to give something in payment of a non-existent debt lacks cause; and the promise to give something in recompense of a crime or immoral act has a unlawful cause.

**Article 1468:** What has been given or paid for an unlawful object or cause knowingly may not be repeated.

**Article 1469:** Acts or contracts declared invalid by law shall not cease to be so by clauses introduced into them renouncing the action of nullity.

---

## TITLE III: OF CIVIL OBLIGATIONS AND OF MERE NATURAL OBLIGATIONS

**Article 1470:** Obligations are civil or merely natural.

Civil are those giving the right to demand their fulfillment.

Natural are those not conferring the right to demand their fulfillment, but which, once fulfilled, authorize the retention of what has been given or paid by reason of them.

Such are:

1. Those contracted by persons who, having sufficient judgment and discernment, are nevertheless incapable of obliging themselves according to law, such as adult minors;  
2. Civil obligations extinguished by prescription;  
3. Those proceeding from acts lacking the formalities required by law to produce civil effects; such as paying a legacy imposed by a will not executed in due form;  
4. Those not recognized in court for lack of proof.

For restitution not to be demanded by virtue of these four classes of obligations, it is necessary that payment has been made voluntarily by the one who had the free administration of their property.

**Article 1471:** The judicial sentence rejecting the action brought against the naturally obliged person does not extinguish the natural obligation.

**Article 1472:** Sureties, mortgages, pledges, and penal clauses constituted by third parties for the security of these obligations shall be valid.

---

## TITLE IV: OF CONDITIONAL AND MODAL OBLIGATIONS

**Article 1473:** A conditional obligation is one that depends on a condition, that is, on a future event that may or may not occur.

**Article 1474:** A condition is positive or negative.

Positive consists in a thing occurring; negative, in a thing not occurring.

**Article 1475:** The positive condition must be physically and morally possible.

Physically impossible is that which is contrary to the laws of physical nature; and morally impossible that which consists of an act prohibited by law, or is opposed to good morals or public order.

Those conceived in unintelligible terms shall also be regarded as impossible.

**Article 1476:** If the condition is negative of a physically impossible thing, the obligation is pure and simple; if it consists in the creditor abstaining from an immoral or prohibited act, it vitiates the disposition.

**Article 1477:** A condition is called potestative if it depends on the creditor's or debtor's will; casual if it depends on a third party's will or chance; mixed if it partly depends on the creditor's will and partly on a third party's will or chance.

**Article 1478:** Obligations contracted under a potestative condition consisting of the mere will of the person who obliges themselves are null.

If the condition consists of a voluntary act of either party, it shall be valid.

**Article 1479:** A condition is called suspensive if, while not fulfilled, it suspends the acquisition of a right; and resolutory when by its fulfillment a right is extinguished.

**Article 1480:** If the suspensive condition is or becomes impossible, it shall be deemed failed.

The same rule applies to conditions whose meaning and mode of fulfillment are entirely unintelligible.

And conditions inducing illegal or immoral acts.

The resolutory condition that is impossible by its nature, or unintelligible, or inducing an illegal or immoral act, shall be deemed not written.

**Article 1481:** The rule of the preceding article, paragraph 1, applies even to testamentary dispositions. Thus, when the condition is an act depending on the will of the assignee, and on the will of another person, and ceases to be fulfilled by some accident making it impossible, or because the other person whose will is partly required cannot or will not fulfill it, it shall be deemed failed, notwithstanding the assignee has been disposed to fulfill it.

However, if the person who is to provide the assignment uses unlawful means for the condition not to be fulfilled, or for the other person on whose will its fulfillment partly depends not to cooperate in it, it shall be deemed fulfilled.

**Article 1482:** The positive condition is deemed to have failed or the negative to have been fulfilled when it has become certain that the event contemplated in it will not occur, or when the time within which the event should have occurred has expired and has not occurred.

**Article 1483:** The condition must be fulfilled in the manner the parties have probably understood it should be, and the most rational manner of fulfilling it shall be presumed to be the one the parties have understood.

When, for example, the condition consists in paying a sum of money to a person under guardianship or curatorship, the condition shall not be deemed fulfilled if it is delivered to the same person, and they spend it.

**Article 1484:** Conditions must be fulfilled literally, in the agreed form.

**Article 1485:** Fulfillment of the conditional obligation may not be demanded until the condition is totally fulfilled.

Anything paid before the suspensive condition is fulfilled may be repeated as long as it has not been fulfilled.

**Article 1486:** If before fulfillment of the condition the promised thing perishes without the debtor's fault, the obligation is extinguished; and by the debtor's fault, the debtor is obliged to the price and to damages.

If the thing exists at the time the condition is fulfilled, it is owed in the state in which it is found, the creditor taking advantage of increases or improvements the thing has received, without being obliged to pay more for it, and suffering its deterioration or diminution, without any right to a reduction in price; except if the deterioration or diminution proceeds from the debtor's fault, in which case the creditor may demand either rescission of the contract or delivery of the thing, and in addition to one or the other shall have the right to damages.

Anything that destroys the fitness of the thing for the purpose to which according to its nature or according to the agreement it is destined is understood to destroy the thing.

**Article 1487:** Once the resolutory condition is fulfilled, what has been received under such condition must be restored, unless the condition was imposed exclusively in favor of the creditor, in which case the creditor may, if they wish, renounce it; but shall be obliged to declare their determination, if the debtor requires it.

**Article 1488:** Once a resolutory condition is verified, fruits perceived in the intervening time shall not be owed, except if law, the testator, the donor, or the contractors, according to the various cases, have provided otherwise.

**Article 1489:** In bilateral contracts, the resolutory condition of one of the contractors not fulfilling what was agreed is implied.

But in such case, the other contractor may demand at their option either the resolution or the fulfillment of the contract, with damages.

**Article 1490:** If one who owes a movable thing at term, or under suspensive or resolutory condition, alienates it, there shall be no right to claim it against third-party possessors in good faith.

**Article 1491:** If one who owes an immovable under condition alienates it, or encumbers it with mortgage, ground rent, or servitude, the alienation or encumbrance shall not be resolvable except when the condition appeared in the respective title, registered or granted by public deed.

**Article 1492:** The right of the creditor who dies in the interval between the conditional contract and the fulfillment of the condition is transmitted to their heirs; and the same happens with the debtor's obligation.

This rule does not apply to testamentary assignments, nor to donations between living persons.

The creditor may during said interval request the necessary conservatory measures.

**Article 1493:** The provisions of Title IV of Book III on conditional or modal testamentary assignments apply to agreements insofar as they do not conflict with the provisions of the preceding articles.

---

## TITLE V: OF OBLIGATIONS AT TERM

**Article 1494:** A term is the time fixed for the fulfillment of the obligation, and may be express or tacit. It is tacit if indispensable to fulfill it.

The judge may not, except in special cases designated by law, set a term for the fulfillment of an obligation: they may only interpret one conceived in vague or obscure terms, on whose understanding and application the parties disagree.

**Article 1495:** What is paid before the term expires is not subject to restitution.

This rule does not apply to terms that have the value of conditions.

**Article 1496:** Payment of the obligation may not be demanded before the term expires, except:

1. From a debtor who has such status in a concursal liquidation proceeding, or is in notorious insolvency and does not have the status of debtor in a reorganization proceeding;  
2. From a debtor whose securities, by their act or fault, have been extinguished or have considerably decreased in value. But in this case, the debtor may reclaim the benefit of the term by renewing or improving the securities.

**Article 1497:** The debtor may renounce the term, unless the testator has provided or the parties stipulated otherwise, or the anticipation of payment causes the creditor a harm that by the term they manifestly intended to avoid.

In the contract of loan for consumption at interest, the provisions of article 2204 shall be observed.

**Article 1498:** What is said in Title IV of Book III on testamentary assignments to a day applies to agreements.

---

## TITLE VI: OF ALTERNATIVE OBLIGATIONS

**Article 1499:** An alternative obligation is one by which several things are owed, in such a way that performance of one of them exempts from performance of the others.

**Article 1500:** For the debtor to be freed, they must pay or execute in its entirety one of the things they alternatively owe; and they may not oblige the creditor to accept part of one and part of another.

The choice is the debtor's, unless otherwise agreed.

**Article 1501:** If the choice is the debtor's, the creditor may not demand determinedly one of the things owed, except under the alternative in which they are owed.

**Article 1502:** If the choice is the debtor's, it is at their discretion to alienate or destroy any of the things they alternatively owe as long as one of them subsists.

But if the choice is the creditor's, and any of the things alternatively owed perishes by the debtor's fault, the creditor may, at their option, demand the price of this thing and damages, or any of the remaining things.

**Article 1503:** If one of the things alternatively promised could not be the object of the obligation or is destroyed, the alternative obligation of the others subsists; and if only one remains, the debtor is obliged to it.

**Article 1504:** If all the things comprised in the alternative obligation perish without the debtor's fault, the obligation is extinguished.

If with the debtor's fault, they shall be obliged to the price of whichever thing they choose, when the choice is theirs; or to the price of whichever thing the creditor chooses, when the choice is the creditor's.

---

## TITLE VII: OF FACULTATIVE OBLIGATIONS

**Article 1505:** A facultative obligation is one having for its object a determined thing, but granting the debtor the faculty to pay with this thing or with another designated thing.

**Article 1506:** In the facultative obligation, the creditor has no right to demand another thing than that to which the debtor is directly obliged, and if said thing perishes without the debtor's fault and before the debtor has been in default, they have no right to demand anything.

**Article 1507:** In case of doubt as to whether the obligation is alternative or facultative, it shall be considered alternative.

---

## TITLE VIII: OF OBLIGATIONS OF KIND

**Article 1508:** Obligations of kind are those in which an undetermined individual of a determined class or kind is owed.

**Article 1509:** In an obligation of kind, the creditor may not demand determinedly any individual, and the debtor is freed by delivering any individual of the kind, provided it is of at least medium quality.

**Article 1510:** The loss of some things of the kind does not extinguish the obligation, and the creditor may not oppose the debtor alienating or destroying them, as long as others subsist for fulfillment of what they owe.

---

## TITLE IX: OF JOINT AND SEVERAL OBLIGATIONS

**Article 1511:** In general, when the obligation of a divisible thing has been contracted by many persons or in favor of many, each of the debtors, in the former case, is obliged only to their part or share in the debt, and each of the creditors, in the latter, has only the right to demand their part or share in the credit.

But by virtue of agreement, will, or law, the total of the debt may be demanded from each of the debtors or by each of the creditors, and then the obligation is joint and several or in solutum.

Joint and several liability must be expressly declared in all cases where it is not established by law.

**Article 1512:** The thing owed jointly and severally by many or in favor of many must be the same, even if owed in different ways; for example, purely and simply with respect to some, under condition or at term with respect to others.

**Article 1513:** The debtor may make payment to any of the joint creditors they choose, unless they have been sued by one of them, in which case they must make payment to the plaintiff.

The remission of the debt, compensation, novation intervening between the debtor and any of the joint creditors extinguishes the debt with respect to the others, in the same manner as payment would do; provided one of these has not already sued the debtor.

**Article 1514:** The creditor may proceed against all the joint debtors jointly, or against any of them at their option, without the benefit of division being opposable to them by the latter.

**Article 1515:** The claim brought by the creditor against any of the joint debtors does not extinguish the joint obligation of any of them, except in the part satisfied by the defendant.

**Article 1516:** The creditor may expressly or tacitly renounce joint and several liability with respect to one of the joint debtors or with respect to all.

Tacitly in favor of one of them, when they have demanded or recognized payment of their part or share of the debt, expressing it so in the claim or in the receipt, without special reservation of joint and several liability, or without general reservation of their rights.

But this express or tacit renunciation does not extinguish the creditor's joint action against the other debtors, for all the part of the credit not covered by the debtor in whose favor joint and several liability was renounced.

Joint and several liability is renounced with respect to all joint debtors when the creditor consents to the division of the debt.

**Article 1517:** Express or tacit renunciation of joint and several liability of a periodic pension is limited to accrued payments, and extends to future ones only when the creditor expresses it.

**Article 1518:** If the creditor remits the debt to any of the joint debtors, they may not later exercise the action granted by article 1514, except with deduction of the share corresponding to the first in the debt.

**Article 1519:** Novation between the creditor and any of the joint debtors frees the others, unless they accede to the newly constituted obligation.

**Article 1520:** The sued joint debtor may oppose to the claim all exceptions arising from the nature of the obligation, and in addition all their personal exceptions.

But they may not oppose by way of compensation the credit of a co-debtor against the plaintiff, if the co-debtor has not assigned their right.

**Article 1521:** If the thing perishes by the fault or during the default of one of the joint debtors, all of them are jointly and severally obliged to the price, saving the co-debtors' action against the guilty or defaulting debtor. But the action for damages to which fault or default gives rise may only be brought by the creditor against the guilty or defaulting debtor.

**Article 1522:** The joint debtor who has paid the debt, or extinguished it by some of the means equivalent to payment, is subrogated in the creditor's action with all its privileges and securities, but limited with respect to each of the co-debtors to the part or share this co-debtor has in the debt.

If the business for which the joint obligation was contracted concerned only some of the joint debtors, these shall be responsible among themselves according to the parts or shares corresponding to them in the debt, and the other co-debtors shall be considered as sureties.

The part or share of the insolvent co-debtor is distributed among all the others in proportion to their shares, including even those whom the creditor may have exempted from joint and several liability.

**Article 1523:** The heirs of each of the joint debtors are, among themselves, obliged to the total of the debt; but each heir shall be responsible only for that share of the debt corresponding to their hereditary portion.

---

## TITLE X: OF DIVISIBLE AND INDIVISIBLE OBLIGATIONS

**Article 1524:** An obligation is divisible or indivisible according to whether it has or does not have for its object a thing susceptible of division, whether physical, intellectual, or of share.

Thus the obligation to grant a servitude of transit or to have a house built are indivisible; the obligation to pay a sum of money is divisible.

**Article 1525:** An obligation being joint does not give it the character of indivisible.

**Article 1526:** If the obligation is not joint nor indivisible, each of the creditors may only demand their share, and each of the co-debtors is only obliged to pay theirs; and the share of the insolvent debtor shall not burden their co-debtors. Excepted are the following cases:

1. The mortgage or pledge action is directed against that of the co-debtors who possesses, in whole or in part, the mortgaged or pledged thing. The co-debtor who has paid their part of the debt cannot recover the pledge or obtain cancellation of the mortgage, even in part, as long as the total of the debt is not extinguished; and the creditor to whom their part of the credit has been satisfied cannot remit the pledge or cancel the mortgage, even in part, as long as their co-creditors have not been fully satisfied.  
2. If the debt is of a species or certain body, that of the co-debtors who possesses it is obliged to deliver it.  
3. That of the co-debtors by whose act or fault fulfillment of the obligation has become impossible is exclusively and jointly responsible for all damage to the creditor.  
4. When by will or by agreement among the heirs, or by partition of the inheritance, one of the heirs has been obliged to pay the total of a debt, the creditor may proceed either against this heir for the total of the debt, or against each of the heirs for the part corresponding to them proportionally. If it was expressly stipulated with the deceased that payment could not be made in parts, not even by the debtor's heirs, each of these may be obliged to agree with their co-heirs to pay the total of the debt, or to pay it themselves, saving their warranty action. But the creditor's heirs, if they do not bring their action jointly, may not demand payment of the debt except proportionally to their shares.  
5. If a piece of land, or any other indeterminate thing whose division would cause serious damage to the creditor, is owed, each of the co-debtors may be obliged to agree with the others for payment of the entire thing, or to pay it themselves, saving their action for indemnity from the others. But the creditor's heirs may not demand payment of the entire thing except by bringing their action jointly.  
6. When the obligation is alternative, if the choice is the creditors', all must make it jointly; and if the debtors', all of them must make it jointly.

**Article 1527:** Each of those who have jointly contracted an indivisible obligation is obliged to satisfy it in full, even if joint liability has not been stipulated, and each of the creditors of an indivisible obligation has likewise the right to demand the total.

**Article 1528:** Each of the heirs of one who has contracted an indivisible obligation is obliged to satisfy it in full, and each of the creditor's heirs may demand its total execution.

**Article 1529:** Prescription interrupted with respect to one of the debtors of the indivisible obligation is likewise interrupted with respect to the others.

**Article 1530:** One of the debtors of the indivisible obligation when sued may request a period to agree with the other debtors to fulfill it among themselves; unless the obligation is of such a nature that they alone can fulfill it, in which case they may be condemned immediately to total fulfillment, their action against the other debtors for the indemnity owed by them remaining.

**Article 1531:** Fulfillment of the indivisible obligation by any of the obliged persons extinguishes it with respect to all.

**Article 1532:** If there are two or more creditors of the indivisible obligation, none of them may, without the consent of the others, remit the debt or receive the price of the thing owed. If any of the creditors remits the debt or receives the price of the thing, their co-creditors may still demand the thing itself, paying the debtor the part or share of the creditor who has remitted the debt or received the price of the thing.

**Article 1533:** The action for damages resulting from non-fulfillment or delay of the indivisible obligation is divisible: none of the creditors may bring it and none of the debtors is subject to it, except in the part falling to them.

If by the act or fault of one of the debtors of the indivisible obligation fulfillment has become impossible, that one alone shall be responsible for all damages.

**Article 1534:** If of two co-debtors of an act to be performed in common, one is ready to fulfill it, and the other refuses or delays, the latter alone shall be responsible for the damage resulting to the creditor from the non-execution or delay of the act.

---

## TITLE XI: OF OBLIGATIONS WITH PENAL CLAUSE

**Article 1535:** A penal clause is one in which a person, to ensure fulfillment of an obligation, subjects themselves to a penalty consisting of giving or doing something in case of not executing or delaying the principal obligation.

**Article 1536:** Nullity of the principal obligation entails nullity of the penal clause, but nullity of the latter does not entail nullity of the principal obligation.

However, when one person promises for another, imposing on themselves a penalty for the case of the latter not fulfilling what was promised, the penalty shall be valid, even if the principal obligation has no effect for lack of that person's consent.

The same shall happen when one stipulates with another in favor of a third party, and the person with whom one stipulates subjects themselves to a penalty for the case of not fulfilling what is promised.

**Article 1537:** Before the debtor is in default, the creditor may not demand at their option the principal obligation or the penalty, but only the principal obligation; nor, once the debtor is in default, may the creditor demand at the same time fulfillment of the principal obligation and the penalty, but only either of the two at their option; unless it appears that the penalty was stipulated for mere delay, or unless it has been stipulated that by payment of the penalty the principal obligation is not understood to be extinguished.

**Article 1538:** Whether or not a term has been stipulated within which the principal obligation is to be fulfilled, the debtor incurs the penalty only when they have been in default, if the obligation is positive.

If the obligation is negative, the penalty is incurred from the moment the act from which the debtor has obliged themselves to abstain is executed.

**Article 1539:** If the debtor fulfills only a part of the principal obligation and the creditor accepts that part, they shall have the right for the penalty stipulated for lack of fulfillment of the principal obligation to be proportionally reduced.

**Article 1540:** When the obligation contracted with a penal clause is of a divisible thing, the penalty, like the principal obligation, is divided among the debtor's heirs in proportion to their hereditary shares. The heir who contravenes the obligation therefore incurs that part of the penalty corresponding to their hereditary share; and the creditor shall have no action against the co-heirs who have not contravened the obligation.

Excepted is the case where, the penal clause having been made with the express intention that payment could not be partially executed, one of the heirs has prevented total payment: the entire penalty may then be demanded from this heir, or each their respective share, their recourse against the offending heir remaining.

The same shall be observed when the obligation contracted with a penal clause is of an indivisible thing.

**Article 1541:** If an immovable is mortgaged for the penalty, the entire penalty may be pursued in it, saving the recourse of indemnity against whomever it may apply.

**Article 1542:** The penalty may be demanded in all cases where it has been stipulated, without the debtor being able to allege that non-performance of the agreement has caused no harm to the creditor or has produced benefit to them.

**Article 1543:** The penalty and damages may not be demanded at the same time, unless expressly stipulated; but it shall always be at the creditor's option to demand indemnity or the penalty.

**Article 1544:** When by the principal agreement one of the parties obliged themselves to pay a determined amount, as equivalent to what is to be provided by the other party, and the penalty likewise consists in payment of a determined amount, it may be demanded that all exceeding double the former be deducted from the latter, including the former in it.

The preceding provision does not apply to loan for consumption nor to obligations of inappreciable or indeterminate value.

In the former, the penalty may be reduced insofar as it exceeds the maximum interest permitted to be stipulated.

In the latter, it is left to the judge's prudence to moderate it, when considering the circumstances it appears excessive.

---

## TITLE XII: OF THE EFFECT OF OBLIGATIONS

**Article 1545:** Every legally celebrated contract is a law for the contractors, and may not be invalidated except by their mutual consent or by legal causes.

**Article 1546:** Contracts must be executed in good faith, and consequently bind not only to what is expressed in them, but to all things that emanate precisely from the nature of the obligation, or that by law or custom belong to it.

**Article 1547:** The debtor is responsible only for gross fault in contracts that by their nature are only useful to the creditor; is responsible for slight fault in contracts made for reciprocal benefit of the parties; and for the slightest fault in contracts in which the debtor is the only one deriving benefit.

The debtor is not responsible for fortuitous event, unless they have been in default (the fortuitous event being of those that would not have harmed the thing owed if it had been delivered to the creditor), or the fortuitous event has arisen by their fault.

Proof of diligence or care falls on the one who should have employed it; proof of the fortuitous event on the one who alleges it.

All this, however, is understood without prejudice to the special provisions of the laws, and to the express stipulations of the parties.

**Article 1548:** The obligation to give includes delivery of the thing; and if this is a species or certain body, it also includes preserving it until delivery, under penalty of paying damages to the creditor who is not in default of receiving.

**Article 1549:** The obligation to preserve the thing requires that due care be employed in its custody.

**Article 1550:** The risk of the certain body whose delivery is owed is always at the creditor's charge; except if the debtor is in default of effecting it, or has undertaken to deliver the same thing to two or more persons by distinct obligations; in either of these cases, the risk of the thing shall be at the debtor's charge until delivery.

**Article 1551:** The debtor is in default:

1. When they have not fulfilled the obligation within the stipulated term, except if the law in special cases requires the debtor to be requested to be in default;  
2. When the thing could only be given or executed within a certain space of time, and the debtor has let it pass without giving or executing it;  
3. In other cases, when the debtor has been judicially summoned by the creditor.

**Article 1552:** In bilateral contracts, neither of the contractors is in default by failing to fulfill what was agreed, as long as the other does not fulfill on their part, or does not agree to fulfill it in due form and time.

**Article 1553:** If the obligation is to do and the debtor is in default, the creditor may demand, together with indemnity for the default, any of these three things at their choice:

1. That the debtor be compelled to execute the agreed act;  
2. That they be authorized themselves to have it executed by a third party at the debtor's expense;  
3. That the debtor indemnify them for damages resulting from breach of the contract.

**Article 1554:** The promise to celebrate a contract produces no obligation; except the following circumstances concur:

1. That the promise is in writing;  
2. That the promised contract is not one of those the laws declare ineffective;  
3. That the promise contains a term or condition fixing the time for celebration of the contract;  
4. That it specifies the promised contract in such a way that only the tradition of the thing, or the formalities prescribed by law, are lacking for it to be perfect.

If these circumstances concur, the provisions of the preceding article shall apply.

**Article 1555:** Every obligation not to do a thing resolves itself into indemnifying damages, if the debtor contravenes and cannot undo what was done.

If the thing done can be destroyed, and its destruction is necessary for the object intended at the time of celebrating the contract, the debtor shall be obliged to it, or the creditor authorized to carry it out at the debtor's expense.

If said object can be fully obtained by other means, the debtor who agrees to provide it shall be heard.

The creditor shall in all cases remain indemnified.

**Article 1556:** Indemnity for damages includes actual loss and lost profit, whether they arise from non-fulfillment of the obligation, or from having been imperfectly fulfilled, or from having delayed fulfillment.

Excepted are cases where the law expressly limits it to actual loss.

**Article 1557:** Indemnity for damages is owed from the time the debtor has been in default, or if the obligation is not to do, from the moment of contravention.

**Article 1558:** If fraud cannot be imputed to the debtor, they are only responsible for damages foreseen or that could have been foreseen at the time of the contract; but if there is fraud, they are responsible for all damages that were an immediate or direct consequence of non-fulfillment of the obligation or of delay in its fulfillment.

Default produced by force majeure or fortuitous event does not give rise to damages.

Stipulations of the contractors may modify these rules.

**Article 1559:** If the obligation is to pay a sum of money, indemnity for default is subject to the following rules:

1. Conventional interest continues to be owed, if a higher interest than the legal one has been agreed, or legal interest begins to be owed in the contrary case; the special provisions authorizing the collection of current interest in certain cases remaining in force.  
2. The creditor does not need to justify damages when only collecting interest; the fact of delay is sufficient.  
3. Overdue interest does not produce interest.  
4. The preceding rule applies to all kinds of rents, rents, and periodic pensions.

---

## TITLE XIII: OF THE INTERPRETATION OF CONTRACTS

**Article 1560:** Once the intention of the contractors is clearly known, it must be adhered to more than to the literal meaning of the words.

**Article 1561:** However general the terms of a contract may be, they shall only apply to the matter on which it was contracted.

**Article 1562:** The sense in which a clause can produce some effect shall be preferred to that in which it is incapable of producing any effect.

**Article 1563:** In those cases where contrary will does not appear, the interpretation that best suits the nature of the contract shall be adhered to.

Clauses of common use are presumed even if not expressed.

**Article 1564:** Clauses of a contract shall be interpreted by one another, giving each the sense that best suits the contract as a whole.

They may also be interpreted by those of another contract between the same parties and on the same matter.

Or by the practical application the parties have made of them, or one of the parties with the other's approval.

**Article 1565:** When a case has been expressed in a contract to explain the obligation, it shall not be understood by that alone that the agreement was intended to be restricted to that case, excluding others to which it naturally extends.

**Article 1566:** If none of the preceding rules of interpretation can be applied, ambiguous clauses shall be interpreted in favor of the debtor.

But ambiguous clauses that have been extended or dictated by one of the parties, whether creditor or debtor, shall be interpreted against them, whenever the ambiguity arises from the lack of an explanation that should have been given by them.

---

## TITLE XIV: OF THE MODES OF EXTINGUISHING OBLIGATIONS, AND FIRSTLY OF SOLUTION OR EFFECTIVE PAYMENT

**Article 1567:** Every obligation may be extinguished by an agreement in which the interested parties, being capable of freely disposing of their property, consent to consider it null.

Obligations are also extinguished in whole or in part:

1. By solution or effective payment;  
2. By novation;  
3. By transaction;  
4. By remission;  
5. By compensation;  
6. By confusion;  
7. By loss of the thing owed;  
8. By declaration of nullity or rescission;  
9. By the event of the resolutory condition;  
10. By prescription.

Of transaction and prescription, treatment will be given at the end of this Book; of the resolutory condition has been treated in the title On conditional obligations.

### § 1\. Of Effective Payment in General

**Article 1568:** Effective payment is the performance of what is owed.

**Article 1569:** Payment shall be made in all respects in accordance with the tenor of the obligation; without prejudice to what the laws may provide in special cases.

The creditor may not be obliged to receive something other than what is owed to them, even under pretext of it being of equal or greater value.

**Article 1570:** In periodic payments, the receipt of three determined and consecutive periods shall create a presumption of payment of the previous periods, provided they should have been made between the same creditor and debtor.

**Article 1571:** Expenses occasioned by payment shall be for the debtor's account; without prejudice to what is stipulated and what the judge orders regarding court costs.

### § 2\. By Whom Payment May Be Made

**Article 1572:** Anyone may pay on behalf of the debtor, even without their knowledge or against their will, and even despite the creditor.

But if the obligation is to do, and if for the work in question the aptitude or talent of the debtor has been taken into consideration, the work may not be executed by another person against the creditor's will.

**Article 1573:** One who pays without the debtor's knowledge shall have no action except for the debtor to reimburse them for what was paid; and shall not be understood to be subrogated by law in the place and rights of the creditor, nor may they compel the creditor to subrogate them.

**Article 1574:** One who pays against the debtor's will has no right for the debtor to reimburse them for what was paid; unless the creditor voluntarily assigns their action.

**Article 1575:** Payment in which property is to be transferred is not valid, except insofar as the payer is the owner of the thing paid, or pays with the owner's consent.

Payment in which property is to be transferred is also not valid, except insofar as the payer has the faculty to alienate.

However, when the thing paid is fungible and the creditor has consumed it in good faith, payment is validated, even if made by the one who was not owner, or who had no faculty to alienate.

### § 3\. To Whom Payment Must Be Made

**Article 1576:** For payment to be valid, it must be made either to the creditor himself (under whose name are understood all who have succeeded them in the credit, even by singular title), or to the person whom the law or the judge authorizes to receive for them, or to the person delegated by the creditor for collection.

Payment made in good faith to the person who was then in possession of the credit is valid, even if it later appears that the credit did not belong to them.

**Article 1577:** Payment made to a person other than those expressed in the preceding article is valid, if the creditor ratifies it expressly or tacitly, being able to legitimately do so; or if the one who received payment succeeds in the credit, as heir of the creditor, or under any other title.

When payment made to an incompetent person is ratified by the creditor, it shall be regarded as valid from the beginning.

**Article 1578:** Payment made to the creditor is null in the following cases:

1. If the creditor does not have the administration of their property; except insofar as it is proved that the thing paid has been used for the creditor's benefit, and insofar as this benefit is justified in accordance with article 1688;  
2. If by the judge the debt has been attached or its payment ordered retained;  
3. If payment is made to the insolvent debtor in fraud of creditors in whose favor a concurrence has been opened.

**Article 1579:** Guardians and curators legitimately receive for their respective represented persons; executors having this special charge or the holding of the deceased's property; husbands for their wives insofar as they have the administration of their property; fathers or mothers exercising parental authority for their children; fiscal collectors or of communities or public establishments for the Treasury or respective communities or establishments; and other persons authorized by special law or judicial decree.

**Article 1580:** Delegation to receive payment may be conferred by general power for the free administration of all the creditor's affairs, or by special power for the free administration of the business or businesses in which the payment is included, or by a simple mandate communicated to the debtor.

**Article 1581:** Anyone to whom the creditor entrusts this charge may be delegated to collect and validly receive payment, even if at the time of conferral they do not have the administration of their property nor are capable of having it.

**Article 1582:** Power conferred by the creditor on a person to sue the debtor in court does not by itself authorize them to receive payment of the debt.

**Article 1583:** The faculty to receive for the creditor is not transmitted to the heirs or representatives of the person delegated by them for this purpose, unless the creditor has so expressed.

**Article 1584:** The person designated by both contractors to receive does not lose this faculty by the sole will of the creditor; who, however, may be authorized by the judge to revoke this charge, in all cases where the debtor has no interest in opposing it.

**Article 1585:** If it has been stipulated that payment be made to the creditor himself, or to a third party, payment made to either is equally valid. And the creditor may not prohibit payment to the third party, unless before the prohibition they have sued the debtor, or prove just cause for it.

**Article 1586:** The person delegated to receive becomes incompetent by dementia or interdiction, by having made assignment of property or execution having been levied on all of them; and in general by all causes that cause a mandate to expire.

### § 4\. Where Payment Must Be Made

**Article 1587:** Payment must be made in the place designated by the agreement.

**Article 1588:** If no place for payment has been stipulated and it concerns a certain body, payment shall be made in the place where said body existed at the time the obligation was constituted.

But if it concerns another thing, payment shall be made at the debtor's domicile.

**Article 1589:** If the creditor or debtor has changed domicile between the celebration of the contract and payment, it shall always be made in the place where without that change it would correspond, unless the parties agree otherwise.

### § 5\. How Payment Must Be Made

**Article 1590:** If the debt is of a certain body, the creditor must receive it in the state in which it is found; unless it has deteriorated and the deteriorations proceed from the act or fault of the debtor, or of persons for whom they are responsible; or unless the deteriorations have arisen after the debtor has been in default, and do not proceed from a fortuitous event to which the thing would have been equally exposed in the creditor's power.

In either of these two suppositions, rescission of the contract and damages may be demanded by the creditor; but if the creditor prefers to take the species, or if the deterioration does not appear important, only damages shall be granted.

If the deterioration occurred before the debtor was in default, but not by their act or fault, but of another person for whom they are not responsible, payment of the thing in the state in which it is found is valid; but the creditor may demand that the action their debtor has against the third party, author of the damage, be assigned to them.

**Article 1591:** The debtor may not oblige the creditor to receive in parts what is owed to them, except in the case of contrary agreement; and without prejudice to what the laws may provide in special cases.

Total payment of the debt includes payment of interest and indemnities owed.

**Article 1592:** If there is controversy over the amount of the debt, or its accessories, the judge may order, while the question is decided, payment of the undisputed amount.

**Article 1593:** If the obligation is to pay in installments, payment shall be understood to be divided into equal parts; unless in the contract the part or share to be paid at each installment has been determined.

**Article 1594:** When several different debts concur between the same creditor and debtor, each of them may be satisfied separately; and consequently the debtor of many years of a pension, rent, or ground rent may oblige the creditor to receive payment of one year, even if they do not pay the others at the same time.

### § 6\. Of Imputation of Payment

**Article 1595:** If principal and interest are owed, payment shall be imputed first to interest, unless the creditor expressly consents to its imputation to principal.

If the creditor gives receipt for the principal without mentioning interest, the latter is presumed paid.

**Article 1596:** If there are different debts, the debtor may impute payment to whichever they choose; but without the creditor's consent, they may not prefer the debt not accrued to the one that is accrued; and if the debtor does not impute payment to any in particular, the creditor may make the imputation in the receipt; and if the debtor accepts it, they may not later claim.

**Article 1597:** If neither party has imputed payment, the debt that at the time of payment was accrued to the one not accrued shall be preferred; and if there is no difference in this respect, the debt the debtor chooses.

### § 7\. Of Payment by Consignation

**Article 1598:** For payment to be valid, it is not necessary that it be made with the creditor's consent; payment is valid even against the creditor's will, by means of consignation.

**Article 1599:** Consignation is the deposit of the thing owed, made by virtue of the creditor's repugnance or non-appearance to receive it, or of uncertainty about the person of the creditor, and with necessary formalities, in the hands of a third person.
