**Article 1600:** Consignation must be preceded by offer, and for the offer to be valid, it must have the following circumstances:

1. That it be made by a person capable of paying;  
2. That it be made to the creditor, being capable of receiving payment, or to their legal representative;  
3. That if the obligation is at term or under suspensive condition, the term has expired or the condition has been fulfilled. However, if the obligation is at term, the offer may also be made on the last two business days of the term;  
4. That the payment be offered to be executed in the due place;  
5. That the offer be made by a competent notary or receiver, without prior court order. For this purpose, the debtor shall place in their hands a minute of what is owed, with accrued interest, if any, and other liquid charges, including in it an individual description of the thing offered. For the validity of the offer, material presentation of the thing offered shall not be necessary. In communes where there is no notary, the Civil Registry Officer of the place where payment is to be made may act;  
6. That the notary, receiver, or Civil Registry Officer, as applicable, draw up a record of the offer, copying the aforementioned minute in it;  
7. That the record of the offer express the creditor's or their representative's response, and whether the one or the other has signed, refused to sign, or declared not knowing or not being able to sign.

However, if the creditor judicially demands fulfillment of the obligation or brings any other action that can be defeated by payment of the debt, it shall suffice that the thing owed with accrued interest, if any, and other liquid charges, be consigned to the order of the court hearing the proceeding in some of the forms indicated in article 1601, without need for prior offer. In this case, the sufficiency of payment shall be determined by said court in the same proceeding.

**Article 1601:** If the creditor or their representative refuses to receive the thing offered, the debtor may consign it in the bank account of the competent court, or in the communal treasury, or in a bank or office of the National Savings Bank, the Agricultural Credit Bank, auction, or general deposit warehouse of the place where payment is to be made, according to the nature of the thing offered.

Consignation may also be made in the power of a depositary appointed by the competent judge.

No prior judicial decree shall be necessary to make the offer or to make the consignation.

In payment by consignation, no judicial management or recourse of the creditor tending to obstruct the offer or consignation shall be admitted. Consequently, no opposition or request of the creditor shall be processed.

When it concerns periodic payment of sums of money arising from the same obligation, installments following the one consigned shall be deposited in the court's bank account without need for new offers.

The judge of Major Letters of the place where payment is to be made shall be the competent judge for the purposes of this article.

**Article 1602:** If the creditor or their representative has no domicile in the place where payment is to be made, or is not found, or there is uncertainty about the person of the creditor, the provisions of numbers 1, 3, 4, 5, and 6 of article 1600 shall apply.

The offer shall in this case be made to the respective communal treasurer, who shall limit themselves to taking knowledge of it, and the debtor may proceed to consignation in the manner provided in the preceding article.

**Article 1603:** Once consignation is made, the debtor shall request the judge indicated in the final paragraph of article 1601 to order it brought to the creditor's knowledge, with intimation to receive the consigned thing.

The sufficiency of payment by consignation shall be determined in the proceeding corresponding brought by the debtor or by the creditor before the court competent according to general rules.

However, if the creditor does not prove, within thirty business days from the date they have been notified of the consignation, the circumstance of there being a proceeding in which the sufficiency of payment must be determined, the judge who ordered said notification shall declare it sufficient, at the debtor's request, and order the securities to be lifted, without further procedure. Resolutions issued by virtue of this paragraph shall be appealable only in the devolutive effect.

Notwithstanding, the judge may extend the period established in the preceding paragraph by up to thirty days if for reasons beyond the creditor's will it has not been possible to notify the debtor.

A proceeding shall be understood to exist from the moment the claim is notified.

**Article 1604:** Expenses of any valid offer and consignation shall be at the creditor's charge.

**Article 1605:** The effect of sufficient consignation is to extinguish the obligation, to cause interest to cease, and to exempt the debtor from risk of the thing, all from the day of consignation.

However, if it concerns an obligation at term or under condition, once the consignation is accepted by the creditor, or payment declared sufficient by final resolution, the obligation shall be considered fulfilled in due time provided the offer was made no later than the business day following the maturity of the obligation; but the debtor shall remain obliged in all cases to pay interest owed and care for the thing until consignation.

**Article 1606:** As long as the consignation has not been accepted by the creditor, or payment declared sufficient by a judgment having the force of res judicata, the debtor may withdraw the consignation; and once withdrawn, it shall be regarded as of no value or effect with respect to the consignor and their co-debtors and sureties.

**Article 1607:** When the obligation has been irrevocably extinguished, the consignation may still be withdrawn if the creditor consents. But in this case, the obligation shall be regarded as entirely new; co-debtors and sureties shall remain exempt from it; and the creditor shall not retain the privileges or mortgages of their original credit. If by will of the parties the previous mortgages are renewed, they shall be registered anew and their date shall be the day of the new registration.

### § 8\. Of Payment with Subrogation

**Article 1608:** Subrogation is the transmission of the creditor's rights to a third party who pays them.

**Article 1609:** A third party is subrogated in the creditor's rights either by virtue of law or by virtue of an agreement of the creditor.

**Article 1610:** Subrogation is effected by the ministry of law and even against the creditor's will, in all cases indicated by the laws, and especially in favor of:

1. The creditor who pays another creditor of better right by reason of a privilege or mortgage;  
2. One who, having purchased an immovable, is obliged to pay the creditors to whom the immovable is mortgaged;  
3. One who pays a debt to which they are jointly or subsidiarily obliged;  
4. The beneficiary heir who pays the debts of the inheritance with their own money;  
5. One who pays another's debt, the debtor consenting expressly or tacitly;  
6. One who has lent money to the debtor for payment; appearing so in the public deed of the loan, and also appearing in the public deed of payment that the debt was satisfied with the same money.

**Article 1611:** Subrogation is effected by virtue of an agreement of the creditor; when the creditor, receiving payment of the debt from a third party, voluntarily subrogates them in all the rights and actions corresponding to them as such creditor: subrogation in this case is subject to the rule of assignment of rights, and must be made in the receipt.

**Article 1612:** Subrogation, both legal and conventional, transfers to the new creditor all the rights, actions, privileges, pledges, and mortgages of the old one, both against the principal debtor and against any third parties jointly or subsidiarily obliged to the debt.

If the creditor has only been partially paid, they may exercise their rights, relative to what remains owed to them, with preference over the one who has only paid a part of the credit.

**Article 1613:** If several persons have lent money to the debtor for payment of a debt, there shall be no preference among them, whatever the dates of the different loans or subrogations.

### § 9\. Of Payment by Assignment of Property or by Executive Action of the Creditor or Creditors

**Article 1614:** Assignment of property is the voluntary surrender the debtor makes of all theirs to their creditor or creditors, when, as a consequence of unavoidable accidents, they are not in a state to pay their debts.

**Article 1615:** This assignment of property shall be admitted by the judge with knowledge of the case, and the debtor may request it notwithstanding any stipulation to the contrary.

**Article 1616:** To obtain the assignment, it is incumbent on the debtor to prove their inculpability in the bad state of their affairs, whenever any of the creditors requires it.

**Article 1617:** Creditors shall be obliged to accept the assignment, except in the following cases:

1. If the debtor has alienated, pledged, or mortgaged, as their own, another's property knowingly;  
2. If they have been convicted of theft or robbery, forgery, or any of the crimes indicated in Paragraph 7 of Title IX of Book Two of the Criminal Code;  
3. If they have obtained remissions or extensions from their creditors;  
4. If they have wasted their property;  
5. If they have not made a circumstantial and truthful exposition of the state of their affairs, or have used any other fraudulent means to harm their creditors.

**Article 1618:** The assignment shall include all the debtor's property, rights, and actions, except non-attachable ones.

The following are not attachable:

1. Two-thirds of the salary of public service employees, provided they do not exceed ninety centavos of the escudo; if they exceed, two-thirds of this sum shall not be attachable, nor half of the excess. The same rule applies to military pensions, to all remuneratory pensions of the State, and to compulsory alimentary pensions;  
2. The debtor's bed, their spouse's, those of children living with them and at their expense, and clothing necessary for the protection of all these persons;  
3. Books relating to the debtor's profession up to the value of twenty centavos of the escudo and at the debtor's choice;  
4. Machines and instruments used by the debtor for teaching any science or art up to said value and subject to the same choice;  
5. Uniforms and equipment of military personnel, according to their arm and rank;  
6. Utensils of the debtor artisan or field worker, necessary for their individual work;  
7. Articles of food and fuel in the debtor's possession, up to the amount necessary for the family's consumption during one month;  
8. The property of objects the debtor possesses in trust;  
9. Rights whose exercise is entirely personal, such as those of use and habitation;  
10. Real property donated or bequeathed with the expression of non-attachable, provided their value has been recorded at the time of delivery by judicially approved appraisal; but they may be attached for the additional value subsequently acquired.

**Article 1619:** Assignment of property produces the following effects:

1. The debtor is freed from all personal restraint;  
2. Debts are extinguished up to the amount satisfied with the assigned property;  
3. If the assigned property has not sufficed for the complete payment of debts, and the debtor subsequently acquires other property, they are obliged to complete payment with these.

Assignment does not transfer the property of the debtor's property to the creditors, but only the faculty to dispose of them and their fruits until paid from their credits.

**Article 1620:** The debtor may repent of the assignment before sale of the property or any part of them, and recover those that exist, by paying their creditors.

**Article 1621:** Once assignment of property is made, creditors may leave the administration of it to the debtor, and make with them the arrangements they deem convenient, provided the majority of concurring creditors consents.

**Article 1622:** The agreement of the majority obtained in the form prescribed by the Code of Procedure shall be binding on all creditors duly summoned.

But privileged, pledge, or mortgage creditors shall not be prejudiced by the majority's agreement, if they have abstained from voting.

**Article 1623:** Assignment of property does not benefit joint or subsidiary co-debtors, nor the one who accepted the debtor's inheritance without benefit of inventory.

**Article 1624:** The provisions regarding assignment in articles 1618 and following apply to attachment of property by executive action of the creditor or creditors; but regarding exemption from personal restraint, the provisions of the Code of Procedure shall apply.

### § 10\. Of Payment with Benefit of Competence

**Article 1625:** Benefit of competence is that granted to certain debtors not to be obliged to pay more than they can reasonably afford, leaving them consequently what is indispensable for a modest subsistence, according to their class and circumstances, and with the obligation of restitution when they improve their fortune.

**Article 1626:** The creditor is obliged to grant this benefit:

1. To their descendants or ascendants; these not having inflicted on the creditor any offense classified among the causes of disinheritance;  
2. To their spouse; not being judicially separated by their fault;  
3. To their siblings; provided they have not been guilty toward the creditor of an offense equally grave as those indicated as causes of disinheritance with respect to descendants or ascendants;  
4. To their associates in the same case; but only in reciprocal actions arising from the society contract;  
5. To the donor; but only insofar as it concerns making them fulfill the promised donation;  
6. To the debtor in good faith who made assignment of property and is pursued in those subsequently acquired for complete payment of debts prior to the assignment; but only creditors in whose favor it was made owe them this benefit.

**Article 1627:** Maintenance and benefit of competence may not be requested at the same time. The debtor shall choose.

---

## TITLE XV: OF NOVATION

**Article 1628:** Novation is the substitution of a new obligation for a previous one, which is thereby extinguished.

**Article 1629:** The attorney or agent may not novate unless they have special faculty for it, or have the free administration of the principal's affairs or of the business to which the debt belongs.

**Article 1630:** For novation to be valid, it is necessary that both the primitive obligation and the novation contract be valid, at least naturally.

**Article 1631:** Novation may be effected in three ways:

1. By substituting a new obligation for another, without new creditor or debtor intervening;  
2. By the debtor contracting a new obligation with respect to a third party, and consequently the first creditor declaring them freed from the primitive obligation;  
3. By substituting a new debtor for the old one, who consequently is freed.

This third kind of novation may be effected without the consent of the first debtor. When effected with their consent, the second debtor is called the delegate of the first.

**Article 1632:** If the debtor does no more than depute a person to pay for them, or the creditor a person to receive for them, there is no novation.

Nor is there when a third party is subrogated in the creditor's rights.

**Article 1633:** If the old obligation is pure and the new one depends on a suspensive condition, or if, on the contrary, the old one depends on a suspensive condition and the new one is pure, there is no novation while the condition is pending; and if the condition fails, or if before its fulfillment the old obligation is extinguished, there shall be no novation.

However, if the parties, upon celebrating the second contract, agree that the first be immediately abolished, without awaiting fulfillment of the pending condition, the will of the parties shall be followed.

**Article 1634:** For there to be novation, it is necessary that the parties declare it, or that it appears indubitably that their intention has been to novate, because the new obligation involves extinction of the old one.

If the intention to novate does not appear, the two obligations shall be regarded as coexisting, and the primitive obligation shall be valid in everything in which the later one does not oppose it, the privileges and securities of the first subsisting in that part.

**Article 1635:** Substitution of a new debtor for another does not produce novation, if the creditor does not express their will to free the primitive debtor. In the absence of this expression, the third party shall be understood to be merely deputed by the debtor to make payment, or that the third party obliges themselves with them jointly or subsidiarily, as appears to be deduced from the tenor or spirit of the act.

**Article 1636:** If the delegate is substituted against their will for the delegant, there is no novation, but only assignment of actions of the delegant to their creditor, and the effects of this act are subject to the rules of assignment of actions.

**Article 1637:** The creditor who has freed the primitive debtor shall have no later action against them, even if the new debtor falls into insolvency; unless in the novation contract this case has been expressly reserved, or the insolvency was prior and public or known to the primitive debtor.

**Article 1638:** One who, delegated by someone whom they believed to be their debtor and was not, promises the latter's creditor to pay to free themselves from the false debt, is obliged to fulfill their promise; but their right against the delegant for them to pay for them or reimburse what was paid shall remain.

**Article 1639:** One who was delegated by someone who believed themselves to be a debtor and was not, is not obliged to the creditor, and if they pay in the belief that the debt was true, they are for the delegant in the same case as if the debt had been true, the delegant's right to restitution of what was unduly paid remaining.

**Article 1640:** In whatever way novation is made, interest on the first debt is extinguished by it, unless otherwise expressed.

**Article 1641:** Whether novation is operated by substitution of a new debtor or without it, the privileges of the first debt are extinguished by novation.

**Article 1642:** Even if novation is operated without substitution of a new debtor, pledges and mortgages of the primitive obligation do not pass to the later obligation, unless the creditor and debtor expressly agree to the reservation.

But the reservation of pledges and mortgages of the primitive obligation is not valid when the pledged or mortgaged things belong to third parties who do not expressly accede to the second obligation.

Nor is the reservation valid in what the second obligation has more than the first. If, for example, the first debt did not produce interest, and the second does, the mortgage of the first shall not extend to interest.

**Article 1643:** If novation is operated by substitution of a new debtor, the reservation cannot have effect on the new debtor's property, not even with their consent.

And if novation is operated between the creditor and one of their joint debtors, the reservation cannot have effect except relatively to this one. Pledges and mortgages constituted by their joint co-debtors are extinguished, despite any contrary stipulation; except that they expressly accede to the second obligation.

**Article 1644:** In the cases and amount in which the reservation cannot have effect, pledges and mortgages may be renewed; but with the same formalities as if constituted for the first time, and their date shall be that corresponding to the renewal.

**Article 1645:** Novation frees joint or subsidiary co-debtors who have not acceded to it.

**Article 1646:** When the second obligation simply consists in adding or removing a species, kind, or quantity to the first, subsidiary and joint co-debtors may be obliged up to the amount in which both obligations agree.

**Article 1647:** If the new obligation is limited to imposing a penalty in case the first is not fulfilled, and the first obligation and the penalty are jointly demandable, privileges, sureties, pledges, and mortgages shall subsist up to the amount of the principal debt without the penalty. But if in the case of infringement only the penalty is demandable, novation shall be understood from the time the creditor demands only the penalty, and privileges, pledges, and mortgages of the primitive obligation shall be extinguished by the same fact, and those who jointly or subsidiarily acceded to the primitive obligation and not to the penal stipulation shall be exempted.

**Article 1648:** Mere change of place for payment shall leave privileges, pledges, and mortgages of the obligation subsisting, and the responsibility of joint and subsidiary co-debtors, but without new encumbrance.

**Article 1649:** Mere extension of the term of a debt does not constitute novation; but puts an end to the responsibility of sureties and extinguishes pledges and mortgages constituted over property other than the debtor's; unless the sureties or the owners of the pledged or mortgaged things expressly accede to the extension.

**Article 1650:** Mere reduction of the term also does not constitute novation; but joint or subsidiary co-debtors may not be sued until the term originally stipulated expires.

**Article 1651:** If the creditor has consented to the new obligation under condition that joint or subsidiary co-debtors accede to it, and if they do not accede, the novation shall be deemed not made.

---

## TITLE XVI: OF REMISSION

**Article 1652:** Remission or remission of a debt has no value, except insofar as the creditor is competent to dispose of the thing that is its object.

**Article 1653:** Remission proceeding from mere liberality is entirely subject to the rules of donation between living persons; and needs insinuation in cases where donation between living persons needs it.

**Article 1654:** There is tacit remission when the creditor voluntarily delivers to the debtor the title of the obligation, or destroys or cancels it, with the intention of extinguishing the debt. The creditor is admitted to prove that the delivery, destruction, or cancellation of the title was not voluntary or was not made with the intention of remitting the debt. But in the absence of this proof, it shall be understood that there was intention to remit it.

Remission of the pledge or mortgage does not suffice for remission of the debt to be presumed.

---

## TITLE XVII: OF COMPENSATION

**Article 1655:** When two persons are debtors to each other, a compensation occurs between them that extinguishes both debts, in the manner and in the cases to be explained.

**Article 1656:** Compensation operates by the sole ministry of law and even without the knowledge of the debtors; and both debts are reciprocally extinguished up to the concurrence of their values, from the moment one and the other have the following qualities:

1. That both be of money or of fungible or indeterminate things of the same kind and quality;  
2. That both debts be liquid;  
3. That both be currently demandable.

Extensions granted to the debtor prevent compensation; but this provision does not apply to the grace period granted by a creditor to their debtor.

**Article 1657:** For compensation to take place, it is necessary that the two parties be reciprocally debtors.

Thus the principal debtor may not oppose to their creditor by way of compensation what the creditor owes to the surety.

Nor when the debtor of a ward is required by the guardian or curator may they oppose by way of compensation what the guardian or curator owes to them.

Nor when one of several joint debtors is required may they compensate their debt with the credits of their co-debtors against the same creditor, except if the latter have assigned them to them.

**Article 1658:** The agent may oppose to the principal's creditor not only the principal's credits, but their own credits against the same creditor, giving security that the principal will ratify the compensation. But they may not compensate with what the same agent owes to a third party what the latter owes to the principal, except with the principal's will.

**Article 1659:** The debtor who accepts without reservation the assignment the creditor has made of their rights to a third party may not oppose in compensation to the assignee credits that before acceptance they could have opposed to the assignor.

If the assignment has not been accepted, the debtor may oppose to the assignee all credits acquired against the assignor before notification of the assignment, even if they did not become demandable until after notification.

**Article 1660:** Notwithstanding compensation being effected by the ministry of law, the debtor who does not allege it, ignoring a credit they can oppose to the debt, shall retain along with the credit itself the sureties, privileges, pledges, and mortgages constituted for their security.

**Article 1661:** Compensation cannot take place to the prejudice of third parties' rights.

Thus, once a credit is attached, the debtor may not compensate it, to the prejudice of the attaching party, for any credit acquired after the attachment.

**Article 1662:** Compensation may not be opposed to the demand for restitution of a thing of which its owner has been unjustly dispossessed, nor to the demand for restitution of a deposit or loan for use, even if, the thing being lost, only the obligation to pay it in money remains.

Nor may compensation be opposed to the demand for indemnity for an act of violence or fraud, nor to the demand for non-attachable maintenance.

**Article 1663:** When there are many compensable debts, the same rules as for imputation of payment shall be followed for compensation.

**Article 1664:** When both debts are not payable in the same place, neither party may oppose compensation, unless both debts are of money, and the one opposing compensation takes into account the costs of remittance.

---

## TITLE XVIII: OF CONFUSION

**Article 1665:** When the qualities of creditor and debtor concur in the same person, a confusion occurs by operation of law that extinguishes the debt and produces the same effects as payment.

**Article 1666:** Confusion that extinguishes the principal obligation extinguishes the surety; but confusion that extinguishes the surety does not extinguish the principal obligation.

**Article 1667:** If the concurrence of the two qualities occurs only in a part of the debt, there is no confusion, nor is the debt extinguished except in that part.

**Article 1668:** If there is confusion between one of several joint debtors and the creditor, the former may claim from each of their co-debtors for the part or share corresponding to them respectively in the debt.

If on the contrary, there is confusion between one of several joint creditors and the debtor, the former shall be obliged to each of their co-creditors for the part or share corresponding to them respectively in the credit.

**Article 1669:** The credits and debts of the heir who accepted with benefit of inventory are not confused with the debts and credits of the inheritance.

---

## TITLE XIX: OF THE LOSS OF THE THING OWED

**Article 1670:** When the certain body owed perishes, either because it is destroyed, or because it ceases to be in commerce, or because it disappears and it is unknown whether it exists, the obligation is extinguished; excepting however the exceptions of the following articles.

**Article 1671:** Whenever the thing perishes in the debtor's power, it is presumed to have been by their act or fault.

**Article 1672:** If the certain body perishes by the debtor's fault or during their default, the debtor's obligation subsists, but changes object; the debtor is obliged to the price of the thing and to indemnify the creditor.

However, if the debtor is in default and the certain body owed perishes by fortuitous event that would have equally happened to said body in the creditor's power, only indemnity for damages of the default shall be owed. But if the fortuitous event could not have equally happened in the creditor's power, the price of the thing and damages of the default are owed.

**Article 1673:** If the debtor has made themselves responsible for all fortuitous event, or for some in particular, what was agreed shall be observed.

**Article 1674:** The debtor is obliged to prove the fortuitous event they allege.

If being in default they claim that the certain body would have perished equally in the creditor's power, they shall also be obliged to prove it.

**Article 1675:** If the lost thing whose existence was unknown reappears, the creditor may claim it, restoring what they have received by reason of its price.

**Article 1676:** One who has stolen or robbed a certain body shall not be permitted to allege that the thing has perished by fortuitous event, even of those that would have produced the destruction or loss of the certain body in the creditor's power.

**Article 1677:** Even if by the thing having perished the debtor's obligation is extinguished, the creditor may demand that the debtor assign to them the rights or actions they have against those by whose act or fault the thing perished.

**Article 1678:** If the thing owed is destroyed by a voluntary act of the debtor, who inculpably ignored the obligation, only the price shall be owed without other damages.

**Article 1679:** In the act or fault of the debtor, the act or fault of persons for whom they are responsible is included.

**Article 1680:** Destruction of the thing in the debtor's power, after it has been offered to the creditor, and during the creditor's delay in receiving it, does not make the debtor responsible except for gross fault or fraud.

---

## TITLE XX: OF NULLITY AND RESCISSION

**Article 1681:** Any act or contract lacking any of the requirements prescribed by law for the validity of the same act or contract, according to its species and the quality or status of the parties, is null.

Nullity may be absolute or relative.

**Article 1682:** Nullity produced by an unlawful object or cause, and nullity produced by omission of some requirement or formality that the laws prescribe for the validity of certain acts or contracts in consideration of their nature, and not of the quality or status of the persons executing or agreeing them, are absolute nullities.

There is also absolute nullity in acts and contracts of absolutely incapable persons.

Any other kind of defect produces relative nullity and gives the right to rescission of the act or contract.

**Article 1683:** Absolute nullity may and must be declared by the judge, even without a party's request, when it appears manifestly in the act or contract; may be alleged by anyone having an interest in it, except the one who executed the act or celebrated the contract, knowing or having reason to know the defect that invalidated it; may likewise be requested by the public ministry in the interest of morality or law; and cannot be cured by ratification of the parties, nor by a lapse of time not exceeding ten years.

**Article 1684:** Relative nullity may not be declared by the judge except at the request of a party; nor may its declaration be requested by the public ministry in the sole interest of the law; nor may it be alleged except by those in whose benefit the laws have established it or by their heirs or assignees; and it may be cured by lapse of time or by ratification of the parties.

**Article 1685:** If on the part of the incapable person there has been fraud to induce the act or contract, neither they nor their heirs or assignees may allege nullity.

However, the assertion of legal age, or of the interdiction or other cause of incapacity not existing, shall not disqualify the incapable person from obtaining a declaration of nullity.

**Article 1686:** Acts and contracts of incapable persons in which the necessary formalities and requirements have not been omitted may not be declared null or rescinded except for the causes for which persons who freely administer their property would enjoy this benefit.

**Article 1687:** Nullity pronounced in a judgment having the force of res judicata gives the parties the right to be restored to the same state in which they would be if the null act or contract had not existed; without prejudice to the provisions on unlawful object or cause.

In the mutual restitutions to be made by the contractors by virtue of this pronouncement, each shall be responsible for the loss of species or their deterioration, interest and fruits, and for the allowance of necessary, useful, or voluptuary improvements, taking into consideration fortuitous events and the possession of good or bad faith of the parties; all according to general rules and without prejudice to the provisions of the following article.

**Article 1688:** If the contract celebrated with an incapable person without the requirements required by law is declared null, the one who contracted with them may not request restitution or reimbursement of what they spent or paid by virtue of the contract, except insofar as they prove that the incapable person became richer by it.

The incapable person shall be understood to have become richer insofar as the things paid or acquired by means of them were necessary to them; or insofar as the things paid or acquired by means of them, which were not necessary to them, subsist and are desired to be retained.

**Article 1689:** Nullity judicially pronounced gives reclamation action against third-party possessors; without prejudice to legal exceptions.

**Article 1690:** When two or more persons have contracted with a third party, the nullity declared in favor of one of them shall not benefit the others.

**Article 1691:** The period for requesting rescission shall last four years.

This four-year period shall be counted, in the case of violence, from the day it ceased; in the case of error or fraud, from the day of celebration of the act or contract.

When nullity arises from a legal incapacity, the four-year period shall be counted from the day this incapacity ceased.

All this is understood in cases where special laws have not designated another period.

**Article 1692:** Adult heirs shall enjoy the full four-year period if it has not begun to run; and shall enjoy the remainder otherwise.

For minor heirs, the four-year period or its remainder begins to run from when they reach legal age.

But in this case, nullity may not be requested after ten years from celebration of the act or contract.

**Article 1693:** Ratification necessary to cure nullity when the defect of the contract is susceptible to this remedy may be express or tacit.

**Article 1694:** For express ratification to be valid, it shall be made with the formalities to which the act or contract being ratified is subject by law.

**Article 1695:** Tacit ratification is the voluntary execution of the contracted obligation.

**Article 1696:** Neither express nor tacit ratification shall be valid unless emanating from the party or parties having the right to allege nullity.

**Article 1697:** The express or tacit ratification of one who is not capable of contracting is not valid.

---

## TITLE XXI: OF THE PROOF OF OBLIGATIONS

**Article 1698:** It is incumbent to prove obligations or their extinction on the one who alleges them.

Proofs consist of public or private instruments, witnesses, presumptions, confession of party, deferred oath, and personal inspection by the judge.

**Article 1699:** A public or authentic instrument is one authorized with legal formalities by the competent official.

Executed before a notary and incorporated in a protocol or public registry, it is called a public deed.

**Article 1700:** The public instrument gives full faith as to the fact of having been executed and its date, but not as to the truth of the declarations made in it by the interested parties. In this part, it gives full faith only against the declarants.

The obligations and discharges contained in it give full proof with respect to the grantors and to the persons to whom said obligations and discharges are transferred by universal or singular title.

**Article 1701:** The lack of a public instrument cannot be supplied by other proof in acts and contracts where the law requires that formality; and they shall be regarded as not executed or celebrated even if in them they promise to reduce them to a public instrument within a certain period, under a penal clause: this clause shall have no effect.

Outside the cases indicated in this article, the instrument defective by incompetence of the official or by other defect in form shall be valid as a private instrument if signed by the parties.

**Article 1702:** The private instrument, recognized by the party against whom it is opposed, or ordered to be considered as recognized in the cases and with the requirements provided by law, has the value of a public deed with respect to those appearing or reputed to have subscribed it, and to persons to whom the obligations and rights of these have been transferred.

**Article 1703:** The date of a private instrument is not counted with respect to third parties except from the death of any of those who have signed it, or from the day it has been copied in a public registry, or it appears to have been presented in court, or a competent official has taken note of it or inventoried it, in such capacity.

**Article 1704:** Domestic entries, registers, and papers only give faith against the one who has written or signed them, but only in what appears with complete clarity, and provided the one wishing to take advantage of them does not reject them in the part unfavorable to them.

**Article 1705:** A note written or signed by the creditor at the end, in the margin, or on the back of a deed that has always been in their power gives faith in everything favorable to the debtor.

The same shall extend to the note written or signed by the creditor at the end, in the margin, or on the back of the duplicate of a deed, if said duplicate is in the debtor's power.

But the debtor who wishes to take advantage of what is favorable to them in the note must also accept what in it is unfavorable to them.

**Article 1706:** The public or private instrument gives faith between the parties even in what is merely enunciative, provided it has direct relation to the dispositive part of the act or contract.

**Article 1707:** Private writings made by the contractors to alter what was agreed in a public deed shall not produce effect against third parties.

Nor shall public counter-deeds produce effect, when note of their content has not been taken in the margin of the master deed whose dispositions are altered in the counter-deed, and of the copy by virtue of which the third party has acted.

**Article 1708:** Testimonial evidence shall not be admitted with respect to an obligation that should have been recorded in writing.

**Article 1709:** Acts or contracts containing delivery or promise of a thing worth more than two tax units must be recorded in writing. Testimonial evidence shall not be admissible insofar as it adds or alters in any way what is expressed in the act or contract, nor on what is alleged to have been said before, or at the time or after its execution, even if in some of these additions or modifications it concerns a thing whose value does not reach the said sum. Fruits, interest, or other accessories of the species or amount owed shall not be included in this sum.

**Article 1710:** One who claims a thing of more than two tax units in value shall not be admitted testimonial evidence, even if they limit the claim to that value.

Testimonial evidence is also not admissible in claims of less than two tax units when it is declared that what is claimed is part or remainder of a credit that should have been recorded in writing and was not.

**Article 1711:** Excepted from the provisions of the three preceding articles are cases where there is a beginning of proof in writing, that is, a written act of the defendant or their representative that makes the litigious fact plausible.

Thus a promissory note of more than two tax units in which a thing has been purchased to be delivered to the debtor shall not give full proof of the debt because it does not certify delivery; but it is a beginning of proof for testimonial evidence to supply this circumstance.

Also excepted are cases where it has been impossible to obtain written proof, and others expressly excepted in this Code and in the special Codes.

**Article 1712:** Presumptions are legal or judicial.

Legal presumptions are governed by article 47\.

Those deduced by the judge must be grave, precise, and concordant.

**Article 1713:** Confession made in court by someone personally, or through a special attorney, or their legal representative, and relating to a personal fact of the same party, shall give full faith against them, even if there is no beginning of proof in writing; except for cases included in article 1701, paragraph 1, and others excepted by law.

The confessor may not revoke it unless it is proved to have been the result of an error of fact.

**Article 1714:** Regarding the oath deferred by the judge or by one of the parties to the other and the personal inspection by the judge, the provisions of the Code of Procedure shall apply.

---

## TITLE XXII: OF MATRIMONIAL AGREEMENTS AND OF THE CONJUGAL PARTNERSHIP

### § 1\. General Rules

**Article 1715:** Matrimonial agreements are the patrimonial agreements celebrated by the spouses before contracting marriage or in the act of its celebration.

In matrimonial agreements celebrated in the act of marriage, only total separation of property or the regime of participation in the partnership gains may be agreed. In the case of spouses of the same sex, the provisions of the following paragraph shall apply.

Spouses of the same sex may celebrate matrimonial agreements, but in no case may they agree to the regime of conjugal partnership.

**Article 1716:** Matrimonial agreements shall be executed by public deed, and shall only be valid between the parties and with respect to third parties from the day of the celebration of marriage, and provided they are sub-registered in the margin of the respective marriage registration at the time of the celebration or within thirty days following. But in the cases referred to in the second paragraph of the preceding article, it shall suffice that these agreements appear in said registration. Without this requirement, they shall have no value.

In the case of marriages celebrated in a foreign country and not registered in Chile, it shall be necessary to first proceed to their registration in the Registry of the First Section of the commune of Santiago, for which the marriage certificate duly legalized shall be exhibited to the corresponding civil officer. In these cases, the period referred to in the preceding paragraph shall be counted from the date of registration of the marriage in Chile.

Once the marriage is celebrated, the agreements may not be altered, even with the consent of all persons who intervened in them, except in the case established in paragraph 1 of article 1723\.

**Article 1717:** Matrimonial agreements shall not contain stipulations contrary to good morals or to the laws. They shall not be, therefore, to the detriment of the rights and obligations that the laws assign to each spouse with respect to the other or to common descendants.

**Article 1718:** In the absence of an agreement to the contrary, the conjugal partnership shall be understood to be contracted by the mere fact of marriage, in accordance with the provisions of this title.

**Article 1719:** The wife, notwithstanding the conjugal partnership, may renounce her right to the partnership gains resulting from the husband's administration, provided she makes this renunciation before marriage or after dissolution of the partnership.

The foregoing is understood without prejudice to the legal effects of participation in the partnership gains, separation of property, and divorce.

In the case of the regime of participation in the partnership gains, the provisions of Title XXII-A of Book IV must be followed.

**Article 1720:** In matrimonial agreements, total or partial separation of property may be stipulated. In the former case, the rules given in articles 158, paragraph 2, 159, 160, 161, 162, and 163 of this Code shall apply; and in the latter, the provisions of article 167 shall apply.

It may also be stipulated that the wife freely dispose of a determined sum of money, or of a determined periodic pension, and this agreement shall produce the effects indicated in article 167\.

**Article 1721:** One who is under curatorship shall need the authorization of their curator for matrimonial agreements.

**Article 1722:** Deeds that alter or add to matrimonial agreements, executed before marriage, shall not be valid if they do not comply with the formalities prescribed in this title for the agreements themselves.

**Article 1723:** During marriage, spouses may substitute the regime of partnership of property for that of participation in the partnership gains or for that of total separation. They may also substitute total separation for the regime of participation in the partnership gains.

The agreement spouses celebrate in accordance with this article shall be executed by public deed and shall not produce effects between the parties nor with respect to third parties, except from when that deed is sub-registered in the margin of the respective marriage registration. This sub-registration may only be made within thirty days following the date of the deed in which separation is agreed. The agreement contained in it shall not prejudice, in any case, rights validly acquired by third parties with respect to the husband or wife, and once celebrated, may not be set aside by mutual consent of the spouses.

In the public deed of total separation of property, or in the one agreeing participation in the partnership gains, as applicable, spouses may liquidate the conjugal partnership or proceed to determine the participation credit or celebrate other lawful agreements, or one and the other; but all this shall not produce any effect between the parties nor with respect to third parties except from the sub-registration referred to in the preceding paragraph.

In the case of marriages celebrated in a foreign country and not registered in Chile, it shall be necessary to first proceed to their registration in the Registry of the First Section of the commune of Santiago, for which the marriage certificate duly legalized shall be exhibited to the corresponding civil officer.

The agreements referred to in this article and in paragraph 2 of article 1715 are not susceptible to any condition, term, or mode.

**Article 1724:** If a donation is made to either spouse, or an inheritance or legacy is left with the condition that the fruits of the donated, inherited, or bequeathed things do not belong to the conjugal partnership, the condition shall be valid, unless it concerns property donated or assigned by way of rigorous legitimate share.

### § 2\. Of the Estate of the Conjugal Partnership and Its Burdens

**Article 1725:** The estate of the conjugal partnership is composed of:

1. Salaries and emoluments of all kinds of employments and offices, earned during the marriage;  
2. All fruits, proceeds, pensions, interest, and profits of any nature, arising either from social property or from the own property of each spouse, and accruing during the marriage;  
3. Money either spouse contributes to the marriage, or acquires during it; the partnership undertaking to pay the corresponding reimbursement;  
4. Fungible things and movable species either spouse contributes to the marriage, or acquires during it; the partnership undertaking to pay the corresponding reimbursement. But spouses may exempt from community any part of their movable species, designating them in the matrimonial agreements;  
5. All property either spouse acquires during the marriage for consideration.

**Article 1726:** Acquisitions of real property made by either spouse by way of donation, inheritance, or legacy shall be added to the property of the donee, heir, or legatee spouse; and acquisitions of real property made by both spouses simultaneously by any of these titles shall not increase the social estate, but that of each spouse.

If the acquired property is movable, it shall increase the social estate, which shall owe the corresponding reimbursement to the acquiring spouse or spouses.

**Article 1727:** Notwithstanding the provisions of article 1725, the following shall not enter to compose the social estate:

1. The immovable duly subrogated to another immovable of either spouse;  
2. Things purchased with own funds of either spouse, destined for it in the matrimonial agreements or in a donation by reason of marriage;  
3. All material increases accreting to any species of either spouse forming one body with it, by alluvion, building, planting, or any other cause.

**Article 1728:** Land contiguous to an own estate of either spouse, and acquired by them during marriage by any title that makes it communicable according to article 1725, shall be understood to belong to the partnership; unless with it and the old estate an estate or building has been formed from which the newly acquired land cannot be dismembered without damage; in which case the partnership and the said spouse shall be co-owners of the whole, in proportion to the respective values at the time of incorporation.

**Article 1729:** The property of things one spouse possessed with others in common, and of which during marriage they became owner by any onerous title, shall belong in common to said spouse and the partnership, in proportion to the value of the share belonging to the former, and to what the acquisition of the rest cost.

**Article 1730:** Mines denounced by either spouse or by both shall be added to the social estate.

**Article 1731:** The part of the treasure that according to law belongs to the finder shall be added to the social estate, which shall owe the corresponding reimbursement to the spouse who finds it; and the part of the treasure that according to law belongs to the owner of the land in which it is found shall be added to the social estate, which shall owe reimbursement to the spouse who is the owner of the land.

**Article 1732:** Immovable property donated or assigned by any other gratuitous title shall be understood to belong exclusively to the donee or assignee spouse; and it shall not be considered whether donations or other gratuitous acts in favor of one spouse have been made out of consideration for the other.

If the things donated or assigned by any other gratuitous title are movable, they shall be understood to belong to the partnership, which shall owe the corresponding reimbursement to the donee or assignee spouse.

**Article 1733:** For an immovable to be understood as subrogated to another immovable of one of the spouses, it is necessary that the second has been exchanged for the first, or that, sold during the marriage, the first was purchased with its price; and that in the deed of exchange or in the deeds of sale and purchase, the intention to subrogate is expressed.

An immovable may also be subrogated to own funds of one of the spouses, not consisting of real property; but for the subrogation to be valid, it shall be necessary that the funds have been destined for it, in accordance with number 2 of article 1727, and that in the purchase deed of the immovable, the investment of said funds and the intention to subrogate appear.

If one estate is subrogated to another and the sale price of the old estate exceeds the purchase price of the new one, the partnership shall owe reimbursement for this excess to the subrogating spouse; and if, on the contrary, the purchase price of the new estate exceeds the sale price of the old one, the subrogating spouse shall owe reimbursement for this excess to the partnership.

If two estates are exchanged and a cash balance is received, the partnership shall owe reimbursement for this balance to the subrogating spouse, and if on the contrary a balance is paid, the reimbursement shall be owed by said spouse to the partnership.

The same rule shall apply to the case of subrogating an immovable to funds.

But there shall be no subrogation when the balance in favor or against the partnership exceeds half the price of the estate received, which shall then belong to the social estate, the partnership remaining obliged to reimburse the spouse for the price of the alienated estate, or for the funds invested, and the latter retaining the right to carry out the subrogation by purchasing another estate.

Subrogation made in the wife's property also requires her authorization.

**Article 1734:** All reimbursements shall be paid in money, so that the sum paid has, as far as possible, the same purchasing value as the sum invested at the time the reimbursement originated.

The partitioner shall apply this rule according to natural equity.

**Article 1735:** The spouse administering the partnership may make donations of social property if they are of small amount, considering the resources of the social estate.

**Article 1736:** The species acquired during the partnership does not belong to it even if acquired for consideration, when the cause or title of the acquisition preceded it.

Consequently:

1. Species one spouse possessed as lord before it shall not belong to the partnership, even if the prescription or transaction by which they truly made them theirs is completed or verified during it;  
2. Nor property possessed before it by a defective title, but whose defect has been cured during it by ratification or by other legal remedy;  
3. Nor property returning to one spouse by nullity or resolution of a contract, or by revocation of a donation;  
4. Nor litigious property of which one spouse has acquired peaceful possession during the partnership;  
5. Nor shall the right of usufruct that consolidates with property belonging to the same spouse belong to the partnership; only fruits shall belong to the partnership;  
6. What is paid to either spouse for capital of credits constituted before marriage shall belong to the creditor spouse. The same applies to interest accrued by one spouse before marriage and paid after.  
7. Property the spouse acquires during the partnership by virtue of an act or contract whose celebration was promised before it shall also belong to the spouse, provided the promise appears in a public instrument, or in a private instrument whose date is opposable to third parties in accordance with article 1703\. If the acquisition is made with partnership property and the spouse's, the latter shall owe the respective reimbursement. If the property referred to in the preceding numbers is movable, it shall enter the social estate, which shall owe the corresponding reimbursement to the acquiring spouse.

**Article 1737:** Property acquired during the partnership is deemed to be that which should have been acquired during it by one of the spouses, and which in fact was not acquired until after dissolution of the partnership, because no news of them was had or because their acquisition or enjoyment was unjustly hindered.

Fruits that without this ignorance or without this hindrance should have been perceived by the partnership, and that after it were restored to said spouse or their heirs, shall be regarded as belonging to the partnership.

**Article 1738:** Remuneratory donations of real property made to one spouse or both, for services that gave no action against the person served, do not increase the social estate; but those made for services that would have given action against said person increase the social estate up to the amount of what there would have been action to demand for them, and no more; except that said services were rendered before the partnership, in which case said donations shall not be adjudicated to the partnership in any part.

If the remuneratory donation is of movable things, it shall increase the social estate, which shall owe reimbursement to the donee spouse if the services gave no action against the person served or if the services were rendered before the partnership.

**Article 1739:** Any amount of money and of fungible things, all species, credits, rights, and actions existing in the power of either spouse during the partnership or at the time of its dissolution shall be presumed to belong to it, unless the contrary appears or is proved.

Neither the declaration of one spouse affirming that a thing is theirs or owed to them, nor the confession of the other, nor both together, shall be considered sufficient proof, even if made under oath.

The confession shall, however, be regarded as a revocable donation, which, confirmed by the donor's death, shall be executed in their share of partnership gains or in their own property, as applicable.

In the case of movable property, third parties contracting for consideration with either spouse shall be protected from any claim they may bring based on the property being social or the other spouse's, provided the contracting spouse has made delivery or tradition of the respective property to the third party in good faith.

The third party's good faith shall not be presumed when the property object of the contract appears registered in the other spouse's name in a register open to the public, such as in the case of automobiles, shares of corporations, ships, aircraft, etc.

Any property acquired for consideration by either spouse after dissolution of the conjugal partnership and before its liquidation is presumed to have been acquired with social property. The spouse shall therefore owe reimbursement to the partnership, unless they prove having acquired it with own property or from their sole personal activity.

**Article 1740:** The partnership is obliged to pay:

1. All pensions and interest running either against the partnership or against either spouse and accruing during the partnership;  
2. Debts and obligations contracted during the marriage by the husband, or the wife with the husband's authorization, or of the court in subsidium, and not being personal of the former or the latter, as would be those contracted for the establishment of children of a previous marriage. The partnership is consequently obliged, with the same limitation, to the cost of any surety, mortgage, or pledge constituted by the husband;  
3. Personal debts of each spouse, the debtor remaining obliged to compensate the partnership for what it invests in them;  
4. All usufructuary charges and repairs of social property or of each spouse;  
5. Maintenance of the spouses; maintenance, education, and establishment of common descendants; and any other family burden.

Maintenance one spouse is obliged by law to give to their descendants or ascendants, even if not of both spouses, shall be regarded as a family burden; but the judge may moderate this expense if it appears excessive, charging the excess to the spouse's estate.

If the wife reserves in the matrimonial agreements the right to have delivered to her once or periodically an amount of money of which she may freely dispose, this payment shall be for the partnership's account, provided in the matrimonial agreements it has not been expressly imposed on the husband.

**Article 1741:** If some thing of the husband or wife is sold, the partnership shall owe reimbursement for the price to the selling spouse, except insofar as said price has been invested in the subrogation mentioned in article 1733, or in another personal business of the spouse whose thing was sold; such as in payment of their personal debts, or in the establishment of their descendants of a previous marriage.

**Article 1742:** The husband or wife shall owe reimbursement to the partnership for the value of any donation they make of any part of the social estate; unless it is of small amount, considering the resources of the social estate, or is made for an object of eminent piety or charity, and without causing serious impairment to said estate.

**Article 1743:** If the husband or wife disposes by reason of death of a species belonging to the partnership, the assignee of said species may pursue it on the testator's succession provided the species, in the division of the partnership gains, has been adjudicated to the testator's heirs; but otherwise, they shall only have the right to pursue its price on the testator's succession.

**Article 1744:** Ordinary and extraordinary expenses of education of a common descendant, and those made to establish or marry them, shall be charged to the partnership gains, provided it does not appear in an authentic manner that the husband, wife, or both jointly intended these expenses to be taken from their own property. Even if they are immediately taken from the own property of either spouse, they shall be understood to be made at the partnership's expense, unless contrary declaration.

In the case of these expenses having been made by one spouse without contradiction or claim from the other, and not appearing in an authentic manner that the husband or wife intended to make them from their own, the wife, husband, or heirs of either may request reimbursement from the other's own property, by half, for the part of said expenses not covered by the partnership gains; and it shall be left to the judge's prudence to grant this request in whole or in part, considering the resources and obligations of the two estates, and the discretion and moderation with which the spouse proceeded in said expenses.

All this applies to the case where the descendant has no own property; if they do, extraordinary expenses shall be charged to their property insofar as they fit, and insofar as they were effectively useful to them; unless it appears in an authentic manner that the husband, wife, or both jointly intended to make them from their own.

**Article 1745:** In general, prices, balances, court costs, and expenses of all kinds incurred in the acquisition or collection of property, rights, or credits belonging to either spouse shall be presumed paid by the partnership, unless proved otherwise, and shall be reimbursed to it.

Consequently: The spouse acquiring property by way of inheritance must reimburse the partnership for all hereditary or testamentary debts and burdens they cover, and for all costs of acquisition; except insofar as they prove having covered them with the same hereditary property or with their own.

**Article 1746:** Reimbursement is likewise owed for expenses of all kinds made on property of either spouse, insofar as said expenses have increased the value of the property, and insofar as this value subsists on the date of dissolution of the partnership; unless this increase in value exceeds the expenses, in which case only the amount of the latter shall be owed.

**Article 1747:** In general, reimbursement is owed to the partnership for any gratuitous and substantial expenditure in favor of a third party who is not a common descendant.

**Article 1748:** Each spouse shall also owe reimbursement to the partnership for damage caused to it with fraud or gross fault, and for payment it makes of fines and pecuniary reparations to which they are condemned for any crime or quasi-crime.

### § 3\. Of the Ordinary Administration of the Property of the Conjugal Partnership

**Article 1749:** The husband is the head of the conjugal partnership, and as such administers the social property and that of his wife; subject, however, to the obligations and limitations imposed on him by this Title and to those he may have contracted by the matrimonial agreements.

As administrator of the conjugal partnership, the husband shall exercise the rights of the wife who, being a partner of a civil or commercial society, marries, without prejudice to the provisions of article 150\.

The husband may not alienate or voluntarily encumber nor promise to alienate or encumber the social real property nor the wife's hereditary rights without her authorization.

Nor may he, without said authorization, dispose between living persons gratuitously of social property, except in the case of article 1735, nor lease or assign the holding of urban social real property for more than five years, nor rural for more than eight, including extensions agreed by the husband.

If the husband constitutes himself as guarantor, joint co-debtor, surety, or grants any other security with respect to obligations contracted by third parties, he shall only bind his own property.

In the cases referred to in the preceding paragraph, to bind social property he shall need the wife's authorization.

The wife's authorization shall be specific and granted in writing, or by public deed if the act requires this solemnity, or by her intervening expressly and directly in any way in the act. It may be granted in all cases by special mandate in writing or by public deed as the case may be.

The authorization referred to in this article may be supplied by the judge, after hearing the wife, if she refuses without just cause. It may also be supplied by the judge in case of any impediment of the wife, such as dementia, real or apparent absence, or other, and delay would cause harm. But said authorization may not be supplied if the wife opposes the donation of social property.

**Article 1750:** The husband is, with respect to third parties, owner of social property, as if they and his own property formed a single estate, so that during the partnership the husband's creditors may pursue both his property and social property; without prejudice to the allowances or compensations that consequently the husband owes to the partnership or the partnership owes to the husband.

Creditors may, however, pursue their rights over the wife's property by virtue of a contract entered into by them with the husband, insofar as it is proved that the contract benefited the wife personally, such as in payment of her debts prior to marriage.

**Article 1751:** Any debt contracted by the wife with the husband's general or special mandate is, with respect to third parties, the husband's debt and consequently the partnership's; and the creditor may not pursue payment of this debt over the wife's own property, but only over partnership property and the husband's own property; without prejudice to the provisions of paragraph 2 of the preceding article.

If the wife agent contracts in her own name, the provisions of article 2151 shall apply.

Contracts entered into by the husband and wife jointly or in which the wife binds herself jointly or subsidiarily with the husband shall not be valid against the wife's own property, except in the cases and terms of the said paragraph 2, and without prejudice to the provisions of paragraph 1 of article 137\.

**Article 1752:** The wife by herself alone has no right over social property during the partnership, except in the cases of article 145\.

**Article 1753:** Even if the wife renounces the partnership gains in the matrimonial agreements, she shall not thereby have the faculty to receive the fruits of her own property, which are understood to be granted to the husband to support the burdens of marriage, but with the obligation to preserve and restore said property, as will be stated later.

The foregoing shall be understood without prejudice to the rights of the divorced or separated wife as to property.

**Article 1754:** The wife's real property may not be alienated or encumbered except with her consent.

The wife's consent shall be specific and granted by public deed, or by her intervening expressly and directly in any way in the act. It may be granted in all cases by special mandate in a public deed.

The wife's consent may be supplied by the judge when she is unable to express her will.

The wife, on her part, may not alienate or encumber nor lease or assign the holding of the property of hers administered by the husband, except in the cases of articles 138 and 138 bis.

**Article 1755:** To alienate or encumber other property of the wife, which the husband is or may be obliged to restore in species, the wife's consent shall suffice, which may be supplied by the judge when the wife is unable to express her will.

**Article 1756:** Without the wife's authorization, the husband may not lease or assign the holding of her rural estates for more than eight years, nor urban for more than five, including extensions agreed by the husband.

The provisions of paragraphs 7 and 8 of article 1749 apply to this case.

**Article 1757:** Acts executed without complying with the requirements prescribed in articles 1749, 1754, and 1755 shall suffer from relative nullity. In the case of lease or assignment of holding, the contract shall only apply for the period indicated in articles 1749 and 1756\.

The preceding nullity or inopposability may be alleged by the wife, her heirs, or assignees.

The four-year period to request nullity shall be counted from dissolution of the conjugal partnership, or from when the incapacity of the wife or her heirs ceases.

In no case may nullity be requested after ten years from celebration of the act or contract.

### § 4\. Of the Extraordinary Administration of the Conjugal Partnership

**Article 1758:** The wife who, in the case of interdiction of the husband, or by prolonged absence of the husband without communication with his family, has been appointed curator of the husband, or curator of his property, shall by the same fact have the administration of the conjugal partnership.

If by incapacity or excuse of the wife these curatorships are entrusted to another person, the curator shall direct the administration of the conjugal partnership.

**Article 1759:** The wife who has the administration of the partnership shall administer with the same faculties as the husband.

Nevertheless, without judicial authorization with prior knowledge of the case, she may not alienate or voluntarily encumber nor promise to alienate or encumber social real property.

Nor may she, without said authorization, dispose between living persons gratuitously of social property, except in the case of article 1735\.

Any act in contravention of this article shall be relatively null. The action shall correspond to the husband, his heirs, or assignees, and the four-year period to request nullity shall be counted from when the cause that motivated the curatorship ceases.

In no case may nullity be requested after ten years from celebration of the act or contract.

If the wife who has the extraordinary administration of the conjugal partnership constitutes herself as guarantor, joint co-debtor, surety, or grants any other security with respect to third parties, she shall only bind her own property and that she administers in accordance with articles 150, 166, and 167\. To bind social property, she shall need authorization from the court, given with knowledge of the case.

In the administration of the husband's own property, the rules of curatorships shall apply.

**Article 1760:** All acts and contracts of the wife administrator, not forbidden by the preceding article, shall be regarded as acts and contracts of the husband, and shall consequently bind the partnership and the husband; except insofar as it appears or is proved that said acts and contracts were made in the wife's personal business.

**Article 1761:** The wife administrator may lease social immovable property or assign its holding, and the husband or his heirs shall be obliged to fulfillment of what is agreed for a space of time not exceeding the limits indicated in paragraph 4 of article 1749\.

This lease or assignment, however, may last longer if the wife, to stipulate it so, has been specially authorized by the court, after information of utility.

**Article 1762:** The wife who does not wish to take upon herself the administration of the conjugal partnership, nor submit to the direction of a curator, may request separation of property; and in such case the provisions of Title VI, paragraph 3 of Book I shall be observed.

**Article 1763:** Once the cause of the extraordinary administration mentioned in the preceding articles ceases, the husband shall recover his administrative faculties, after judicial decree.

### § 5\. Of the Dissolution of the Conjugal Partnership and Distribution of Partnership Gains

**Article 1764:** The conjugal partnership is dissolved:

1. By dissolution of the marriage;  
2. By presumption of death of one of the spouses, according to the provisions of the title On the beginning and end of persons;  
3. By the judgment of judicial separation or total separation of property: if separation is partial, the partnership shall continue over property not included in it;  
4. By declaration of nullity of the marriage;  
5. By the agreement of participation in the partnership gains or total separation of property, according to Title XXII-A of Book IV and article 1723\.

**Article 1765:** Once the partnership is dissolved, an inventory and appraisal of all property it usufructed or for which it was responsible shall immediately proceed, in the period and form prescribed for succession by reason of death.

**Article 1766:** Inventory and appraisal made without judicial solemnity shall have no value in court, except against the spouse, heirs, or creditors who have duly approved and signed them.

If among the participants in the partnership gains there are minors, demented, or other persons incompetent for the administration of their property, solemn inventory and appraisal shall be necessary; and if omitted, the one to whom this omission is imputable shall be liable for damages; and proceedings shall proceed as soon as possible to legalize said inventory and appraisal in due form.

**Article 1767:** The wife who has not renounced the partnership gains before marriage or after dissolution of the partnership shall be understood to accept them with benefit of inventory.

**Article 1768:** That spouse or their heirs who fraudulently concealed or diverted any thing from the partnership shall lose their share in the same thing and shall be obliged to restore it doubled.

**Article 1769:** To the social estate shall be imaginarily added all that the spouses are respectively debtors to the partnership, by way of reimbursement or indemnity, according to the rules given above.

**Article 1770:** Each spouse, personally or through their heirs, shall have the right to take from the mass the species or certain bodies belonging to them, and the prices, balances, and reimbursements constituting the remainder of their estate.

Restitution of species or certain bodies shall be made as soon as possible after completion of the inventory and appraisal; and payment of the remainder of the estate within one year from said completion. The judge may, however, extend or restrict this period at the request of interested parties, with prior knowledge of the case.

**Article 1771:** Losses or deteriorations occurring in said species or certain bodies shall be borne by the owner, except if due to fraud or gross fault of the other spouse, in which case they must compensate for them.

For increase arising from natural causes independent of human industry, nothing shall be owed to the partnership.

**Article 1772:** Fruits pending at the time of restitution, and all perceived since dissolution of the partnership, shall belong to the owner of the respective species.

Fruits perceived from social property since dissolution of the partnership accrue to the social estate.

**Article 1773:** The wife shall make the deductions mentioned in the preceding articles before the husband; and those consisting in money, whether belonging to the wife or husband, shall be executed on the money and movable property of the partnership, and subsidiarily on its immovable property.

The wife, if the partnership property is insufficient, may make the deductions corresponding to her over the husband's own property, chosen by common agreement. If not agreed, the judge shall choose.

**Article 1774:** Once the aforementioned deductions are executed, the residue shall be divided by half between the two spouses.

**Article 1775:** Testamentary assignments made by the deceased spouse to the surviving spouse shall not be charged to the surviving spouse's half of partnership gains, except if the deceased so ordered; but in such case the surviving spouse may repudiate them, if they prefer to abide by the result of the partition.

**Article 1776:** The division of social property shall be subject to the rules given for the partition of hereditary property.

**Article 1777:** The wife is not responsible for debts of the partnership except up to the amount of her half of partnership gains.

But to enjoy this benefit, she must prove the excess of the contribution required of her over her half of partnership gains, whether by inventory and appraisal, or by other authentic documents.

**Article 1778:** The husband is responsible for the total of the debts of the partnership; saving his action against the wife for reimbursement of half of these debts, according to the preceding article.

**Article 1779:** That spouse who, by effect of a mortgage or pledge constituted over a species that fell to them in the division of the social mass, pays a debt of the partnership, shall have action against the other spouse for reimbursement of half of what they pay; and paying a debt of the other spouse, shall have action against them for reimbursement of all they pay.

**Article 1780:** The heirs of each spouse enjoy the same rights and are subject to the same actions as the spouse they represent.

### § 6\. Of the Renunciation of Partnership Gains Made by the Wife After Dissolution of the Partnership

**Article 1781:** Once the partnership is dissolved, the wife or her adult heirs shall have the faculty to renounce the partnership gains to which they are entitled.

**Article 1782:** The wife may renounce as long as she has not received into her power any part of the social estate by way of partnership gains.

Once made, the renunciation may not be rescinded, unless it is proved that the wife or her heirs were induced to renounce by deceit or by a justifiable error about the true state of the social affairs.

This rescissory action shall prescribe in four years, counted from the dissolution of the partnership.

**Article 1783:** Upon renunciation by the wife or her heirs, the rights of the partnership and of the husband are confused and identified, even with respect to her.

**Article 1784:** The wife who renounces retains her rights and obligations to the reimbursements and indemnities expressed above.

**Article 1785:** If only a part of the wife's heirs renounces, the portions of those who renounce accrue to the husband's portion.

### § 7\. Of Dowry and Donations by Reason of Marriage
