**Article 1786:** Donations one spouse makes to the other before marriage and in consideration of it, and donations a third party makes to either spouse before or after marriage and in consideration of it, are called in general donations by reason of marriage.

**Article 1787:** Promises one spouse makes to the other before marriage and in consideration of it, or a third party makes to one spouse in consideration of marriage, shall be subject to the same rules as present donations, but must be recorded in a public deed, or by confession of the third party.

**Article 1788:** Neither spouse may make donations to the other by reason of marriage except up to the value of one-fourth of the property they contribute.

**Article 1789:** Donations by reason of marriage, whether called dowry, gifts, or any other denomination, admit terms, conditions, and any other lawful stipulations, and are subject to the general rules of donations, in all that does not oppose the special provisions of this title.

In all of them, the condition of the marriage being or having been celebrated is understood.

**Article 1790:** Once nullity of the marriage is declared, all donations made by reason of the same marriage to the one who contracted it in bad faith may be revoked, provided the donation and its cause appear in a public deed.

The final judgment of judicial separation or divorce authorizes, on its part, the revocation of all donations made by reason of the same marriage to the spouse who gave cause for judicial separation or divorce through their fault, the condition indicated in the preceding paragraph being verified.

In the deed of the donor spouse, the cause of marriage is always presumed, even if not expressed.

The putative spouse who also contracted in bad faith shall lack this revocatory action.

**Article 1791:** In donations between living persons or testamentary assignments by reason of marriage, the resolutory condition of the donee or assignee failing without leaving succession shall not be understood, nor any other not expressed in the respective instrument, or prescribed by law.

**Article 1792:** If by act of one spouse the marriage is dissolved before consummation, donations made by reason of marriage to them may be revoked in the terms of article 1790\.

The spouse by whose act the marriage is dissolved shall lack this revocatory action.

---

## TITLE XXII-A: REGIME OF PARTICIPATION IN PARTNERSHIP GAINS

### Chapter I: § 1\. General Rules

**Article 1792-1:** In the matrimonial agreements they celebrate in accordance with paragraph one of Title XXII of Book Four of the Civil Code, spouses may agree to the regime of participation in the partnership gains.

Spouses may, subject to the provisions of article 1723 of the same Code, substitute the regime of conjugal partnership or of separation for the regime of participation contemplated in this Title. In the same way, they may substitute the regime of participation in the partnership gains for total separation of property.

**Article 1792-2:** In the regime of participation in the partnership gains, the estates of the spouses remain separate and each spouse administers, enjoys, and freely disposes of their own. At the end of the regime's duration, the value of the gains obtained by the spouses is compensated and they have the right to participate by halves in the surplus.

The preceding principles apply in the form and with the limitations indicated in the following articles and in paragraph I of Title VI of Book I of the Civil Code.

### § 2\. Of the Administration of the Spouses' Estate

**Article 1792-3:** Neither spouse may grant personal securities for third-party obligations without the other spouse's consent. Said authorization shall be subject to the provisions of articles 142, paragraph 2, and 144, of the Civil Code.

**Article 1792-4:** Acts executed in contravention of the preceding article shall suffer from relative nullity.

The four-year period to request nullity shall be counted from the day the spouse alleging it had knowledge of the act.

But in no case may rescission be pursued after ten years from celebration of the act or contract.

**Article 1792-5:** Upon dissolution of the regime of participation in the partnership gains, the estates of the spouses shall remain separate, they or their heirs retaining full faculties of administration and disposition over their property.

On the same date, the gains obtained during the validity of the regime of participation in the partnership gains shall be determined.

### § 3\. Of the Determination and Calculation of Gains

**Article 1792-6:** Gains are understood to be the difference in net value between the initial estate and the final estate of each spouse.

The initial estate of each spouse is understood to be that existing at the time of opting for the regime established in this Title, and their final estate that existing at the end of said regime.

**Article 1792-7:** The initial estate shall result from deducting from the total value of property of which the spouse is owner at the beginning of the regime, the total value of obligations of which they are debtor on that same date. If the value of obligations exceeds the value of property, the initial estate shall be considered without value.

Gratuitous acquisitions made during the validity of the regime shall be added to the initial estate, deducting the burdens with which they are encumbered.

**Article 1792-8:** Property acquired during the validity of the regime of participation in the partnership gains shall be added to the assets of the initial estate, even if acquired for consideration, when the cause or title of acquisition is prior to the beginning of the property regime.

Consequently, and without the following enumeration being exhaustive, the following shall be added to the assets of the initial estate:

1) Property one spouse possessed before the property regime, even if the prescription or transaction by which they made them their own occurred or was agreed during the validity of the property regime.  
2) Property possessed before the property regime under a defective title, provided the defect was cured during the validity of the property regime by ratification or other legal means.  
3) Property returning to one spouse by nullity or resolution of a contract, or by revocation of a donation.  
4) Litigious property of which either spouse acquired peaceful possession during the validity of the regime.  
5) The right of usufruct that has consolidated with the bare property belonging to the same spouse.  
6) What is paid to either spouse for capital of credits constituted before the validity of the regime. The same shall apply to interest accrued before and paid after.  
7) The proportion of the price paid prior to the beginning of the regime for property acquired as a result of promise contracts.

**Article 1792-9:** Fruits, even those arising from initial property, shall not be incorporated into the initial estate. Nor mines denounced by one spouse, nor remuneratory donations for services that gave action against the person served.

**Article 1792-10:** Spouses are co-owners, according to general rules, of property acquired jointly for consideration. If the acquisition has been gratuitous by both spouses, the rights shall be added to the respective initial estates, in the proportion established by the respective title, or in equal parts, if the title says nothing about it.

**Article 1792-11:** Spouses, at the time of agreeing to this regime, shall make a simple inventory of property composing the initial estate.

In the absence of inventory, the initial estate may be proved by other instruments, such as registries, invoices, or credit titles.

However, other means of proof shall be admitted if it is shown that, given the circumstances, the spouse was not in a position to obtain an instrument.

**Article 1792-12:** At the end of the regime of participation in the partnership gains, movable property acquired during it is presumed common, except for personal use property of the spouses. Proof to the contrary must be based on written records.

**Article 1792-13:** Property composing the initial assets is valued according to its state at the time of entry into force of the property regime or of its acquisition. Consequently, its price at the time of incorporation into the initial estate shall be prudentially updated to the date of termination of the regime.

Valuation may be made by the spouses or by a third party designated by them. In subsidium, by the judge.

The preceding rules also apply to the valuation of liabilities.

**Article 1792-14:** The final estate shall result from deducting from the total value of property of which the spouse is owner at the time the regime ends, the total value of obligations they have on that same date.

**Article 1792-15:** In the final estate of a spouse, the amounts of decreases in their assets that are a consequence of the following acts, executed during the validity of the regime of participation in the partnership gains, shall be imaginarily added:

1) Irrevocable donations that do not correspond to proportionate fulfillment of moral duties or social uses, considering the person of the donee.  
2) Any kind of fraudulent or wasteful acts to the detriment of the other spouse.  
3) Payment of prices of life annuities or other expenses intended to secure a future income for the spouse who incurred them. The provisions of this number shall not apply with respect to life annuities agreed under the provisions of Decree Law No. 3,500 of 1980, except for voluntary additional contributions in the individual capitalization account and deposits in voluntary savings accounts, which shall be imaginarily added in accordance with the first paragraph of this article.

The additions referred to shall be made considering the state things had at the time of their alienation.

The provisions of this article do not apply if the act was authorized by the other spouse.

**Article 1792-16:** Within three months following the end of the regime of participation in the partnership gains, each spouse shall be obliged to provide the other with a valued inventory of the property and obligations comprising their final estate. The judge may extend this period once and for the same term.

The simple inventory, signed by the spouse, shall constitute proof in favor of the other spouse to determine their final estate. However, the latter may challenge the inventory, alleging it is not trustworthy. In such case, they may use all means of proof to demonstrate the composition or effective value of the other spouse's estate.

Either spouse may request the preparation of an inventory in accordance with the rules of the Code of Civil Procedure and request the precautionary measures that apply.

**Article 1792-17:** Property composing the final assets is valued according to its state at the time of termination of the property regime.

Property referred to in article 1792-15 shall be valued according to the value they would have had at the end of the property regime.

Valuation of property may be made by the spouses or by a third party designated by them. In subsidium, by the judge.

The preceding rules also apply to the valuation of liabilities.

**Article 1792-18:** If either spouse, in order to decrease gains, conceals or diverts property or simulates obligations, double the value of the former or the latter shall be added to their final estate.

**Article 1792-19:** If a spouse's final estate is less than the initial one, they alone shall bear the loss.

If only one spouse has obtained gains, the other shall participate in half of their value.

If both spouses have obtained gains, these shall be compensated up to the concurrence of those of lesser value and the one who obtained lesser gains shall have the right for the other to pay them, by way of participation, half of the surplus.

The participation credit in the partnership gains shall be without prejudice to other credits and obligations between spouses.

### § 4\. Of the Participation Credit in the Partnership Gains

**Article 1792-20:** The participation credit in the partnership gains shall originate at the end of the property regime.

Any agreement or contract regarding that eventual credit, as well as its renunciation, is prohibited before the end of the regime of participation in the partnership gains.

**Article 1792-21:** The participation credit in the partnership gains is pure and simple and shall be paid in money.

However, if the foregoing causes serious harm to the debtor spouse or the common children, and this is duly proved, the judge may grant a period of up to one year for payment of the credit, which shall be expressed in monthly tax units. This period shall not be granted if it is not secured, by the debtor themselves or a third party, that the creditor spouse will in any case remain indemnified.

**Article 1792-22:** Spouses, or their heirs, may agree to payment in kind to settle the participation credit in the partnership gains.

The credit shall revive, in the terms of the first paragraph of the preceding article, if the thing given in payment is evicted, unless the creditor spouse has assumed the risk of eviction, specifying it.

**Article 1792-23:** To determine the participation credits in the partnership gains, attributions of rights over family property, made to one spouse in accordance with article 147 of the Civil Code, shall be prudentially valued by the judge.

**Article 1792-24:** The creditor spouse shall pursue payment, first, in the debtor's money; if insufficient, in movable property and, in subsidium, in immovable property.

In the absence or insufficiency of all indicated property, they may pursue their credit in property donated between living persons, without their consent, or alienated in fraud of their rights. If they pursue property donated between living persons, they must proceed against the donees in an order inverse to the dates of the donations, that is, beginning with the most recent. This action shall prescribe in four years from the date of the act.

**Article 1792-25:** Credits against a spouse whose cause is prior to the end of the property regime shall prevail over the participation credit in the partnership gains.

**Article 1792-26:** The action to request the liquidation of the gains shall be processed briefly and summarily, prescribe in five years from the termination of the regime, and shall not be suspended between spouses. However, it shall be suspended in favor of their minor heirs.

### § 5\. Of the Termination of the Regime of Participation in the Partnership Gains

**Article 1792-27:** The regime of participation in the partnership gains ends:

1) By the death of one spouse.  
2) By the presumption of death of one spouse, according to the provisions of Title II, "Of the beginning and end of the existence of persons", of Book One of the Civil Code.  
3) By declaration of nullity of marriage or judgment of divorce.  
4) By judicial separation of spouses.  
5) By the judgment declaring separation of property.  
6) By the agreement of separation of property.  
7) By dissolution of marriage in the case provided by numeral 5 of article 42 of the Civil Marriage Law, contained in article 1 of Law No. 19,947.

---

## TITLE XXIII: OF PURCHASE AND SALE

**Article 1793:** Purchase and sale is a contract in which one party obliges themselves to give a thing and the other to pay for it in money. The former is said to sell and the latter to buy. The money the buyer gives for the thing sold is called the price.

**Article 1794:** When the price consists partly in money and partly in another thing, it shall be understood as exchange if the thing is worth more than the money; and as sale in the opposite case.

### § 1\. Of Capacity for the Contract of Sale

**Article 1795:** All persons not declared incompetent by law to celebrate it or to celebrate any contract are competent for the contract of sale.

**Article 1796:** The contract of purchase and sale between spouses not judicially separated, and between the father or mother and the child subject to parental authority, is null.

**Article 1797:** Administrators of public establishments are prohibited from selling any part of the property they administer, and whose alienation is not included in their ordinary administrative faculties; except in the case of express authorization from the competent authority.

**Article 1798:** Public employees are prohibited from buying public or private property sold by their ministry; and judges, lawyers, attorneys, or notaries from buying property in whose litigation they have intervened, and which is sold as a consequence of the litigation; even if the sale is made at public auction.

**Article 1799:** It is not lawful for guardians and curators to buy any part of their wards' property, except in accordance with the provisions of the title On the administration of guardians and curators.

**Article 1800:** Attorneys, receivers in bankruptcies, and executors are subject, regarding the purchase or sale of things that are to pass through their hands by virtue of these charges, to the provisions of article 2144\.

### § 2\. Form and Requirements of the Contract of Sale

**Article 1801:** Sale is considered perfect from the time the parties have agreed on the thing and the price; except for the following exceptions.

The sale of real property, servitudes, and ground rents, and that of a hereditary succession, are not considered perfect before the law, as long as a public deed has not been executed.

Pending fruits and flowers, trees whose wood is sold, materials of a building to be demolished, materials naturally adhering to the ground, such as stones and mineral substances of all kinds, are not subject to this exception.

**Article 1802:** If the contractors stipulate that the sale of other things than those enumerated in paragraph 2 of the preceding article shall not be considered perfect until the execution of a public or private deed, either party may withdraw as long as the deed is not executed or delivery of the thing sold has not begun.

**Article 1803:** If sold with earnest money, that is, giving a thing in pledge of the celebration or execution of the contract, it is understood that each of the contractors may withdraw; the one who gave the earnest money losing it; and the one who received it returning it doubled.

**Article 1804:** If the contractors have not fixed a period within which they may withdraw, losing the earnest money, there shall be no withdrawal after two months following the agreement, nor after a public deed of the sale has been executed or delivery has begun.

**Article 1805:** If earnest money is expressly given as part of the price, or as a sign of the contractors being agreed, the sale shall be perfect; without prejudice to the provisions of article 1801, paragraph 2\.

If none of these expressions appears in writing, it shall be presumed as a matter of law that the contractors reserve the faculty to withdraw according to the two preceding articles.

**Article 1806:** Fiscal or municipal taxes, costs of the deed, and any other solemnities of the sale shall be for the seller's account; unless otherwise agreed.

**Article 1807:** Sale may be pure and simple, or under suspensive or resolutory condition.

It may be made at term for delivery of the thing or of the price.

It may have for its object two or more alternative things.

In all these respects, it is governed by the general rules of contracts, insofar as not modified by those of this title.

### § 3\. Of the Price

**Article 1808:** The price of the sale must be determined by the contractors.

This determination may be made by any means or indications that fix it.

If it concerns fungible things and sold at the market price, it shall be understood as the price of the day of delivery, unless otherwise stated.

**Article 1809:** The price may also be left to the discretion of a third party; and if the third party does not determine it, any other person on whom the contractors agree may do so; in case of not agreeing, there shall be no sale.

The price may not be left to the discretion of one of the contractors.

### § 4\. Of the Thing Sold

**Article 1810:** All corporeal or incorporeal things whose alienation is not prohibited by law may be sold.

**Article 1811:** The sale of all present or future property or of both, whether selling the total or a share, is null; but the sale of all species, kinds, and quantities designated by public deed shall be valid, even if it extends to what the seller possesses or expects to acquire, provided it does not include unlawful objects.

Things not included in this designation shall be understood not to be in the sale: any contrary stipulation is null.

**Article 1812:** If the thing is common to two or more persons in common, between whom there is no partnership contract, each of them may sell their share, even without the consent of the others.

**Article 1813:** The sale of things that do not exist but are expected to exist shall be understood to be made under the condition of existing, unless otherwise expressed, or if by the nature of the contract it appears that the chance was purchased.

**Article 1814:** The sale of a thing that at the time the contract is perfected is supposed to exist and does not exist produces no effect.

If a considerable part of it was lacking at the time the contract was perfected, the buyer may at their option withdraw from the contract, or consider it subsisting, paying the price at fair appraisal.

The one who knowingly sold what in whole or in a considerable part did not exist shall compensate the buyer in good faith.

**Article 1815:** The sale of another's thing is valid, without prejudice to the rights of the owner of the thing sold, as long as they are not extinguished by lapse of time.

**Article 1816:** The purchase of one's own thing is not valid: the buyer shall have the right to have what they gave for it restored to them.

Natural fruits, pending at the time of sale, and all fruits, both natural and civil, that the thing later produces shall belong to the buyer, unless it has been stipulated to deliver the thing after a certain time or in the event of a certain condition; in which case fruits shall not belong to the buyer except upon expiration of the term or fulfillment of the condition.

All said in this article may be modified by express stipulations of the contractors.

### § 5\. Of the Immediate Effects of the Contract of Sale

**Article 1817:** If someone separately sells the same thing to two persons, the buyer who has entered into possession shall be preferred to the other; if delivery has been made to both, the one to whom it was made first shall be preferred; if delivered to neither, the oldest title shall prevail.

**Article 1818:** The sale of another's thing, later ratified by the owner, gives the buyer the rights of such from the date of the sale.

**Article 1819:** If another's thing is sold and delivered, and the seller later acquires ownership of it, the buyer shall be regarded as the true owner from the date of tradition.

Consequently, if the seller sells it to another person after acquiring ownership, the buyer's ownership of it shall subsist.

**Article 1820:** Loss, deterioration, or improvement of the species or certain body sold belongs to the buyer from the moment the contract is perfected, even if the thing has not been delivered; except if sold under suspensive condition and the condition is fulfilled, then, the species totally perishing while the condition is pending, the loss shall be the seller's, and the improvement or deterioration shall belong to the buyer.

**Article 1821:** If a thing is sold of those usually sold by weight, count, or measure, but designated so that it cannot be confused with another portion of the same thing, such as all the wheat contained in a certain granary, the loss, deterioration, or improvement shall belong to the buyer, even if said thing has not been weighed, counted, or measured; provided the price has been fixed.

If of things usually sold by weight, count, or measure, only an undetermined part is sold, such as ten bushels of wheat from those contained in a certain granary, the loss, deterioration, or improvement shall not belong to the buyer except after the price has been fixed and said part weighed, counted, or measured.

**Article 1822:** If seller and buyer, having agreed on the price, set a day for weighing, counting, or measuring, and one or the other does not appear on that day, they shall be obliged to compensate the other for damages resulting from their negligence; and the seller or buyer who did not miss the appointment may, if convenient, withdraw from the contract.

**Article 1823:** If it is stipulated that sale is on trial, it is understood that there is no contract as long as the buyer does not declare that the thing pleases them, and the loss, deterioration, or improvement belongs in the meantime to the seller.

Without need of express stipulation, the sale of all things customarily sold in that way is understood to be on trial.

### § 6\. Of the Seller's Obligations and Firstly of the Obligation to Deliver

**Article 1824:** The seller's obligations reduce in general to two: delivery or tradition, and warranty of the thing sold.

Tradition shall be subject to the rules given in Title VI of Book II.

**Article 1825:** Costs incurred to put the thing in condition to be delivered naturally belong to the seller, and those incurred to transport it after delivery to the buyer.

**Article 1826:** The seller is obliged to deliver the thing sold immediately after the contract or at the time fixed in it.

If the seller by their act or fault has delayed delivery, the buyer may at their option persist in the contract or withdraw from it, and in both cases with the right to be indemnified for damages according to general rules.

All this is understood if the buyer has paid or is ready to pay the full price or has stipulated to pay at term.

But if after the contract the buyer's fortune has considerably diminished, so that the seller is in imminent danger of losing the price, delivery may not be demanded even if a term for payment of the price has been stipulated, except by paying or securing payment.

**Article 1827:** If the buyer is in default of receiving, they shall pay the seller the rent of the warehouses, granaries, or vessels containing what was sold, and the seller shall be discharged from the ordinary care of preserving the thing, and shall only be responsible for fraud or gross fault.

**Article 1828:** The seller is obliged to deliver what the contract states.

**Article 1829:** The sale of a cow, mare, or other female naturally includes the offspring she carries in her womb or suckles; but not the one that can graze and feed itself.

**Article 1830:** In the sale of an estate, all accessories that according to articles 570 and following are deemed immovable are naturally included.

**Article 1831:** A rural estate may be sold with relation to its area or as a species or certain body.

It is sold with relation to its area whenever it is expressed in any way in the contract, except if the parties declare they do not intend to make a difference in price even if the actual area is greater or less than the area stated in the contract.

It is indifferent whether a total price is fixed directly, or it is deduced from the area or number of measures expressed, and from the price of each measure.

It is likewise indifferent whether a total area is expressed or the areas of the various portions of different qualities and prices contained in the estate, provided from these data the total price and total area result.

The same applies to the alienation of two or more estates by a single sale.

In all other cases, the estate or estates shall be understood to be sold as a certain body.

**Article 1832:** If the estate is sold with relation to its area, and the actual area is greater than the declared area, the buyer shall proportionally increase the price; except if the price of the excess area reaches more than one-tenth of the price of the actual area; in which case the buyer may, at their option, either proportionally increase the price or withdraw from the contract; and if they withdraw, they shall be compensated for damages according to general rules.

And if the actual area is less than the declared area, the seller shall complete it; and if this is not possible for them, or is not demanded, they shall suffer a proportional reduction of the price; but if the price of the missing area reaches more than one-tenth of the price of the complete area, the buyer may, at their option, either accept the reduction of the price or withdraw from the contract in the terms of the preceding paragraph.

**Article 1833:** If the estate is sold as a certain body, there shall be no right on the part of the buyer or seller to request reduction or increase of the price, whatever the area of the estate.

However, if sold with designation of boundaries, the seller shall be obliged to deliver all included in them; and if they cannot or it is not demanded, the provisions of paragraph 2 of the preceding article shall be observed.

**Article 1834:** Actions given in the two preceding articles expire within one year from delivery.

**Article 1835:** The rules given in the two said articles apply to any whole or set of effects or merchandise.

**Article 1836:** In addition to the actions given in said articles, the contractors have the action of enormous injury as applicable.

### § 7\. Of the Obligation of Warranty and Firstly of Warranty for Eviction

**Article 1837:** The obligation of warranty includes two objects: to protect the buyer in the ownership and peaceful possession of the thing sold, and to be liable for its hidden defects, called redhibitory vices.

**Article 1838:** There is eviction of the thing purchased when the buyer is deprived of all or part of it by judicial sentence.

**Article 1839:** The seller is obliged to warrant to the buyer all evictions that have a cause prior to the sale, except insofar as otherwise stipulated.

**Article 1840:** The warranty action is indivisible. It may therefore be brought in solutum against any of the seller's heirs.

But from the time the obligation to protect the buyer in possession is succeeded by the obligation to indemnify them in money, the action is divided; and each heir is responsible only proportionally to their hereditary share.

The same rule applies to sellers who have alienated the thing by a single act of sale.

**Article 1841:** One sued for a purchased thing may bring against the third party from whom their seller acquired it, the warranty action that would belong against said third party to the seller, if the seller had remained in possession of the thing.

**Article 1842:** Any agreement exempting the seller from warranty for eviction is null, provided there has been bad faith on their part in that agreement.

**Article 1843:** The buyer sued for the thing sold, for a cause prior to the sale, must summon the seller to appear to defend it.

This summons shall be made within the period indicated by the Code of Procedure.

If the buyer omits to summon them, and the thing is evicted, the seller shall not be obliged to warrant; and if the summoned seller does not appear to defend the thing sold, they shall be responsible for the eviction; unless the buyer has failed to oppose some defense or exception of their own, and for that reason the thing is evicted.

**Article 1844:** If the seller appears, the claim shall proceed against them alone; but the buyer may always intervene in the lawsuit for the preservation of their rights.

**Article 1845:** If the seller does not oppose any means of defense, and acquiesces to the warranty, the buyer may nevertheless sustain the defense by themselves; and if they are defeated, they shall have no right to demand from the seller reimbursement of the costs incurred defending themselves, nor of the fruits perceived during said defense and paid to the owner.

**Article 1846:** The obligation to warrant shall cease in the following cases:

1. If the buyer and the one claiming the thing as their own submit to arbitration without the seller's consent, and the arbitrators rule against the buyer;  
2. If the buyer lost possession through their fault, and eviction followed from it.

**Article 1847:** Warranty for eviction, to which the seller is obliged, includes:

1. Restitution of the price, even if the thing at the time of eviction is worth less;  
2. Of the legal costs of the contract of sale paid by the buyer;  
3. Of the value of fruits the buyer was obliged to restore to the owner; without prejudice to the provisions of article 1845;  
4. Of the costs the buyer has suffered as a consequence and by effect of the claim; without prejudice to the provisions of the same article;  
5. The increase in value the evicted thing has taken in the buyer's power, even by natural causes or by the mere passage of time.

All with the following limitations.

**Article 1848:** If the lesser value of the thing arises from deteriorations from which the buyer has derived benefit, due discount shall be made in the restitution of the price.

**Article 1849:** The seller shall be obliged to reimburse the buyer for the increase in value arising from necessary or useful improvements made by the buyer, except insofar as the one who obtained eviction has been condemned to pay for them.

The seller in bad faith shall be obliged even to reimbursement of what the voluptuary improvements cost.

**Article 1850:** Increase in value due to natural causes or to time shall not be paid insofar as it exceeds one-fourth of the sale price; unless the seller's bad faith is proved, in which case they shall be obliged to pay all increase in value, whatever cause it arises from.

**Article 1851:** In forced sales made by authority of the court, the seller is not obliged, by reason of eviction suffered by the thing sold, except to restore the price produced by the sale.

**Article 1852:** The stipulation exempting the seller from the obligation to warrant eviction does not exempt them from the obligation to restore the price received.

And they shall be obliged to restore the full price, even if the thing has deteriorated or diminished in any way its value, even by the buyer's act or negligence, except insofar as the buyer has derived benefit from the deterioration.

The obligation to restore the price shall cease if the buyer bought knowing it was another's thing, or if they expressly assumed the risk of eviction, specifying it.

If eviction does not affect the entire thing sold, and the evicted part is such that it is to be presumed the thing would not have been bought without it, there shall be the right to request rescission of the sale.

**Article 1853:** By virtue of this rescission, the buyer shall be obliged to restore to the seller the non-evicted part, and for this restitution they shall be considered as a possessor in good faith, unless proved otherwise; and the seller in addition to restoring the price shall pay the value of the fruits the buyer was obliged to restore with the evicted part, and all other damage resulting to the buyer from the eviction.

**Article 1854:** If the evicted part is not of such importance, or if rescission of the sale is not requested, the buyer shall have the right to demand warranty for the partial eviction in accordance with articles 1847 and following.

**Article 1855:** If the judgment denies the eviction, the seller shall not be obliged to indemnify for damages the claim caused the buyer, except insofar as the claim is imputable to the seller's act or fault.

**Article 1856:** The warranty action for eviction prescribes in four years; but as to the sole restitution of the price, it prescribes according to general rules.

Time shall be counted from the date of the eviction judgment; or if it has not been pronounced, from restitution of the thing.

### § 8\. Of Warranty for Redhibitory Vices

**Article 1857:** The redhibitory action is the one the buyer has for the sale to be rescinded or the price proportionally reduced for the hidden vices of the thing sold, real or movable, called redhibitory.

**Article 1858:** Redhibitory vices are those having the following qualities:

1. Having existed at the time of sale;  
2. Being such that by them the thing sold does not serve for its natural use, or only serves imperfectly, so that it is to be presumed that knowing them the buyer would not have bought it or would have bought it at much less price;  
3. The seller not having manifested them, and them being such that the buyer could have ignored them without grave negligence on their part, or such that the buyer could not easily know them by reason of their profession or trade.

**Article 1859:** If it has been stipulated that the seller is not obliged to warranty for hidden vices of the thing, they shall nevertheless be obliged to warrant those of which they had knowledge and of which they did not give notice to the buyer.

**Article 1860:** Redhibitory vices give the buyer the right to demand either rescission of the sale or reduction of the price, as they see fit.

**Article 1861:** If the seller knew the vices and did not declare them, or if the vices were such that the seller should have known them by reason of their profession or trade, they shall be obliged not only to restitution or reduction of the price, but to indemnification of damages; but if the seller did not know the vices nor were they such that by their profession or trade they should know them, they shall only be obliged to restitution or reduction of the price.

**Article 1862:** If the vicious thing has perished after the contract of sale was perfected, the buyer shall not thereby lose the right to reduction of the price, even if the thing perished in their power and through their fault.

But if it perished by effect of the inherent vice, the rules of the preceding article shall apply.

**Article 1863:** The parties may by the contract make redhibitory vices that naturally are not such.

**Article 1864:** When two or more things are sold together, whether a price has been fixed for the set or for each of them, there shall only be place for the redhibitory action for the vicious thing and not for the set; unless it appears that the set would not have been bought without that thing; as when a team, yoke, or pair of animals, or a set of furniture is bought.

**Article 1865:** The redhibitory action does not apply in forced sales made by authority of the court. But if the seller, not being able or not having reason to ignore the vices of the thing sold, did not declare them at the buyer's request, there shall be place for the redhibitory action and indemnification of damages.

**Article 1866:** The redhibitory action shall last six months with respect to movable things and one year with respect to real property, in all cases where special laws or the stipulations of the contractors have not extended or restricted this period. The time shall be counted from real delivery.

**Article 1867:** Once the redhibitory action has prescribed, the buyer shall still have the right to demand reduction of the price and indemnification of damages according to the preceding rules.

**Article 1868:** If the hidden vices are not of the importance expressed in number 2 of article 1858, the buyer shall not have the right to rescission of the sale but only to reduction of the price.

**Article 1869:** The action to demand reduction of the price, whether in the case of article 1858 or in the case of article 1868, prescribes in one year for movable property and in eighteen months for real property.

**Article 1870:** If the purchase was made to send the thing to a distant place, the action for reduction of the price shall prescribe in one year from delivery to the consignee, plus the term of summons corresponding to the distance.

But it shall be necessary that the buyer in the intervening time between the sale and the sending could have ignored the vice of the thing without negligence on their part.

### § 9\. Of the Buyer's Obligations

**Article 1871:** The principal obligation of the buyer is to pay the agreed price.

**Article 1872:** The price shall be paid in the place and time stipulated, or in the place and time of delivery, there being no stipulation to the contrary.

However, if the buyer is disturbed in possession of the thing or proves that there exists against it a real action of which the seller has not given them notice before the contract was perfected, they may deposit the price with authority of the court, and the deposit shall last until the seller causes the disturbance to cease or provides security for the results of the lawsuit.

**Article 1873:** If the buyer is in default of paying the price in the place and time said, the seller shall have the right to demand the price or the resolution of the sale, with damages.

**Article 1874:** The clause of ownership not being transferred except by payment of the price shall produce no other effect than the alternative demand stated in the preceding article; and upon payment of the price, alienations the buyer may have made of the thing or rights constituted over it in the intervening time shall in all cases subsist.

**Article 1875:** The resolution of the sale for non-payment of the price shall give the seller the right to retain the earnest money, or demand it doubled, and in addition to have the fruits restored, either in their entirety if no part of the price has been paid, or in the proportion corresponding to the part of the price not paid.

The buyer in turn shall have the right for the part of the price they have paid to be restored.

For the allowance of expenses to the buyer and of deteriorations to the seller, the former shall be considered as a possessor in bad faith, unless they prove having suffered in their fortune, and without their fault, such great losses as made it impossible for them to fulfill what was agreed.

**Article 1876:** The resolution for non-payment of the price does not give the seller action against third-party possessors, except in accordance with articles 1490 and 1491\.

If in the deed of sale the payment of the price is expressed, no contrary proof shall be admitted except that of nullity or falsification of the deed, and only by virtue of this proof shall there be action against third-party possessors.

### § 10\. Of the Pact of Forfeiture

**Article 1877:** By the pact of forfeiture, it is expressly stipulated that, the price not being paid at the agreed time, the contract of sale shall be resolved.

This stipulation is always understood in the contract of sale; and when expressed, it takes the name of pact of forfeiture, and produces the effects to be indicated.

**Article 1878:** The pact of forfeiture does not deprive the seller of the choice of actions granted by article 1873\.

**Article 1879:** If it is stipulated that by the price not being paid at the agreed time, the contract of sale is resolved ipso facto, the buyer may nevertheless make it subsist by paying the price, at the latest, within twenty-four hours following judicial notification of the claim.

**Article 1880:** The pact of forfeiture prescribes at the period fixed by the parties, if not exceeding four years from the date of the contract.

After these four years, it necessarily prescribes, whether a longer period or none has been stipulated.

### § 11\. Of the Pact of Retro-Sale

**Article 1881:** By the pact of retro-sale, the seller reserves the faculty to recover the thing sold, reimbursing the buyer the determined amount stipulated, or in default of this stipulation what the purchase cost them.

**Article 1882:** The pact of retro-sale in its effects against third parties is subject to the provisions of articles 1490 and 1491\.

**Article 1883:** The seller shall have the right for the buyer to restore the thing sold with its natural accessions.

They shall also have the right to be indemnified for deteriorations imputable to the buyer's act or fault.

They shall be obliged to pay necessary expenses, but not those invested in useful or voluptuary improvements made without their consent.

**Article 1884:** The right arising from the pact of retro-sale may not be assigned.

**Article 1885:** The time in which the retro-sale action may be brought shall not exceed four years from the date of the contract.

But in all cases, the buyer shall have the right to prior notice, not less than six months for real property nor fifteen days for movable things; and if the thing is fruit-bearing and does not give fruits except from time to time and as a consequence of preparatory work and investments, the restitution demanded may not be demanded until after the next perception of fruits.

### § 12\. Of Other Accessory Pacts to the Contract of Sale

**Article 1886:** If it is agreed that, upon a person appearing within a certain time (not exceeding one year) who improves the purchase, the contract shall be resolved, what is agreed shall be fulfilled; unless the buyer or the person to whom the buyer alienated the thing agrees to improve in the same terms the purchase.

The provision of article 1882 applies to the present contract.

Once the contract is resolved, mutual payments shall take place as in the case of the pact of retro-sale.

**Article 1887:** Any other lawful accessory pacts may be added to the contract of sale; and shall be governed by the general rules of contracts.

### § 13\. Of Rescission of Sale for Enormous Injury

**Article 1888:** The contract of purchase and sale may be rescinded for enormous injury.

**Article 1889:** The seller suffers enormous injury when the price received is less than half the fair price of the thing sold; and the buyer in turn suffers enormous injury when the fair price of the thing bought is less than half the price they pay for it.

The fair price refers to the time of the contract.

**Article 1890:** The buyer against whom rescission is pronounced may, at their option, consent to it, or complete the fair price with deduction of one-tenth; and the seller in the same case may, at their option, consent to rescission, or restore the excess of the price received over the fair price increased by one-tenth.

Interest or fruits shall not be owed except from the date of the claim, nor may anything be demanded in reason of expenses caused by the contract.

**Article 1891:** There shall be no place for the rescissory action for enormous injury in sales of movable property, nor in those made by the ministry of the court.

**Article 1892:** If it is stipulated that the rescissory action for enormous injury may not be brought, the stipulation shall not be valid; and if on the seller's part the intention to donate the excess is expressed, this clause shall be considered not written.

**Article 1893:** If the thing is lost in the buyer's power, there shall be no right on either side for rescission of the contract.

The same shall be if the buyer has alienated the thing; except if they sold it for more than they had paid for it; in which case the first seller may claim this excess, but only up to the fair value of the thing, with deduction of one-tenth.

**Article 1894:** The seller may not demand anything in reason of deteriorations the thing has suffered; except insofar as the buyer has taken advantage of them.

**Article 1895:** The buyer who is in the case of restoring the thing must previously free it from mortgages or other real rights they have constituted on it.

**Article 1896:** The rescissory action for enormous injury expires in four years from the date of the contract.

---

## TITLE XXIV: OF EXCHANGE

**Article 1897:** Exchange or barter is a contract in which the parties mutually oblige themselves to give one species or certain body for another.

**Article 1898:** Exchange is considered perfect by mere consent; except if one of the things exchanged or both are real property or rights of hereditary succession, in which case, for perfection of the contract before the law, a public deed shall be necessary.

**Article 1899:** Things that cannot be sold may not be exchanged.

Nor are persons competent for the contract of exchange who are not competent for the contract of sale.

**Article 1900:** The provisions relating to purchase and sale shall apply to exchange in all that does not oppose the nature of this contract; each exchanger shall be considered as seller of the thing they give, and its fair price on the date of the contract shall be regarded as the price they pay for what they receive in exchange.

---

## TITLE XXV: OF ASSIGNMENT OF RIGHTS

### § 1\. Of Personal Credits

**Article 1901:** Assignment of a personal credit, by any title made, shall have no effect between the assignor and assignee except by delivery of the title.

**Article 1902:** Assignment produces no effect against the debtor nor against third parties, as long as it has not been notified by the assignee to the debtor or accepted by the latter.

**Article 1903:** Notification must be made with exhibition of the title, which shall bear annotated the transfer of the right with the designation of the assignee and under the assignor's signature.

**Article 1904:** Acceptance shall consist of an act that supposes it, such as issue joined with the assignee, a beginning of payment to the assignee, etc.

**Article 1905:** Without the aforementioned notification or acceptance, the debtor may pay the assignor, or the credit may be attached by the assignor's creditors; and in general, the credit shall be considered as existing in the assignor's hands with respect to the debtor and third parties.

**Article 1906:** Assignment of a credit includes its sureties, privileges, and mortgages; but does not transfer the assignor's personal exceptions.

**Article 1907:** The one who assigns a credit for consideration becomes responsible for its existence at the time of assignment, that is, that it truly belonged to them at that time; but they do not become responsible for the debtor's solvency, unless expressly undertaken; nor in such case shall they be understood to be responsible for future solvency, but only for present, except if the former is expressly included; nor shall responsibility extend except to the amount of the price or emolument derived from the assignment, unless expressly stipulated otherwise.

**Article 1908:** The provisions of this title shall not apply to bills of exchange, promissory notes to order, bearer shares, and other species of transmission governed by the Commercial Code or by special laws.

### § 2\. Of the Right of Inheritance

**Article 1909:** One who assigns for consideration a right of inheritance or legacy without specifying the effects composing it becomes responsible only for their quality of heir or legatee.

**Article 1910:** If the heir has taken advantage of fruits or received credits or sold hereditary effects, they shall be obliged to reimburse their value to the assignee.

The assignee on their part shall be obliged to indemnify the assignor for necessary or prudent costs the assignor has incurred in reason of the inheritance.

If a hereditary share is assigned, the hereditary shares that by the right of accrual arise to it shall be understood to be assigned at the same time, unless otherwise stipulated.

The same rules shall apply to the legatee.

### § 3\. Of Litigious Rights

**Article 1911:** A litigious right is assigned when the direct object of the assignment is the uncertain event of the litigation, for which the assignor does not become responsible.

A right is understood to be litigious, for the purposes of the following articles, from the time the claim is judicially notified.

**Article 1912:** It is indifferent whether the assignment has been by way of sale or exchange, and whether it is the assignor or assignee who pursues the right.

**Article 1913:** The debtor shall not be obliged to pay the assignee except the value of what the latter gave for the assigned right, with interest from the date the assignment was notified to the debtor.

Excepted from the provision of this article are entirely gratuitous assignments; those made by the ministry of the court; and those included in the alienation of a thing of which the litigious right forms a part or accession.

Also excepted are assignments made:

1. To a co-heir or co-owner by a co-heir or co-owner, of a right common to both;  
2. To a creditor in payment of what the assignor owes them;  
3. To the one enjoying an immovable as possessor in good faith, usufructuary, or lessee, when the assigned right is necessary for the quiet and secure enjoyment of the immovable.

**Article 1914:** The debtor may not oppose to the assignee the benefit granted by the preceding article after nine days from notification of the decree ordering the judgment to be executed.

---

## TITLE XXVI: OF THE CONTRACT OF LEASE

**Article 1915:** Lease is a contract in which the two parties reciprocally oblige themselves, one to grant the enjoyment of a thing, or to execute a work or provide a service, and the other to pay for this enjoyment, work, or service a determined price.

### § 1\. Of Lease of Things

**Article 1916:** All corporeal or incorporeal things that can be used without being consumed are susceptible to lease; except those the law prohibits leasing, and strictly personal rights, such as those of habitation and use.

Another's thing may even be leased, and the lessee in good faith shall have warranty action against the lessor in case of eviction.

**Article 1917:** The price may consist either in money, or in natural fruits of the leased thing; and in this second case, a determined amount or a share of the fruits of each harvest may be fixed.

It is called rent when paid periodically.

**Article 1918:** The price may be determined in the same ways as in the contract of sale.

**Article 1919:** In the lease of things, the party giving enjoyment of them is called lessor, and the party giving the price lessee.

**Article 1920:** Delivery of the thing leased may be made under any of the forms of tradition recognized by law.

**Article 1921:** If it is agreed that the lease shall not be considered perfect until the deed is signed, either party may withdraw until this is done, or until delivery of the leased thing has proceeded; if earnest money intervenes, the same rules as in the contract of purchase and sale shall apply in this respect.

**Article 1922:** If the same thing has been separately leased to two persons, the lessee to whom the thing has been delivered shall be preferred; if delivered to both, the later delivery shall not be valid; if to neither, the earlier title shall prevail.

**Article 1923:** Leases of national, municipal, or public establishment property are subject to particular regulations, and insofar as not, to the provisions of this title.

### § 2\. Of the Lessor's Obligations in the Lease of Things

**Article 1924:** The lessor is obliged:

1. To deliver to the lessee the leased thing;  
2. To maintain it in a state to serve for the purpose for which it was leased;  
3. To free the lessee from any disturbance or hindrance in the enjoyment of the leased thing.

**Article 1925:** If the lessor by their act or fault or that of their agents or dependents has placed themselves in the impossibility of delivering the thing, the lessee shall have the right to withdraw from the contract, with damages.

There shall be place for this indemnity even when the lessor erroneously and in good faith believed they could lease the thing; except if the impossibility was known to the lessee, or arises from force majeure or fortuitous event.

**Article 1926:** If the lessor by their act or fault or that of their agents or dependents is in default of delivering, the lessee shall have the right to damages.

If by the delay the utility of the contract is notably diminished for the lessee, whether because the thing has deteriorated or because the circumstances that motivated it have ceased, the lessee may withdraw from the contract, their right to damages remaining, provided the delay does not arise from force majeure or fortuitous event.

**Article 1927:** The obligation to maintain the leased thing in good condition consists in making during the lease all necessary repairs, except for ordinary maintenance repairs, which generally correspond to the lessee.

But the lessor shall be obliged even to ordinary maintenance repairs, if the deteriorations that made them necessary arose from force majeure or fortuitous event, or from the bad quality of the leased thing.

The stipulations of the contractors may modify these obligations.

**Article 1928:** The lessor by virtue of the obligation to free the lessee from any disturbance or hindrance may not, without the lessee's consent, change the form of the leased thing, nor make works or constructions on it that may disturb or hinder their enjoyment of it.

However, if it concerns repairs that cannot without serious inconvenience be postponed, the lessee shall be obliged to suffer them, even if they deprive them of enjoyment of a part of the leased thing; but they shall have the right for the price or rent to be reduced in the meantime, in proportion to the part that was.

And if these repairs affect such a great part of the thing that the remainder does not appear sufficient for the purpose for which it was leased, the lessee may terminate the lease.

The lessee shall also have the right for damages to be paid to them, if the repairs proceed from a cause existing already at the time of the contract, and was not then known to the lessee, but was known to the lessor, or was such that the lessor had antecedents to fear it, or should by their profession know it.

The same shall be when the repairs are to hinder enjoyment of the thing for too long, so that the lease cannot subsist without grave annoyance or damage to the lessee.

**Article 1929:** If outside the cases provided in the preceding article, the lessee is disturbed in their enjoyment by the lessor or by any person whom the lessor can prevent, they shall have the right to damages.

**Article 1930:** If the lessee is disturbed in their enjoyment by acts of third parties not claiming right to the leased thing, the lessee in their own name shall pursue reparation of the damage.

And if they are disturbed or molested in their enjoyment by third parties who justify some right over the leased thing, and the cause of this right was prior to the contract, the lessee may demand a proportionate reduction in the price or rent of the lease, for the remaining time.

And if the lessee, by consequence of the rights justified by a third party, is deprived of so much of the leased thing that it is to be presumed they would not have contracted without that part, they may demand the lease cease.

In addition, they may demand damages for all damage, if the cause of the right justified by the third party was or should have been known to the lessor at the time of the contract, but was not known to the lessee, or being known to them, special warranty stipulation with respect to it intervened.

But if the cause of said right was not nor should have been known to the lessor at the time of the contract, the lessor shall not be obliged to pay lost profits.

**Article 1931:** The action of third parties claiming right to the leased thing shall be directed against the lessor.

The lessee shall only be obliged to notify the lessor of the disturbance or molestation they receive from said third parties by consequence of the rights they allege, and if they omit or culpably delay it, they shall pay the damages resulting to the lessor.

**Article 1932:** The lessee has the right to termination of the lease and even to rescission of the contract, according to the cases, if the bad state or quality of the thing prevents them from making the use for which it was leased, whether the lessor knew or did not know the bad state or quality of the thing at the time of the contract; and even in the case of the vice of the thing having begun to exist after the contract, but without the lessee's fault.

If the impediment to enjoyment of the thing is partial or if the thing is partly destroyed, the judge shall decide, according to circumstances, whether termination of the lease should take place, or a reduction of the price or rent be granted.

**Article 1933:** The lessee shall also have the right, in the case of the preceding article, to be indemnified for actual loss, if the vice of the thing had a cause prior to the contract.

And if the vice was known to the lessor at the time of the contract, or was such that the lessor should by antecedents foresee it or by their profession know it, lost profits shall be included in the indemnity.

**Article 1934:** The lessee shall have no right to the damages granted by the preceding article, if they contracted knowing the vice and the lessor did not undertake to warrant it; or if the vice was such that they could not without grave negligence on their part have ignored it; or if they expressly renounced the warranty action for the same vice, designating it.

**Article 1935:** The lessor is obliged to reimburse the lessee for the cost of indispensable non-ordinary maintenance repairs the lessee makes on the leased thing, provided the lessee has not made them necessary by their fault, and that they gave notice to the lessor as soon as possible, for them to make them at their own expense. If notice could not be given in time, or if the lessor did not attempt to make the repairs opportunely, the lessee shall be reimbursed for their reasonable cost, the necessity being proved.

**Article 1936:** The lessor is not obliged to reimburse the cost of useful improvements, to which they have not consented with the express condition of paying for them; but the lessee may separate and take away the materials, without damage to the leased thing; unless the lessor is willing to pay them what the materials would be worth considering them separated.

**Article 1937:** In all cases where indemnity is owed to the lessee, they may not be ejected or deprived of the leased thing, without the amount being previously paid or secured to them by the lessor.

But this rule does not extend to the case of involuntary extinction of the lessor's right over the leased thing.

### § 3\. Of the Lessee's Obligations in the Lease of Things

**Article 1938:** The lessee is obliged to use the thing according to the terms or spirit of the contract; and may not consequently make it serve for other objects than those agreed, or, in the absence of express agreement, those to which the thing is naturally destined, or that should be presumed from the circumstances of the contract or custom of the country.

If the lessee contravenes this rule, the lessor may claim termination of the lease with damages, or limit themselves to this indemnity, leaving the lease subsisting.

**Article 1939:** The lessee shall employ in the conservation of the thing the care of a good father of a family.

Failing this obligation, they shall be liable for damages; and the lessor shall even have the right to terminate the lease in the case of grave and culpable deterioration.

**Article 1940:** The lessee is obliged to ordinary maintenance repairs.

Ordinary maintenance repairs are understood to be those that according to custom of the country are the lessee's responsibility, and in general those of the species of deterioration ordinarily produced by the lessee's fault or their dependents' fault, such as damages to walls or fences, drains and ditches, broken glass, etc.

**Article 1941:** The lessee is responsible not only for their own fault, but for that of their family, guests, and dependents.

**Article 1942:** The lessee is obliged to pay the price or rent.

The lessor may, for security of this payment and of the indemnities to which they are entitled, retain all existing fruits of the leased thing, and all objects with which the lessee has furnished, equipped, or provided it, and belonging to them; and they shall be understood to belong to them, unless proved otherwise.

**Article 1943:** If after the thing is delivered to the lessee there is dispute about the price or rent, and on either side no legal proof of what was stipulated in this respect is produced, the appraisal of experts shall be followed, and the costs of this operation shall be divided between lessor and lessee in equal parts.

**Article 1944:** Payment of the price or rent shall be made in the stipulated periods, or in default of stipulation, according to the custom of the country, and if there is no stipulation nor fixed custom, according to the following rules:

Rent of urban estates shall be paid monthly, that of rural estates annually.

If a movable or semi-movable thing is leased for a certain number of years, months, days, each of the periodic pensions shall be owed immediately after the expiration of the respective year, month, or day.

If leased for a single sum, it shall be owed when the lease ends.

**Article 1945:** When by the lessee's fault the lease is terminated, the lessee shall be obliged to damages, and especially to payment of the rent for the time remaining until the day on which by giving notice they could have terminated the lease, or the lease would have ended without notice.

They may, however, exempt themselves from this payment by proposing under their responsibility a suitable person to substitute them for the remaining time, and giving at the same time sufficient surety or other competent security.

**Article 1946:** The lessee does not have the faculty to assign the lease or sublease, unless expressly granted; but in this case the assignee or sublessee may not use or enjoy the thing in other terms than those stipulated with the direct lessee.

**Article 1947:** The lessee is obliged to restore the thing at the end of the lease.

They must restore it in the state in which it was delivered to them, taking into consideration the deterioration caused by legitimate use and enjoyment.

If the state in which it was delivered does not appear, they shall be understood to have received it in a regular state of service, unless they prove otherwise.

As to damages and losses occurring during their enjoyment, they must prove that they did not occur by their fault, nor by the fault of their guests, dependents, or sublessees, and in the absence of this proof they shall be liable.

**Article 1948:** Restitution of a real thing shall be made by vacating it completely, putting it at the lessor's disposal and delivering the keys.

**Article 1949:** For the lessee to be in default of restoring the leased thing, demand by the lessor shall be necessary, even if notice has preceded; and if upon demand they do not restore it, they shall be condemned to full reparation of all damages of the default, and to whatever else applies against them as an unjust holder.

### § 4\. Of the Expiration of the Lease of Things

**Article 1950:** The lease of things expires in the same ways as other contracts, and especially:

1. By total destruction of the leased thing;  
2. By expiration of the time stipulated for the duration of the lease;  
3. By extinction of the lessor's right, according to the rules to be expressed later;  
4. By judge's sentence in cases provided by law.

**Article 1951:** If no time has been fixed for the duration of the lease, or if the time is not determined by the special service to which the leased thing is destined or by custom, neither party may terminate it except by giving notice to the other, that is, notifying them in advance.

The advance shall be adjusted to the period or measure of time regulating payments. If leased at so much per day, week, month, the notice shall be respectively of one day, one week, one month.

The notice shall begin to run at the same time as the next period.

The provisions of this article do not extend to the lease of immovable property, dealt with in paragraphs 5 and 6 of this title.

**Article 1952:** One who has given notice for termination of the lease may not later revoke it without the other party's consent.

**Article 1953:** If a fixed time has been set for one of the parties and optional for the other, what was stipulated shall be observed, and the party that may terminate the lease at their will shall nevertheless be subject to giving the advance notice aforesaid.

**Article 1954:** If in the contract a time has been fixed for the duration of the lease, or if the duration is determined by the special service to which the leased thing was destined, or by custom, no notice shall be necessary.

**Article 1955:** When the lease is to cease by virtue of notice of either party, or because its duration has been fixed in the contract, the lessee shall be obliged to pay the rent for all the days remaining until it ceases, even if they voluntarily restore the thing before the last day.

**Article 1956:** Once the lease is terminated by notice or in any other way, the lessor's apparent acquiescence to the lessee's retention of the thing shall not in any case be understood as a renewal of the contract.

If on the day of restitution the contract is not expressly renewed, the lessor shall have the right to demand it whenever they wish.

However, if the thing is real and the lessee with the lessor's approval has paid the rent for any space of time subsequent to termination, or if both parties have manifested by any other equally unequivocal act their intention to persist in the lease, the contract shall be understood to be renewed under the same conditions as before, but not for more than three months in urban estates and the time necessary to utilize the work begun and harvest pending fruits in rural estates, without prejudice that at the expiration of this time the lease may be renewed again in the same manner.

**Article 1957:** Once the lease is renewed, sureties as well as pledges or mortgages constituted by third parties shall not extend to obligations resulting from its renewal.

**Article 1958:** If the lessor's right over the leased thing is extinguished by a cause independent of their will, the lease shall expire even before the time stipulated for its duration expires.

If, for example, the lessor was usufructuary or fiduciary owner of the thing, the lease expires upon the arrival of the day on which the usufruct must cease or the property pass to the beneficiary; notwithstanding what has been stipulated between the lessor and lessee on the duration of the lease, and without prejudice to the provisions of article 794, paragraph 2\.

**Article 1959:** When the lessor has contracted in a particular capacity that makes the duration of their right uncertain, such as usufructuary, or fiduciary owner, and in all cases where their right is subject to a resolutory condition, there shall be no place for damages for termination of the lease by virtue of the resolution of the right. But if having a capacity of that kind, they have leased as absolute owner, they shall be obliged to indemnify the lessee; except if the latter contracted knowing that the lessor was not absolute owner.

**Article 1960:** In the case of expropriation for public utility, the following rules shall be observed:

1. The lessee shall be given the precise time to utilize work begun and harvest pending fruits.  
2. If the cause of expropriation is so urgent that it does not allow for this, or if the lease was stipulated for a certain number of years still pending on the date of expropriation, and so appears in a public deed, the lessee shall be owed damages by the State or expropriating corporation.  
3. If only a part of the leased thing has been expropriated, the rule of article 1930, paragraph 3, shall apply.

**Article 1961:** If the lessor's right is extinguished by their act or fault, such as when they sell the leased thing of which they are owner, or being usufructuary they make assignment of the usufruct to the owner, or lose ownership for not having paid the purchase price, they shall be obliged to indemnify the lessee in all cases where the person who succeeds in the right is not obliged to respect the lease.

**Article 1962:** The following shall be obliged to respect the lease:

1. Anyone to whom the lessor's right is transferred by a lucrative title;  
2. Anyone to whom the lessor's right is transferred for consideration, if the lease has been contracted by public deed; excepting mortgage creditors;  
3. Mortgage creditors, if the lease has been granted by public deed registered in the Registrar's Registry before the mortgage registration.

The lessee of real property may request the registration of said deed by themselves alone.

**Article 1963:** Among the damages the lessee suffers by the extinction of their author's right, and which according to the preceding articles must be compensated, those the sublessee suffers on their part shall be counted.

The direct lessee shall claim indemnity for these damages in their own name or assign their action to the sublessee.

The direct lessee shall reimburse the sublessee for advance pensions.

**Article 1964:** The pact not to alienate the leased thing, even with a clause of nullity of the alienation, shall not give the lessee any right except to remain in the lease until its natural termination.

**Article 1965:** If execution and attachment are levied on the leased thing by the lessor's creditor or creditors, the lease shall subsist, and the creditor or creditors shall be substituted in the lessor's rights and obligations.

If the thing is adjudicated to the creditor or creditors, the provisions of article 1962 shall apply.

**Article 1966:** The lessor may terminate the lease in whole or in part when the leased thing needs repairs that in whole or in part prevent its enjoyment, and the lessee shall then have the rights granted by the rules given in article 1928\.

**Article 1967:** The lessor may not in any case, unless otherwise stipulated, terminate the lease under pretext of needing the leased thing for themselves.

**Article 1968:** The declared insolvency of the lessee does not necessarily put an end to the lease.

The creditor or creditors may substitute themselves for the lessee, providing surety to the lessor's satisfaction.

If not, the lessor shall have the right to consider the lease terminated; and shall have action for damages against the lessee according to general rules.

**Article 1969:** Leases made by guardians or curators, by the father or mother as administrators of the child's property, or by the husband or wife as administrators of social property and of the other spouse, shall be subject (relatively to their duration after termination of guardianship or curatorship, or paternal or maternal administration, or administration of the conjugal partnership) to articles 407, 1749, 1756, and 1761\.

### § 5\. Particular Rules Relating to Lease of Houses, Warehouses, or Other Buildings

**Article 1970:** Ordinary maintenance repairs to which the tenant or lessee of a house is obliged reduce to maintaining the building in the state they received it; but they are not responsible for deteriorations arising from time and legitimate use, or from force majeure or fortuitous event, or from the bad quality of the building, by its old age, by the nature of the ground, or by construction defects.

**Article 1971:** The tenant shall be especially obliged:

1. To preserve the interior integrity of walls, roofs, floors, and pipes, replacing stones, bricks, and tiles broken or dislodged during the lease;  
2. To replace broken glass in windows, doors, and partitions;  
3. To maintain doors, windows, and locks in serviceable condition.

They shall be understood to have received the building in good condition under all these respects, unless proved otherwise.

**Article 1972:** The tenant is also obliged to keep walls, floors, and other interior parts of the building moderately clean; to keep wells, ditches, and pipes clean, and to sweep chimneys.

Grave negligence under any of these respects shall give the lessor the right to damages, and even to terminate the lease immediately in grave cases.

**Article 1973:** The lessor shall have the right to eject the tenant who uses the house or building for an unlawful object, or who, having the faculty to sublease, subleases to persons of notoriously bad conduct, who may likewise be ejected.

**Article 1974:** If a furnished house or room is leased, the lease of the furniture shall be understood to be for the same time as the building, unless otherwise stipulated.

**Article 1975:** One who leases a warehouse or shop is not responsible for loss of merchandise introduced there except insofar as the loss was due to their fault.

They shall be especially responsible for the bad state of the building; except if it was manifest or known to the lessee.

**Article 1976:** Notice in cases where it applies shall be given with advance notice of one entire period of those designated by agreement or law for payment of the rent.

**Article 1977:** Default of one entire period in payment of the rent shall give the lessor the right, after two demands, between which at least four days intervene, to terminate the lease immediately, if competent security that payment will be made within a reasonable period of not less than thirty days is not provided.

### § 6\. Particular Rules Relating to Lease of Rural Estates

**Article 1978:** The lessor is obliged to deliver the rural estate in the stipulated terms. If the area is different from the stipulated one, there shall be place for increase or decrease of the price or rent, or rescission of the contract, according to the provisions in the title On purchase and sale.

**Article 1979:** The farmer or rural lessee is obliged to enjoy the estate as a good father of a family; and if they do not, the lessor shall have the right to prevent misuse or deterioration of the estate, requiring for this purpose surety or other competent security, and even to terminate the lease immediately in grave cases.

**Article 1980:** The farmer is especially obliged to preserve trees and forests, limiting enjoyment of them to the stipulated terms.

In the absence of stipulation, the farmer shall limit themselves to using the forest for objects concerning cultivation and benefit of the same estate; but may not cut it for sale of timber, firewood, or charcoal.

**Article 1981:** The farmer's faculty to sow or plant does not include cutting down trees to take advantage of the place occupied by them; except as expressed in the contract.

**Article 1982:** The farmer shall see that no part of the leased land is usurped, and shall be responsible for their omission to inform the lessor, provided the extent and boundaries of the estate have been known to them.

**Article 1983:** The farmer shall not have the right to demand reduction of the price or rent, alleging extraordinary fortuitous events that have deteriorated or destroyed the harvest.

Excepted is the sharecropper farmer, for by virtue of the kind of partnership between the lessor and them, the former bears a proportionate part of the loss that by fortuitous event occurs to the latter before or after fruits are perceived; except if the accident occurs during the sharecropper farmer's default in contributing their share of fruits.
