**Article 2230:** The depositary who is not in default of restoring is not naturally responsible for force majeure or fortuitous event; but if by consequence of the accident they receive the price of the deposited thing, or another in its place, they are obliged to restore to the depositor what has been given to them.

**Article 2231:** If the heirs, not having notice of the deposit, have sold the deposited thing, the depositor (not being able or not wishing to use the reclamation action or being ineffective) may demand them to restore what they have received for said thing, or assign the actions arising from the alienation.

**Article 2232:** Necessary transport costs for restitution of the deposit shall be at the depositor's charge.

**Article 2233:** The rules of articles 2181 to 2185 apply to deposit.

**Article 2234:** The depositary may not without the depositor's consent retain the deposited thing by way of compensation, or in security of what the depositor owes them; except only in reason of expenses and damages mentioned in the following article.

**Article 2235:** The depositor must indemnify the depositary for expenses made for conservation of the thing, and that they would probably have made themselves, having it in their power; as also for damages the deposit has caused them without their fault.

### § 2\. Of Necessary Deposit

**Article 2236:** Deposit properly called is called necessary when the choice of depositary does not depend on the depositor's free will, as in the case of a fire, ruin, pillage, or other similar calamity.

**Article 2237:** About necessary deposit, every kind of proof is admissible.

**Article 2238:** Necessary deposit undertaken by an adult who does not have the free administration of their property, but is in their sound reason, constitutes a quasi-contract that binds the depositary without their legal representative's authorization.

**Article 2239:** The depositary's responsibility extends to slight fault.

**Article 2240:** In other respects, necessary deposit is subject to the same rules as voluntary deposit.

**Article 2241:** Effects introduced by one lodging in an inn, delivered to the innkeeper or their dependents, are regarded as deposited under the innkeeper's custody. This deposit resembles necessary deposit and articles 2237 and following apply to it.

**Article 2242:** The innkeeper is responsible for any damage caused to said effects by their fault or that of their dependents, or of strangers visiting the inn, and even for thefts and robberies; but not for force majeure or fortuitous event, except if imputable to fault or fraud.

**Article 2243:** The innkeeper is additionally obliged to the security of effects the lodger keeps around themselves. In this respect, they are responsible for damage caused or theft or robbery committed by the inn's servants, or by strangers not being family or visitors of the lodger.

**Article 2244:** The lodger complaining of damage, theft, or robbery must prove the number, quality, and value of the disappeared effects.

The judge shall be authorized to reject testimonial proof offered by the plaintiff, when they do not inspire confidence or circumstances appear suspicious.

**Article 2245:** The traveler bringing effects of great value, not ordinarily entering into the luggage of persons of their class, must inform the innkeeper, and even show them if required, for special care to be employed in their custody; and if not, the judge may reject the claim in this part.

**Article 2246:** If the fact is in any way imputable to the lodger's negligence, the innkeeper shall be acquitted.

**Article 2247:** The innkeeper's responsibility shall also cease when it has been agreed to exempt them from it.

**Article 2248:** The provisions of the preceding articles apply to administrators of inns, cafes, billiard halls or baths, and other similar establishments.

### § 3\. Of Sequestration

**Article 2249:** Sequestration is the deposit of a thing disputed by two or more individuals, in the hands of another who must restore it to the one obtaining a decision in their favor.

The depositary is called sequestrator.

**Article 2250:** The rules of sequestration are the same as those of deposit properly called, excepting provisions expressed in the following articles and in the Code of Procedure.

**Article 2251:** Not only movable things but real property may be placed in sequestration.

**Article 2252:** Sequestration is conventional or judicial.

Conventional sequestration is constituted by the sole consent of the persons disputing the litigious object.

Judicial sequestration is constituted by judge's decree, and needs no other proof.

**Article 2253:** Depositors contract with the sequestrator the same obligations as the depositor with respect to the depositary in deposit properly called, as to expenses and damages caused by the sequestration.

**Article 2254:** Losing holding, the sequestrator may claim it against any person, even any of the depositors, who has taken it without the other's consent, or without judge's decree, as the case may be.

**Article 2255:** The sequestrator of an immovable has, relative to its administration, the faculties and duties of an agent, and must render account of their acts to the future adjudicatee.

**Article 2256:** As long as no adjudication judgment passed into res judicata is rendered, the sequestrator may not be discharged from their office except by an imperative need, of which they shall give notice to the depositors, if the sequestration is conventional, or to the judge in the contrary case, for their relief to be provided.

They may also cease, before said judgment, by unanimous will of the parties, if the sequestration is conventional, or by judge's decree in the contrary case.

**Article 2257:** Once said judgment is pronounced and final, the sequestrator must restore the deposit to the adjudicatee.

If the sequestration is judicial, the provisions of the Code of Procedure shall be observed in this part.

---

## TITLE XXXIII: OF ALEATORY CONTRACTS

**Article 2258:** The principal aleatory contracts are:

1. The contract of insurance;  
2. Bottomry loan;  
3. Gambling;  
4. Betting;  
5. Constitution of life annuity;  
6. Constitution of life ground rent.

The first two belong to the Commercial Code.

### § 1\. Of Gambling and Betting

**Article 2259:** On games of chance, the provisions of article 1466 shall be followed.

The following articles relate to lawful games and bets.

**Article 2260:** Gambling and betting produce no action, but only exception.

The winner cannot demand payment.

But if the loser pays, they cannot repeat what was paid, unless won with fraud.

**Article 2261:** There is fraud in the one making the bet, if they know for certain that the fact in question is to be or has been verified.

**Article 2262:** What is paid by persons who do not have the free administration of their property may be repeated in all cases by the one having parental authority, guardians, or curators.

**Article 2263:** Notwithstanding the provisions of article 2260, games of strength or bodily skill shall produce action, such as weapons, foot or horse racing, ball, bowls, and other similar, provided in them laws or police regulations are not contravened.

In case of contravention, the judge shall dismiss the claim entirely.

### § 2\. Of the Constitution of Life Annuity

**Article 2264:** Constitution of life annuity is an aleatory contract in which a person obliges themselves, for consideration, to pay another an income or periodic pension, during the natural life of either of these two persons or of a third party.

**Article 2265:** Life annuity may be constituted in favor of two or more persons enjoying it simultaneously, with or without right of accrual, or successively according to the agreed order, provided all exist at the time of the contract.

**Article 2266:** It may also be stipulated that the life annuity be owed during the natural life of several individuals, designated.

No person not existing at the time of the contract may be designated for this object.

**Article 2267:** The price of the life annuity, or what is paid for the right to receive it, may consist of money or real or movable things.

The pension may only be in money.

**Article 2268:** It is free for contractors to establish whatever pension they wish by way of life annuity. The law does not determine any proportion between the pension and the price.

**Article 2269:** The contract of life annuity must be executed by public deed, and shall not be perfected except by delivery of the price.

**Article 2270:** The contract is null if before perfection the person on whose existence the duration of the annuity depends dies, or at the time of the contract suffered from an illness that caused their death within thirty days following.

**Article 2271:** The creditor may not demand rescission of the contract even in case of non-payment of the pension, nor may the debtor demand it, even offering to restore the price and restore or remit accrued pensions, except if contractors have stipulated otherwise.

**Article 2272:** In case of non-payment of the pension, proceedings may be against the debtor's property for payment of arrears, and to oblige them to give security for future payment.

**Article 2273:** If the debtor does not provide stipulated securities, the creditor may demand the contract be annulled.

**Article 2274:** If the third party on whose existence the duration of the annuity depends survives the person who is to enjoy it, the latter's right is transmitted to those who succeed them by reason of death.

**Article 2275:** To demand payment of the life annuity, existence of the person on whose life it depends must be proved.

**Article 2276:** Upon the death of the person on whose existence the duration of the life annuity depends, the entire current year shall be owed if in the contract it was stipulated to be paid in advance, and in default of this stipulation, only the part corresponding to the number of days elapsed.

**Article 2277:** Life annuity is not extinguished by any prescription; except if it has ceased to be received and demanded for more than five continuous years.

**Article 2278:** When a life annuity is constituted gratuitously, there is no aleatory contract. It shall therefore be subject to the rules of donations and legacies, without prejudice to being governed by the preceding articles as applicable.

### § 3\. Of the Constitution of Life Ground Rent

**Article 2279:** Life annuity is called life ground rent when constituted over a given estate that is to pass with this burden to everyone who possesses it.

The rules of ordinary ground rent shall apply to life ground rent as applicable.

**Article 2280:** Life ground rent is irredeemable, and does not admit the division and reduction of which ordinary ground rent is susceptible.

**Article 2281:** Life ground rent may be constituted in favor of two or more persons enjoying it in the terms of article 2265; provided they exist at the time of the testator's death, or at the time of acceptance of the donation, or at the time of perfection of the contract, as applicable.

**Article 2282:** It may also be stipulated that the ground rent be owed during the life of several persons designated; ceasing with the last survivor.

Designation of any person not existing at the time of the testator's death, or of granting the donation, or of perfecting the contract, shall not be valid for this object.

**Article 2283:** Articles 2266, 2267, 2268, 2270, 2274, 2275, 2276, and 2278 apply to life ground rent.

---

## TITLE XXXIV: OF QUASI-CONTRACTS

**Article 2284:** Obligations contracted without agreement arise either from law, or from the voluntary act of one of the parties. Those arising from law are expressed in it.

If the act from which they arise is lawful, it constitutes a quasi-contract.

If the act is unlawful, and committed with intention to harm, it constitutes a crime.

If the act is culpable, but committed without intention to harm, it constitutes a quasi-crime.

This title deals only with quasi-contracts.

**Article 2285:** There are three principal quasi-contracts: officious agency, payment of what is not due, and community.

### § 1\. Of Officious Agency or Management of Another's Affairs

**Article 2286:** Officious agency or management of another's affairs, commonly called management of affairs, is a quasi-contract by which the one administering without mandate the affairs of some person obliges themselves to that person, and obliges the latter in certain cases.

**Article 2287:** The obligations of the officious agent or manager are the same as those of the agent.

**Article 2288:** They must consequently employ in the management the care of a good father of a family; but their responsibility may be greater or less by reason of circumstances determining them to the management.

If they have undertaken it to save another's interests from imminent danger, they are only responsible for fraud or gross fault; and if they have voluntarily taken the management, they are responsible even for slight fault; except if they have offered themselves, preventing others from doing so, in which case they shall be responsible for any fault.

**Article 2289:** They must likewise undertake all dependencies of the business, and continue in the management until the interested party can take it or entrust it to another.

If the interested party dies, they must continue in the management until the heirs dispose.

**Article 2290:** If the business has been well administered, the interested party shall fulfill obligations the manager has contracted in the management and reimburse them for useful or necessary expenses.

The interested party is not obliged to pay any salary to the manager.

If the business has been badly administered, the manager is responsible for damages.

**Article 2291:** The one administering another's business against the interested party's express prohibition has no claim against them except insofar as that management has been effectively useful to them, and the utility exists at the time of the claim; for example, if from the management the extinction of a debt has resulted, which without it the interested party would have had to pay.

The judge shall, however, in this case grant the interested party the period they ask for payment of the claim, and which by circumstances of the defendant appears equitable.

**Article 2292:** The one who, believing to do their own business, does another's, has the right to be reimbursed up to the amount of the effective utility that has resulted to said person, and existing at the time of the claim.

**Article 2293:** The one who, believing to do the business of one person, does that of another, has with respect to the latter the same rights and obligations they would have had if they had intended to serve the true interested party.

**Article 2294:** The manager may not bring any action against the interested party without a regular account of the management with supporting documents or equivalent proof.

### § 2\. Of Payment of What Is Not Due

**Article 2295:** If the one who by error has made a payment proves it was not owed, they have the right to repeat what was paid.

However, when a person by consequence of their error has paid another's debt, they shall not have the right of repetition against the one who by consequence of the payment has suppressed or cancelled a title necessary for collection of their credit; but they may bring against the debtor the creditor's actions.

**Article 2296:** What has been paid to fulfill a purely natural obligation enumerated in article 1470 may not be repeated.

**Article 2297:** Even what has been paid by error of law may be repeated, when the payment had as its foundation not even a purely natural obligation.

**Article 2298:** If the defendant confesses the payment, the plaintiff must prove it was not due.

If the defendant denies the payment, it is for the plaintiff to prove it; and once proved, it shall be presumed undue.

**Article 2299:** One who gives what they do not owe is not presumed to donate it, unless it is proved they had perfect knowledge of what they were doing, both in fact and in law.

**Article 2300:** One who has received money or fungible thing not owed is obliged to restore the same amount of the same kind and quality.

If received in bad faith, they must also pay current interest.

**Article 2301:** One who has received in good faith is not responsible for deteriorations or losses of the species given to them in the false concept of being owed, even if they have occurred by their negligence; except insofar as they have been made richer by them.

But from the time they know the thing was unduly paid, they contract all obligations of the possessor in bad faith.

**Article 2302:** One who in good faith has sold the species given to them as due, without being so, is only obliged to restore the price of the sale, and to assign actions they have against the buyer who has not paid them fully.

If they were in bad faith when making the sale, they are obliged as any possessor who has fraudulently ceased to possess.

**Article 2303:** The one who paid what they did not owe may not pursue the species possessed by a third party in good faith for consideration; but shall have the right for the third party holding it by any lucrative title to restore it, if the species is reclaimable and exists in their power.

The obligations of the donee who restores are the same as those of their author according to article 2301\.

### § 3\. Of the Quasi-Contract of Community

**Article 2304:** Community of a universal or singular thing, between two or more persons, without any of them having contracted partnership or celebrated another agreement relating to the same thing, is a kind of quasi-contract.

**Article 2305:** The right of each co-owner over the common thing is the same as that of partners in the partnership fund.

**Article 2306:** If the thing is universal, such as an inheritance, each co-owner is obliged to the debts of the common thing, as heirs in hereditary debts.

**Article 2307:** For debts contracted for the benefit of the community during it, only the co-owner who contracted them is obliged; who shall have action against the community for reimbursement of what they have paid for it.

If the debt has been contracted by the co-owners collectively, without expression of shares, all of them, not having stipulated solidarity, are obliged to the creditor in equal parts, saving each one's right against the others for reimbursement of what they have paid in excess over their share.

**Article 2308:** Each co-owner owes the community what they take from it, including current interest on common money employed in their particular affairs; and is responsible even for slight fault for damage caused in common things and affairs.

**Article 2309:** Each co-owner must contribute to works and repairs of the community proportionally to their share.

**Article 2310:** Fruits of the common thing must be divided among co-owners, proportionally to their shares.

**Article 2311:** In payments to which co-owners are obliged among themselves, the share of the insolvent shall burden the others.

**Article 2312:** Community ends:

1. By the reunion of the shares of all co-owners in a single person;  
2. By destruction of the common thing;  
3. By division of the common fund.

**Article 2313:** Division of common things and obligations and rights resulting from it shall be subject to the same rules as in the partition of inheritance.

---

## TITLE XXXV: OF CRIMES AND QUASI-CRIMES

**Article 2314:** The one who has committed a crime or quasi-crime causing damage to another is obliged to indemnification; without prejudice to the penalty the laws impose for the crime or quasi-crime.

**Article 2315:** This indemnity may be demanded not only by the owner or possessor of the thing suffering damage, or their heir, but by the usufructuary, the inhabitant, or the user, if the damage causes harm to their right of usufruct or habitation or use. It may also be demanded in other cases by the one having the thing with the obligation to be responsible for it; but only in the owner's absence.

**Article 2316:** The one who caused the damage, and their heirs, are obliged to indemnify.

The one receiving benefit from another's fraud, without being an accomplice in it, is only obliged up to the amount of the benefit.

**Article 2317:** If a crime or quasi-crime has been committed by two or more persons, each of them shall be jointly responsible for all damage proceeding from the same crime or quasi-crime, excepting exceptions of articles 2323 and 2328\.

Any fraud or fraud committed by two or more persons produces the joint action of the preceding paragraph.

**Article 2318:** The drunkard is responsible for damage caused by their crime or quasi-crime.

**Article 2319:** Children under seven years of age and the demented are not capable of crime or quasi-crime; but persons in whose charge they are shall be responsible for damage caused by them, if negligence can be imputed to them.

It is left to the judge's prudence to determine whether the minor under sixteen has committed the crime or quasi-crime without discernment; and in this case, the rule of the preceding paragraph shall apply.

**Article 2320:** Every person is responsible not only for their own actions, but for the act of those in their care.

Thus parents are responsible for the act of minor children living in the same house.

Thus the guardian or curator is responsible for the conduct of the ward living under their dependence and care.

Thus heads of schools and colleges are responsible for the act of pupils, while under their care; and artisans and entrepreneurs for the act of their apprentices or dependents, in the same case.

But the obligation of these persons shall cease if with the authority and care their respective quality confers and prescribes on them, they could not have prevented the act.

**Article 2321:** Parents shall always be responsible for crimes or quasi-crimes committed by their minor children, and notoriously arising from bad education, or from the vicious habits they have allowed them to acquire.

**Article 2322:** Masters shall be responsible for the conduct of their servants or employees, in the exercise of their respective functions; even if the act in question has not been executed in their view.

But they shall not be responsible for what their servants or employees have done in the exercise of their respective functions, if it is proved they have exercised them in an improper way that the masters had no means to foresee or prevent, employing ordinary care and competent authority. In this case, all responsibility shall fall on said servants or employees.

**Article 2323:** The owner of a building is responsible to third parties (not in the case of article 934\) for damage caused by its ruin occurring for having omitted necessary repairs, or for having failed in another way in the care of a good father of a family.

If the building belongs to two or more persons in common, the indemnity shall be divided among them proportionally to their shares of ownership.

**Article 2324:** If the damage caused by the ruin of a building arises from a construction defect, the responsibility prescribed in rule 3 of article 2003 shall apply.

**Article 2325:** Persons obliged to reparation of damage caused by those depending on them shall have the right to be indemnified over the property of the latter, if any, and if the one who perpetrated the damage did so without order of the person to whom they should have obedience, and was capable of crime or quasi-crime, according to article 2319\.

**Article 2326:** The owner of an animal is responsible for damage caused by the same animal, even after it has been released or strayed; except if the release, straying, or damage cannot be imputed to the owner's fault or the dependant charged with the animal's care or service.

What is said of the owner applies to any person using another's animal; saving their action against the owner, if the damage has occurred by a quality or vice of the animal, which the owner with medium care or prudence should have known or foreseen, and of which they did not give knowledge.

**Article 2327:** Damage caused by a fierce animal, from which no utility is derived for guarding or servicing an estate, shall always be imputable to the one having it, and if they allege it was not possible to avoid the damage, they shall not be heard.

**Article 2328:** Damage caused by a thing falling or thrown from the upper part of a building is imputable to all persons inhabiting the same part of the building, and the indemnity shall be divided among all of them; unless it is proved that the act is due exclusively to the fault or bad intention of some person, in which case this one alone shall be responsible.

If there is any thing that, from the upper part of a building or other elevated place, threatens fall and damage, the owner of the building or site, or their tenant, or the person to whom the thing belongs or who uses it, may be obliged to remove it; and any member of the community shall have the right to request removal.

**Article 2329:** As a general rule, any damage imputable to malice or negligence of another person must be repaired by them.

Especially obliged to this reparation are:

1. The one who imprudently fires a firearm;  
2. The one who removes the stones of a ditch or pipe in a street or road, without necessary precautions for those traveling by day or night not to fall;  
3. The one who, obliged to construction or repair of an aqueduct or bridge crossing a road, has it in a state to cause damage to those traveling on it.

**Article 2330:** Assessment of damage is subject to reduction, if the one suffering it imprudently exposed themselves to it.

**Article 2331:** Injurious imputations against a person's honor or credit do not give the right to demand pecuniary indemnity, unless actual loss or lost profit appreciable in money is proved; but not even then shall pecuniary indemnity apply if the truth of the imputation is proved.

**Article 2332:** Actions granted by this title for damage or fraud prescribe in four years from perpetration of the act.

**Article 2333:** As a general rule, popular action is granted in all cases of contingent damage that by imprudence or negligence of someone threatens undetermined persons; but if the damage threatens only determined persons, only one of them may bring the action.

**Article 2334:** If popular actions to which the preceding articles give right appear well-founded, the actor shall be indemnified for all costs of the action, and shall be paid what the time and diligence employed are worth, without prejudice to the specific remuneration the law grants in determined cases.

---

## TITLE XXXVI: OF SURETYSHIP

### § 1\. Of the Constitution and Requirements of Suretyship

**Article 2335:** Suretyship is an accessory obligation, by virtue of which one or more persons are answerable for another's obligation, undertaking to the creditor to fulfill it in whole or in part, if the principal debtor does not fulfill it.

Suretyship may be constituted not only in favor of the principal debtor, but of another surety.

**Article 2336:** Suretyship may be conventional, legal, or judicial.

The first is constituted by contract, the second is ordered by law, the third by judge's decree.

Legal and judicial suretyship are subject to the same rules as conventional suretyship, except insofar as the law requiring it or the Code of Procedure provides otherwise.

**Article 2337:** The person obliged to provide a surety may not substitute a mortgage or pledge for it, or vice versa, against the creditor's will.

If the surety is required by law or judge's decree, a sufficient pledge or mortgage may be substituted for it.

**Article 2338:** The obligation to which the surety is accessory may be civil or natural.

**Article 2339:** Not only a pure and simple obligation may be guaranteed, but a conditional one and at term. A future obligation may also be guaranteed; and in this case, the surety may withdraw as long as the principal obligation does not exist; remaining, however, responsible to the creditor and third parties in good faith, as the principal in the case of article 2173\.

**Article 2340:** Suretyship may be granted until or from a certain day, or under suspensive or resolutory condition.

**Article 2341:** The surety may stipulate with the debtor a pecuniary remuneration for the service rendered.

**Article 2342:** Persons under parental authority or under guardianship or curatorship may only oblige themselves as sureties in accordance with the provisions of the titles On parental authority and On administration of guardians and curators. If the husband or wife, married under the regime of conjugal partnership, wish to oblige themselves as sureties, the rules given in the title On conjugal partnership shall be observed.

**Article 2343:** The surety may not oblige themselves to more than the principal debtor owes, but may oblige themselves to less.

They may oblige themselves to pay a sum of money in place of another thing of equal or greater value.

Guaranteeing another's act, only the indemnity into which the act by its non-execution is resolved is guaranteed.

The obligation to pay a thing other than money in place of another thing or of a sum of money does not constitute suretyship.

**Article 2344:** The surety may not oblige themselves in terms more burdensome than the principal debtor, not only with respect to amount but to time, place, condition, or mode of payment, or to the penalty imposed for non-execution of the contract to which the surety accedes; but they may oblige themselves in less burdensome terms.

They may, however, oblige themselves in a more effective way, for example, with a mortgage, even if the principal obligation does not have it.

The surety that exceeds under any of the respects indicated in paragraph 1 shall be reduced to the terms of the principal obligation.

In case of doubt, the interpretation most favorable to conformity of the two obligations, principal and accessory, shall be adopted.

**Article 2345:** One may guarantee without order and even without notice and against the principal debtor's will.

**Article 2346:** A juridical person and the vacant estate may be guaranteed.

**Article 2347:** Suretyship is not presumed, nor must it extend beyond the tenor of what is expressed; but it is supposed to include all accessories of the debt, such as interest, court costs of the first demand made to the principal debtor, those of the intimation made consequently to the surety, and all subsequent to this intimation; but not those caused in the intervening time between the first demand and the said intimation.

**Article 2348:** The following are obliged to provide surety at the creditor's request:

1. The debtor who has stipulated it;  
2. The debtor whose means diminish to the point of putting manifestly in danger the fulfillment of their obligation;  
3. The debtor of whom there is reason to fear they will leave the State's territory with the intention of settling elsewhere, as long as they do not leave sufficient property for the security of their obligations.

**Article 2349:** Whenever the surety given by the debtor falls into insolvency, the debtor shall be obliged to give new surety.

**Article 2350:** The person obliged to provide surety must give a surety capable of obliging themselves as such; having more than sufficient property to make it effective, and being domiciled or choosing domicile within the jurisdiction of the respective Court of Appeals.

To qualify sufficiency of property, only immovable property shall be taken into account, except in commercial matters or when the guaranteed debt is modest.

But attached or litigious immovable property, or not existing in the State's territory, or subject to burdensome mortgages or resolutory conditions, shall not be taken into account.

If the surety is burdened with debts that put even non-mortgaged immovable property in danger, these shall not be counted either.

**Article 2351:** The surety is responsible even for slight fault in all payments to which they are obliged.

**Article 2352:** The rights and obligations of sureties are transmissible to their heirs.

### § 2\. Of the Effects of Suretyship Between the Creditor and the Surety

**Article 2353:** The surety may make payment of the debt, even before being sued by the creditor, in all cases where the principal debtor could do so.

**Article 2354:** The surety may oppose to the creditor any real exceptions, such as fraud, violence, or res judicata; but not the debtor's personal exceptions, such as their incapacity to oblige themselves, assignment of property, or the right not to be deprived of what is necessary for subsistence.

Real exceptions are those inherent in the principal obligation.

**Article 2355:** When the creditor has placed the surety in the case of not being able to subrogate themselves in their actions against the principal debtor or against the other sureties, the surety shall have the right for what said surety could have obtained from the principal debtor or from the other sureties by means of legal subrogation to be deducted from the creditor's claim.

**Article 2356:** Even if the surety is not sued, they may require the creditor, from the time the debt is demandable, to proceed against the principal debtor; and if the creditor after this requirement delays it, the surety shall not be responsible for insolvency of the principal debtor occurring during the delay.

**Article 2357:** The sued surety enjoys the benefit of excussion, by virtue of which they may require that before proceeding against them, the debt be pursued in the property of the principal debtor, and in mortgages or pledges provided by the latter for security of the same debt.

**Article 2358:** To enjoy the benefit of excussion, the following conditions are necessary:

1. That it has not been expressly renounced;  
2. That the surety has not obliged themselves as joint co-debtor;  
3. That the principal obligation produces action;  
4. That the surety has not been ordered by the judge;  
5. That the benefit be opposed as soon as the surety is required; except if the debtor at the time of the requirement has no property and later acquires it;  
6. That the debtor's property be indicated to the creditor.

**Article 2359:** The following shall not be taken into account for excussion:

1. Property existing outside the State's territory;  
2. Attached or litigious property, or credits of doubtful or difficult collection;  
3. Property whose ownership is subject to a resolutory condition;  
4. Those mortgaged in favor of preferred debts, in the part appearing necessary for their complete payment.

**Article 2360:** By renunciation of the principal surety, the sub-surety shall not be understood to renounce.

**Article 2361:** The creditor shall have the right for the surety to advance the costs of excussion.

The judge, if necessary, shall fix the amount of the advance, and appoint the person in whose power it is consigned, who may be the creditor himself.

If the surety prefers to make the excussion themselves, within a reasonable period, they shall be heard.

**Article 2362:** When several principal debtors have obliged themselves jointly and one of them has given surety, the sued surety shall have the right for not only this debtor's property but their co-debtors' to be excussed.

**Article 2363:** The benefit of excussion may not be opposed except once.

If excussion of property designated once by the surety produces no effect or is insufficient, they may not designate others; except if subsequently acquired by the principal debtor.

**Article 2364:** If the excussed property produces no more than a partial payment of the debt, the creditor shall nevertheless be obliged to accept it and may not sue the surety except for the unpaid part.

**Article 2365:** If the creditor is remiss or negligent in the excussion, and the debtor falls into insolvency in the meantime, the surety shall only be responsible insofar as it exceeds the value of the property designated for excussion.

If the surety, expressly and unequivocally, has not obliged themselves to pay except what the creditor could not obtain from the debtor, the creditor shall be understood to be obliged to the excussion, and the surety shall not be responsible for the debtor's insolvency, the following circumstances concurring:

1. That the creditor had sufficient means to be paid;  
2. That they were negligent in using them.

**Article 2366:** The sub-surety enjoys the benefit of excussion, both with respect to the surety and the principal debtor.

**Article 2367:** If there are two or more sureties of the same debt, not having obliged themselves jointly to payment, the debt shall be understood divided among them in equal parts, and the creditor may not demand from any except the share falling to them.

The insolvency of one surety shall burden the others; but the one whose sub-surety is not insolvent shall not be regarded as insolvent.

The surety who unequivocally has limited their responsibility to a determined sum or share shall be responsible only up to said sum or share.

**Article 2368:** The division provided in the preceding article shall apply among sureties of the same debtor and for the same debt, even if the sureties have been given separately.

### § 3\. Of the Effects of Suretyship Between the Surety and the Debtor

**Article 2369:** The surety shall have the right for the principal debtor to obtain their release or provide security for the results of the suretyship, or consign means of payment, in the following cases:

1. When the principal debtor wastes or recklessly ventures their property;  
2. When the principal debtor obliged themselves to obtain their release from the suretyship within a certain period, and this period has expired;  
3. When the term has expired or the condition fulfilled that makes the principal obligation immediately demandable in whole or in part;  
4. If five years have elapsed since execution of the suretyship; unless the principal obligation was contracted for a longer determined time, or is one not subject to being extinguished in a determined time, such as that of guardians and curators, that of the usufructuary, that of life annuity, that of employees in collection or administration of public revenues;  
5. If there is well-founded fear that the principal debtor will flee, not leaving sufficient real property for payment of the debt.

The rights here granted to the surety do not extend to the one who guaranteed against the debtor's will.

**Article 2370:** The surety shall have action against the principal debtor for reimbursement of what they have paid for them with interest and expenses, even if the suretyship was unknown to the debtor.

They shall also have the right to damages according to general rules.

But they may not claim reimbursement of inconsiderate expenses, nor of those suffered before notifying the principal debtor of the claim brought against said surety.

**Article 2371:** When the suretyship has been granted at the request of a third party, the surety who has paid shall have action against the mandant; without prejudice to that belonging against the principal debtor.

**Article 2372:** If there are many principal and joint debtors, the one who has guaranteed all of them may demand from each the total of the debt, in the terms of article 2370; but the particular surety of one of them may only claim the whole against them; and shall have against the others only the actions corresponding to them as subrogated in the rights of the debtor they have guaranteed.

**Article 2373:** The surety who paid before the term of the principal obligation expired may not sue the debtor except after the term expires.

**Article 2374:** The surety to whom the creditor has remitted the debt in whole or in part may not claim against the debtor for the remitted amount, unless the creditor has assigned their action for the purpose.

**Article 2375:** The actions granted by article 2370 shall not apply in the following cases:

1. When the principal debtor's obligation is purely natural, and has not been validated by ratification or by lapse of time;  
2. When the surety obliged themselves against the principal debtor's will; except insofar as the debt has been extinguished, and without prejudice to the surety's right to claim against whomever according to general rules;  
3. When because the surety's payment was not valid, the debt has not been extinguished.

**Article 2376:** The debtor who paid without notifying the surety shall be responsible to the surety for what, ignoring the extinction of the debt, they pay anew; but shall have action against the creditor for undue payment.

**Article 2377:** If the surety paid without having notified the debtor, the debtor may oppose to them all exceptions the debtor could have used against the creditor at the time of payment.

If the debtor, ignoring by lack of notice the extinction of the debt, pays it anew, the surety shall have no recourse against them, but may bring against the creditor the debtor's action for undue payment.

### § 4\. Of the Effects of Suretyship Between Co-Sureties

**Article 2378:** The surety who pays more than proportionally corresponds to them is subrogated for the excess in the creditor's rights against the co-sureties.

**Article 2379:** Co-sureties may not oppose to the one who has paid the purely personal exceptions of the principal debtor.

Nor may they oppose to the paying co-surety the purely personal exceptions that corresponded to the latter against the creditor and of which they did not wish to avail themselves.

**Article 2380:** The sub-surety, in case of insolvency of the surety for whom they obliged themselves, is responsible for the latter's obligations to the other sureties.

### § 5\. Of the Extinction of Suretyship

**Article 2381:** Suretyship is extinguished, in whole or in part, by the same means as other obligations according to general rules, and in addition:

1. By release of the suretyship in whole or in part, granted by the creditor to the surety;  
2. Insofar as the creditor by their act or fault has lost the actions in which the surety had the right to subrogate themselves;  
3. By extinction of the principal obligation in whole or in part.

**Article 2382:** If the creditor voluntarily accepts from the principal debtor in discharge of the debt an object different from what this debtor was obliged to give them in payment, the suretyship is irrevocably extinguished, even if eviction of the object later occurs.

**Article 2383:** Suretyship is extinguished by confusion of the qualities of creditor and surety, or of debtor and surety; but in this second case, the sub-surety's obligation shall subsist.

---

## TITLE XXXVII: OF THE CONTRACT OF PLEDGE

**Article 2384:** By the contract of pawn or pledge, a movable thing is delivered to a creditor for the security of their credit.

The thing delivered is called a pledge.

The creditor holding it is called a pledge creditor.

**Article 2385:** The contract of pledge always supposes a principal obligation to which it is accessory.

**Article 2386:** This contract is not perfected except by delivery of the pledge to the creditor.

**Article 2387:** A thing may not be pledged except by a person having the faculty to alienate it.

**Article 2388:** Pledge may be constituted not only by the debtor but by any third party who does this service for the debtor.

**Article 2389:** A credit may be given in pledge by delivering the title; but it shall be necessary for the creditor to notify the debtor of the credit recorded in the title, prohibiting them from paying it in other hands.

**Article 2390:** If the pledge does not belong to the one constituting it, but to a third party who has not consented to the pledge, the contract nevertheless subsists as long as the owner does not claim it; unless the creditor knows it has been stolen, or taken by force, or lost, in which case the provisions of article 2183 shall apply to the pledge.

**Article 2391:** If the owner claims the pledged thing without their consent, and restitution is verified, the creditor may demand that another pledge of equal or greater value be delivered, or other competent security granted, and in default of either, the principal obligation immediately fulfilled, even if a term for payment is pending.

**Article 2392:** Nothing may be taken from the debtor against their will to serve as pledge, except by the ministry of the court.

A thing of the debtor may not be retained in security of the debt without their consent; except in cases the laws expressly designate.

**Article 2393:** If the creditor loses holding of the pledge, they shall have action to recover it against any person in whose power it is found, without excepting the debtor who constituted it.

But the debtor may retain the pledge by paying the totality of the debt for whose security it was constituted.

Upon this payment, the creditor may not claim it, alleging other credits, even if they have the requirements enumerated in article 2401\.

**Article 2394:** The creditor is obliged to guard and preserve the pledge as a good father of a family, and is responsible for deteriorations the pledge has suffered by their act or fault.

**Article 2395:** The creditor may not use the pledge without the debtor's consent. In this respect, their obligations are the same as those of the mere depositary.

**Article 2396:** The debtor may not claim restitution of the pledge in whole or in part, as long as they have not paid the totality of the debt in principal and interest, necessary expenses incurred by the creditor for preservation of the pledge, and damages caused by holding it.

However, if the debtor requests permission to replace the pledge with another without prejudice to the creditor, they shall be heard.

And if the creditor abuses it, they shall lose their right of pledge, and the debtor may demand immediate restitution of the pledged thing.

**Article 2397:** The pledge creditor shall have the right to request that the pledge of the defaulting debtor be sold at public auction for payment with the proceeds; or that, in default of an admissible bid, it be appraised by experts and adjudicated to them in payment, up to the amount of their credit; without any stipulation to the contrary being valid, and without prejudice to their right to pursue the principal obligation by other means.

Nor may it be stipulated that the creditor have the faculty to dispose of the pledge or appropriate it by other means than those here indicated.

**Article 2398:** The creditor and debtor may be admitted to the bidding of the auctioned pledge.

**Article 2399:** As long as the sale or adjudication provided in article 2397 has not been consummated, the debtor may pay the debt, provided payment is complete and includes expenses the sale or adjudication has already caused.

**Article 2400:** Repealed.

**Article 2401:** Once the credit is satisfied in all its parts, the pledge must be restored.

But the creditor may retain it if they have against the same debtor other credits, provided they have the following requirements:

1. That they be certain and liquid;  
2. That they were contracted after the obligation for which the pledge was constituted;  
3. That they became demandable before payment of the previous obligation.

**Article 2402:** If the price of the sold or adjudicated pledge does not cover the totality of the debt, it shall be imputed first to interest and costs; and if the pledge was constituted for security of two or more obligations, or, constituted in favor of a single one, was later extended to others, according to the preceding article, imputation shall be made in accordance with the rules given in the title On the modes of extinguishing obligations, § On imputation of payment.

**Article 2403:** The creditor is obliged to restore the pledge with increases received from nature or time. If the pledge has given fruits, they may impute them to payment of the debt, accounting for them and answering for the surplus.

**Article 2404:** If the debtor sells the pledged thing, the buyer shall have the right to request delivery from the creditor, paying and consigning the amount of the debt for which the pledge was expressly contracted.

The same right is granted to the person to whom the debtor has conferred an onerous title for the enjoyment or holding of the pledge.

In none of these cases may the first creditor excuse themselves from restitution, alleging other credits, even with the requirements enumerated in article 2401\.

**Article 2405:** Pledge is indivisible. Consequently, the heir who has paid their share of the debt may not request restitution of a part of the pledge, as long as any part of the debt exists; and reciprocally, the heir who has received their share of the credit may not remit the pledge, even in part, as long as their co-heirs have not been paid.

**Article 2406:** The right of pledge is extinguished by complete destruction of the pledged thing.

It is also extinguished when ownership of the pledged thing passes to the creditor by any title.

And when by virtue of a resolutory condition ownership that the one who gave the thing in pledge had over it is lost; but the creditor in good faith shall have against the debtor who did not inform them of the condition the same right as in the case of article 2391\.

---

## TITLE XXXVIII: OF MORTGAGE

**Article 2407:** Mortgage is a right of pledge, constituted over immovable property that nevertheless remains in the debtor's power.

**Article 2408:** Mortgage is indivisible.

Consequently, each of the things mortgaged to a debt and each part of them are obliged to payment of the entire debt and of each part of it.

**Article 2409:** Mortgage must be granted by public deed.

The same public deed may be that of the mortgage and that of the contract to which it is accessory.

**Article 2410:** Mortgage must also be registered in the Registrar's Registry; without this requirement it shall have no value; nor shall its date be counted except from registration.

**Article 2411:** Mortgage contracts celebrated in a foreign country shall give mortgage over property situated in Chile, provided they are registered in the competent Registry.

**Article 2412:** If the constitution of the mortgage suffers from relative nullity, and is later validated by lapse of time or ratification, the date of the mortgage shall always be the date of registration.

**Article 2413:** Mortgage may be granted under any condition, and from or until a certain day.

Granted under suspensive condition or from a certain day, it shall not be valid except from the time the condition is fulfilled or the day arrives; but once the condition is fulfilled or the day arrives, its date shall be the same as registration.

It may likewise be granted at any time before or after the contracts to which it accedes, and shall run from the time it is registered.

**Article 2414:** Only the person capable of alienating their property, and with the requirements necessary for its alienation, may constitute a mortgage over it.

One's own property may be mortgaged for security of another's obligation; but there shall be no personal action against the owner if they have not expressly submitted to it.

**Article 2415:** The owner of property encumbered with mortgage may always alienate or mortgage it, notwithstanding any stipulation to the contrary.

**Article 2416:** The one who has only an eventual, limited, or rescindible right over the mortgaged thing is not understood to mortgage it except with the conditions and limitations to which the right is subject; even if not so expressed.

If the right is subject to a resolutory condition, the provisions of article 1491 shall apply.

**Article 2417:** The co-owner may, before division of the common thing, mortgage their share; but once division is verified, the mortgage shall only affect property adjudicated by reason of said share, if mortgageable. If not, the mortgage shall lapse.

However, the mortgage may subsist over property adjudicated to the other participants, if they consent to it, and so appears in a public deed, of which note is taken in the margin of the mortgage registration.

**Article 2418:** Mortgage may only take place over real property possessed in ownership or usufruct, or over ships.

Particular rules relating to mortgage of ships belong to the Commercial Code.

**Article 2419:** Mortgage of future property only gives the creditor the right to have it registered over immovable property the debtor later acquires and as they acquire them.

**Article 2420:** Mortgage constituted over real property affects movable property that by accession to them are deemed immovable according to article 570, but ceases to affect them from the time they belong to third parties.

**Article 2421:** Mortgage extends to all increases and improvements the mortgaged thing receives.

**Article 2422:** Mortgage also extends to pensions accrued by lease of the mortgaged property, and to indemnity owed by insurers of the same property.

**Article 2423:** Mortgage over a usufruct or over mines and quarries does not extend to fruits perceived, nor to mineral substances once separated from the ground.

**Article 2424:** The mortgage creditor has, to be paid over the mortgaged things, the same rights as the pledge creditor over the pledge.

**Article 2425:** Exercise of the mortgage action does not prejudice the creditor's personal action to be paid over the debtor's property not mortgaged; but the former does not communicate to the latter the right of preference corresponding to the former.

**Article 2426:** The owner of the estate pursued by the mortgage creditor may abandon it to them, and as long as adjudication has not been consummated, may also recover it by paying the amount to which the estate is obliged, and in addition costs and expenses this abandonment has caused the creditor.

**Article 2427:** If the estate is lost or deteriorated so as not to be sufficient for security of the debt, the creditor shall have the right for the mortgage to be improved, unless they consent to other equivalent security being given; and in default of both, may demand immediate payment of the liquid debt, even if a term is pending, or request the conservatory measures the case admits, if the debt is liquid, conditional, or indeterminate.

**Article 2428:** Mortgage gives the creditor the right to pursue the mortgaged estate, whoever possesses it and by whatever title acquired.

However, this provision shall not apply against the third party who has acquired the mortgaged estate at public auction ordered by the judge.

But for this exception to take effect in favor of the third party, the auction must be made with personal summons, within the summons period, of creditors having mortgages constituted over the same estate; who shall be paid from the sale price in the corresponding order.

The judge shall in the meantime consign the money.

**Article 2429:** The third-party possessor sued for payment of the mortgage constituted over the estate that later passed to their hands with this encumbrance shall not have the right for the personally obliged debtors to be pursued first.

Making payment, they are subrogated in the creditor's rights in the same terms as the surety.

If they are dispossessed of the estate or abandon it, they shall be fully indemnified by the debtor, including improvements made on it.

**Article 2430:** One who mortgages their immovable for another's debt shall not be understood to be personally obliged, if not stipulated.

Whether personally obliged or not, the provision of the preceding article shall apply to them.

Suretyship is called mortgage suretyship when the surety obliges themselves with mortgage.

Mortgage suretyship is subject to the rules of simple suretyship as to personal action.

**Article 2431:** Mortgage may be limited to a determined sum, provided so expressed unequivocally; but it shall not in any case extend to more than double the known or presumed amount of the principal obligation, even if so stipulated.

The debtor shall have the right for the mortgage to be reduced to said amount; and once reduced, a new registration shall be made at their cost, by virtue of which the first shall not be valid except up to the amount fixed in the second.

**Article 2432:** The registration of the mortgage shall contain:

1. The name, surname, and domicile of the creditor, and their profession, if any, and the same designations with respect to the debtor, and to those who as attorneys or legal representatives of either require the registration. Juridical persons shall be designated by their legal or popular denomination, and by the place of their establishment; and what is said of attorneys or legal representatives in the preceding paragraph shall extend to their representatives.  
2. The date and nature of the contract to which the mortgage accedes, and the archive where it is found. If the mortgage has been constituted by separate act, the date of this act and the archive where it exists shall also be expressed.  
3. The situation of the mortgaged estate and its boundaries. If the mortgaged estate is rural, the province and commune to which it belongs shall be expressed, and if it belongs to several, all of them.  
4. The determined sum to which the mortgage extends in the case of the preceding article.  
5. The date of the registration and the Registrar's signature.

**Article 2433:** The registration shall not be annulled for lack of any of the designations provided under numbers 1, 2, 3, and 4 of the preceding article, provided that by means of it or of the contract or contracts cited in it, what is missing in the registration can be known.

**Article 2434:** Mortgage is extinguished together with the principal obligation.

It is also extinguished by resolution of the right of the one who constituted it, or by the event of the resolutory condition, according to legal rules.

It is also extinguished by the arrival of the day up to which it was constituted.

And by the cancellation granted by the creditor by public deed, of which note is taken in the margin of the respective registration.

---

## TITLE XXXIX: OF ANTI-CHRESIS

**Article 2435:** Anti-chresis is a contract by which a real thing is delivered to the creditor to be paid with its fruits.

**Article 2436:** The real thing may belong to the debtor, or to a third party consenting to the anti-chresis.

**Article 2437:** The contract of anti-chresis is perfected by tradition of the immovable.

**Article 2438:** Anti-chresis does not give the creditor, by itself, any real right over the delivered thing.

The provisions in favor of the lessee in the case of article 1962 apply to the anti-chretic creditor.

Anti-chresis shall not be valid to the prejudice of real rights nor of leases previously constituted over the estate.

**Article 2439:** The immovable previously mortgaged to the same creditor may be given to the creditor in anti-chresis; and the immovable given in anti-chresis may likewise be mortgaged to the creditor, with legal formalities and effects.

**Article 2440:** The creditor having anti-chresis enjoys the same rights as the lessee for allowance of improvements, damages, and expenses, and is subject to the same obligations as the lessee relative to conservation of the thing.

**Article 2441:** The creditor does not become owner of the immovable for non-payment; nor shall they have preference over other creditors except that given by the accessory mortgage contract, if any. Any contrary stipulation is null.

**Article 2442:** If the credit produces interest, the creditor shall have the right for imputation of fruits to be made first to them.

**Article 2443:** The parties may stipulate that fruits be compensated with interest, in their totality, or up to the concurrence of values.

Interest stipulated shall be subject in the case of enormous injury to the same reduction as in the case of loan for consumption.

**Article 2444:** The debtor may not request restitution of the thing given in anti-chresis except after total extinction of the debt; but the creditor may restore it at any time and pursue payment of their credit by other legal means; without prejudice to what has been stipulated to the contrary.

**Article 2445:** Regarding judicial anti-chresis or praetorian pledge, the provisions of the Code of Procedure shall apply.

---

## TITLE XL: OF TRANSACTION

**Article 2446:** Transaction is a contract in which the parties terminate extrajudicially a pending litigation, or prevent an eventual litigation.

An act consisting only in renunciation of a right not disputed is not a transaction.

**Article 2447:** Only the person capable of disposing of the objects included in the transaction may transact.

**Article 2448:** Every agent shall need special power to transact. In this power, the property, rights, and actions on which they wish to transact shall be specified.

**Article 2449:** Transaction may concern the civil action arising from a crime; but without prejudice to the criminal action.

**Article 2450:** One may not transact on a person's civil status.

**Article 2451:** Transaction on future maintenance of persons to whom it is owed by law shall not be valid without judicial approval; nor may the judge approve it if it contravenes the provisions of articles 334 and 335\.

**Article 2452:** Transaction on another's rights or on non-existent rights is not valid.

**Article 2453:** Transaction obtained by false titles, and in general by fraud or violence, is null in all its parts.

**Article 2454:** Transaction celebrated in consideration of a null title is null in all its parts, unless the parties have expressly treated on the nullity of the title.

**Article 2455:** Transaction is likewise null if, at the time of celebration, the litigation was already terminated by judgment passed into res judicata, and the parties or some of them did not have knowledge of it at the time of transacting.

**Article 2456:** Transaction is presumed to have been accepted out of consideration for the person with whom one transacts.

If therefore one believes they are transacting with one person and transacts with another, the transaction may be rescinded.

In the same way, if one transacts with the apparent possessor of a right, this transaction may not be alleged against the person to whom the right truly belongs.

**Article 2457:** Error about the identity of the object on which one wishes to transact annuls the transaction.

**Article 2458:** Calculation error does not annul the transaction, only gives the right to rectify the calculation.

**Article 2459:** If it appears by authentic titles that one of the parties had no right to the object on which they transacted, and these titles at the time of transaction were unknown to the party whose rights they favor, the transaction may be rescinded; except if it has not concerned a particular object, but the entire controversy between the parties, there being several objects of disagreement between them.

In this case, later discovery of unknown titles shall not be cause for rescission, except insofar as they were lost or fraudulently concealed by the adverse party.

If the fraud is only relative to one of the objects on which transaction was made, the injured party may demand restitution of their right over said object.

**Article 2460:** Transaction produces the effect of res judicata in the last instance; but declaration of nullity or rescission may be requested in accordance with the preceding articles.

**Article 2461:** Transaction produces no effect except between the contractors.

If there are many principal interested parties in the business on which one transacts, the transaction consented to by one of them does not harm nor benefit the others; excepting, however, the effects of novation in case of solidarity.

**Article 2462:** If the transaction concerns one or more specific objects, the general renunciation of all rights, actions, or claims shall only be understood of the rights, actions, or claims relating to the object or objects on which one transacts.

**Article 2463:** If a penalty has been stipulated against the one failing to execute the transaction, the penalty shall apply, without prejudice to the transaction being carried out in all its parts.

**Article 2464:** If one of the parties has renounced the right corresponding to them by a title and later acquires another title over the same object, the transaction does not deprive them of the right later acquired.

---

## TITLE XLI: OF THE PREFERENCE OF CREDITS

**Article 2465:** Every personal obligation gives the creditor the right to pursue its execution over all the debtor's real or movable property, present or future, excepting only non-attachable ones designated in article 1618\.

**Article 2466:** Over identifiable species belonging to other persons by reason of ownership, and existing in the insolvent debtor's power, the respective owners shall retain their rights, without prejudice to real rights belonging to the debtor over them, such as usufructuary or pledge creditor, or the right of retention granted by laws; in all of which creditors may subrogate themselves.

They may also subrogate themselves in the debtor's rights as lessor or lessee, according to the provisions of articles 1965 and 1968\.

However, the husband's usufruct over the wife's property, nor the father's or mother's over the child's property subject to parental authority, nor the real rights of use or habitation shall not be attachable.

**Article 2467:** All acts executed by the debtor relative to property of which they have made assignment, or for which a meeting of creditors has been opened, are null.

**Article 2468:** As to acts executed before the assignment of property or opening of the meeting, the following provisions shall be observed:

1. Creditors shall have the right for onerous contracts, and mortgages, pledges, and anti-chreses that the debtor has granted to their prejudice to be rescinded, if the grantor and acquirer are in bad faith, that is, both knowing the bad state of the former's affairs.  
2. Acts and contracts not included under the preceding number, including remissions and pacts of liberation gratuitously, shall be rescindible, upon proof of the debtor's bad faith and prejudice to creditors.  
3. Actions granted in this article to creditors expire in one year from the date of the act or contract.

**Article 2469:** Creditors, with the exceptions indicated in article 1618, may demand that all the debtor's property be sold up to the amount of their credits, including interest and costs of collection, for them to be fully satisfied with the proceeds if the property is sufficient, and in case of insufficiency, proportionally, when there are no special causes to prefer certain credits, according to the following classification.

**Article 2470:** The causes of preference are only privilege and mortgage.

These causes of preference are inherent in the credits for whose security they have been established, and pass with them to all persons acquiring them by assignment, subrogation, or otherwise.

**Article 2471:** Credits of the 1st, 2nd, and 4th class enjoy privilege.

**Article 2472:** The first class of credits comprises those arising from the causes enumerated below:

1. Court costs incurred in the general interest of creditors;  
2. Necessary funeral expenses of the deceased debtor;  
3. Expenses of the debtor's illness. If the illness has lasted more than six months, the judge shall fix, according to circumstances, the amount up to which the preference extends;  
4. Expenses incurred to put the debtor's property at the disposal of the mass, expenses of administration of the liquidation proceeding, of realization of assets, and loans contracted by the liquidator for the mentioned effects.  
5. Workers' remunerations, family allowances, the indemnity established in number 2 of article 163 bis of the Labor Code with a limit of ninety development units at the value corresponding to the last day of the month prior to payment, the excess being considered unsecured if any, contributions owed to social security institutions or collected through them for that purpose, and maintenance owed by law to certain persons in accordance with the rules provided in Title XVIII of Book I, with a limit of one hundred twenty development units at the value corresponding to the last day of the month prior to payment, the excess being considered unsecured if any;  
6. Credits of the treasury against pension fund administrators for contributions the treasury has made in accordance with paragraph 4 of article 42 of Decree Law No. 3,500 of 1980;  
7. Necessary articles of subsistence supplied to the debtor and their family during the last three months;  
8. Legal and conventional indemnities of labor origin corresponding to workers, accrued on the date they are claimed and up to a limit of three monthly minimum remunerational incomes for each year of service and fraction exceeding six months for each worker, with a limit of eleven years. For the excess, if any, they shall be considered unsecured. Likewise, the indemnity established in paragraph 2 of number 4 of article 163 bis of the Labor Code shall be subject to the same limits previously indicated. For purposes of calculating payment of the preference established in this number, the maximum limits indicated in the first and second paragraphs shall be determined independently;  
9. Credits of the treasury for withholding and surcharge taxes.

**Article 2473:** Credits enumerated in the preceding article affect all the debtor's property; and there not being enough to cover them entirely, they shall prefer one another in the order of their numbering, whatever their date, and those included in each number shall concur proportionally.

Credits enumerated in the preceding article shall not in any case pass against third-party possessors.

**Article 2474:** To the second class of credits belong those of the following persons:

1. The innkeeper over effects of the debtor introduced by them into the inn, as long as they remain in it and up to what is owed for lodging, expenses, and damages.  
2. The carrier or transport entrepreneur over carried effects, in their power or in that of their agents or dependents, up to what is owed for carriage, expenses, and damages; provided said effects are the debtor's property. Effects introduced by the debtor into the inn, or carried on their account, are presumed to be the debtor's property.  
3. The pledge creditor over the pledge.

**Article 2475:** Regarding preference of certain commercial credits, such as that of the consignee in consigned effects, and that corresponding to various causes and persons in merchant ships, the provisions of the Commercial Code shall apply. Regarding credits of mine suppliers, and of stewards and workers of them, the provisions of the Mining Code shall be observed.

**Article 2476:** If credits of the first class and credits of the second class affect the same species, the latter shall exclude the former; but if other property is insufficient to cover first class credits, the latter shall have preference as to the deficit and shall concur in said species in the order and form expressed in paragraph 1 of article 2472\.

**Article 2477:** The third class of credits comprises mortgage credits.

To each estate encumbered with mortgage, a particular meeting may be opened, at the request of the respective creditors or any of them, for them to be paid immediately with it, according to the order of dates of their mortgages.

Mortgages of the same date encumbering the same estate shall prefer each other in the order of their registration.

In this meeting, court costs incurred in it shall be paid first.

**Article 2478:** First class credits shall not extend to mortgaged estates except in the case of not being able to be covered in their totality with the debtor's other property.

The deficit shall then be divided among the mortgaged estates in proportion to their values, and what falls to each shall be covered with it in the order and form expressed in article 2472\.

**Article 2479:** Mortgage creditors shall not be obliged to await the results of the general meeting to proceed to exercise their actions against the respective estates: it shall suffice that they consign or secure a prudent amount for payment of first class credits in the part falling on them, and restore to the mass what remains after their actions are covered.

**Article 2480:** For the purposes of preference, duly registered ground rents shall be considered as mortgages. They shall therefore concur indistinctly among themselves and with mortgages according to the dates of their respective registrations.

**Article 2481:** The fourth class of credits comprises:

1. Those of the Treasury against collectors and administrators of fiscal property;  
2. Those of national charity or education establishments, and those of municipalities, churches, and religious communities, against collectors and administrators of their funds;  
3. Those of married women, for property of theirs administered by the husband, over the husband's property or, as applicable, the spouses' partnership gains;  
4. Those of children subject to parental authority, for property of theirs administered by the father or mother, over the latter's property;  
5. Those of persons under guardianship or curatorship against their respective guardians or curators;  
6. Those of every ward against the one who marries the mother or grandmother, guardian or curator, in the case of article 511\.

**Article 2482:** Credits enumerated in the preceding article prefer indistinctly one another according to the dates of their causes; that is:

The date of appointment of administrators and collectors with respect to credits of numbers 1 and 2; The date of the respective marriage in credits of numbers 3 and 6; The date of birth of the child in those of number 4; The date of discernment of guardianship or curatorship in those of number 5\.

**Article 2483:** The preference of number 3, in the case of conjugal partnership, and that of numbers 4, 5, and 6, are understood to be constituted in favor of real property or real rights in them that the wife contributed to the marriage, or of real property or real rights in them belonging to the respective children under parental authority, and persons in guardianship or curatorship and that have entered the power of the husband, father, mother, guardian, or curator; and in favor of all property in which the right of the same persons is justified by solemn inventories, wills, partition acts, adjudication judgments, public deeds of matrimonial agreements, of donation, sale, exchange, or others of equal authenticity.

The preference of the fourth class also extends to the rights and actions of the wife against the husband, or of children under parental authority and persons in guardianship or curatorship, against their parents, guardians, or curators for fault or fraud in the administration of the respective property, the charges being proved in any trustworthy manner.

**Article 2484:** Marriages celebrated in a foreign country and that according to article 119 should produce civil effects in Chile shall give the wife's credits over the husband's property existing in Chilean territory the same right of preference as marriages celebrated in Chile.

**Article 2485:** Confession of either spouse, of the father or mother exercising parental authority, or of the bankrupt guardian or curator shall not by itself constitute proof against creditors.

**Article 2486:** Preferences of credits of the fourth class affect all the debtor's property, but do not give right against third-party possessors, and only take place after credits of the first three classes are covered, whatever their date.

**Article 2487:** Preferences of the first class, to which the deceased debtor's property was subject, shall affect the heir's property in the same manner, except if they have accepted with benefit of inventory, or if creditors enjoy the benefit of separation, in which case they shall only affect inventoried or separated property.

The same rule shall apply to credits of the fourth class, which shall retain their date over all the heir's property when the benefits of inventory or separation do not apply, and shall only retain it in inventoried or separated property when the respective benefits apply.

**Article 2488:** The law recognizes no other causes of preference than those indicated in the preceding articles.

**Article 2489:** The fifth and last class comprises credits that do not enjoy preference.

Credits of the fifth class shall be covered proportionally over the surplus of the meeting's assets, without consideration of their date.

Without prejudice to the foregoing, if among the credits of this class there are some subordinated to others, these shall be paid before the former.

Subordination of credits is an act or contract by virtue of which one or more creditors of the fifth class accept to postpone, in whole or in part, payment of their claims in favor of another or other credits of said class, present or future. Subordination may also be established unilaterally by the debtor in their credit instruments. In this latter case, and when established unilaterally by the creditor who accepts to subordinate themselves, it shall be irrevocable.

The establishment of subordination and its early termination, when applicable, shall appear in a public deed or private document signed before a notary and recorded. Subordination shall include principal and interest, unless otherwise expressed.

Subordination established by one or more creditors shall be binding on the debtor if they have concurred in the act or contract or accept it in writing afterwards, as well as if they are notified of it by a minister of faith, with exhibition of the instrument. Non-compliance with subordination shall give rise to damages against the debtor and action for reimbursement against the subordinated creditor.

Subordination shall bind the assignees or heirs of the subordinated creditor and the time during which it is in force shall not be considered for computing prescription of collection actions of the credit.

**Article 2490:** Preferential credits that cannot be covered in their totality by the means indicated in the preceding articles shall pass for the deficit to the list of credits of the fifth class, with which they shall concur proportionally.

**Article 2491:** Interest shall run until extinction of the debt, and shall be covered with the preference corresponding to their respective principals.

---

## TITLE XLII: OF PRESCRIPTION

### § 1\. Of Prescription in General

**Article 2492:** Prescription is a mode of acquiring another's things, or of extinguishing another's actions and rights, by having possessed the things or not having exercised said actions and rights during a certain lapse of time, and the other legal requirements concurring.
